## 2 Undergraduate Analysis: UniformConvergence

## 2.1 Fall 2018 # 1

Let $f ( x ) = { \frac { 1 } { x } }$ . Show that f is uniformly continuous on $( 1 , \infty )$ but not on $( 0 , \infty )$

Concepts Used:

• Uniform continuity:

$$
\forall \varepsilon > 0 , \exists \delta ( \varepsilon ) > 0 \quad { \mathrm { s u c h ~ t h a t } } \quad | x - y | < \delta \implies | f ( x ) - f ( y ) | < \varepsilon .
$$

• Negating uniform continuity: $\exists \varepsilon > 0$ such that $\forall \delta ( \varepsilon )$ there exist x, y such that $| x - y | < \delta$ and $| f ( x ) - f ( y ) | > \varepsilon .$

• Archimedean property: for all $x , y \in \mathbb { R }$ there exists an $n \in \mathbb { N }$ such that nx $> y$ . Take $x = \varepsilon , y = 1 , \mathrm { ~ s o ~ } n \varepsilon > 1 \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } \frac { 1 } { n } < \varepsilon .$

## Strategy:

1 is the only constant around, so try to use it for uniform continuity. To negate, find a bad x: since $1 / x$ blows up near zero, go hunting for small xs!

Solution: • Claim: $f ( x ) = { \frac { 1 } { x } }$ is uniformly continuous on $( c , \infty )$ for any $c > 0 .$

– Note that

$$
| x | , | y | > c > 0 \implies | x y | = | x | | y | > c ^ { 2 } \implies { \frac { 1 } { | x y | } } < { \frac { 1 } { c ^ { 2 } } } .
$$

– Letting ε be arbitrary, choose $\delta < \varepsilon c ^ { 2 }$

♦ Note that δ does not depend on $x , y$

– Then

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle | f ( x ) - f ( y ) | = \left| \frac { 1 } { x } - \frac { 1 } { y } \right| } \\ { \displaystyle = \frac { | x - y | } { x y } } \\ { \displaystyle \le \frac { \delta } { x y } } \\ { \displaystyle < \frac { \delta } { c ^ { 2 } } } \\ { \displaystyle < \varepsilon . } \end{array}
$$

• Claim: f is not uniformly continuous when $c = 0$

– Take $\varepsilon < 1$ , and let $\delta = \delta ( \varepsilon )$ be arbitrary.

– Let $x _ { n } = { \frac { 1 } { n } }$ for $n \geq 1$

Choose n large enough such that $\frac { 1 } { n } < \delta$

– Then a computation:

$$
\begin{array} { c } { \displaystyle | x _ { n } - x _ { n + 1 } | = \frac 1 n - \frac 1 { n + 1 } } \\ { \displaystyle = \frac 1 { n ( n + 1 ) } } \\ { \displaystyle < \frac 1 n } \\ { \displaystyle < \delta , } \end{array}
$$

– Why this can be done: by the Archimedean property of R, for any $\delta \in \mathbb { R }$ R, one can choose choose n such that nδ $> 1$ . We’ve also used that $n + 1 > 1 \mathrm { ~ s o ~ } \frac { 1 } { n + 1 } < 1$ – Note that $f ( x _ { n } ) = n .$ , so

$$
| f ( x _ { n + 1 } ) - f ( x _ { n } ) | = ( n + 1 ) - n = 1 > \varepsilon .
$$

## 2.2 Fall 2017 # 1

Let

$$
f ( x ) = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { x ^ { n } } { n ! } } .
$$

Describe the intervals on which f does and does not converge uniformly.

Concepts Used:

$f _ { N }  f$ uniformly $\iff \left\| { f _ { N } - f } \right\| _ { \infty } \to 0 .$

Applied to sums:

$$
\sum _ { 0 \leq k \leq N } f _ { n } \stackrel { u } { \to } \sum _ { k \geq 0 } f _ { n } \Longleftrightarrow \left. \sum _ { k \geq N + 1 } f _ { n } \right. _ { \infty } \to 0 .
$$

• An infinite sum is defined as the pointwise limit of its partial sums:

$$
\sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } c _ { n } x ^ { n } : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { N } c _ { n } x ^ { n } .
$$

• Uniformly decaying terms for uniformly convergent series: if $\sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } f _ { n } ( x )$ converges uniformly on a set A, then

$$
\| f _ { n } \| _ { \infty , A } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { x \in A } | f _ { n } ( x ) | \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0 .
$$

• M -test: if $f _ { n } : A \to \mathbb { C }$ with $\| f _ { n } \| _ { \infty } < M _ { n }$ and $\sum M _ { n } < \infty$ , then $\sum f _ { n }$ converges uniformly and absolutely.

– If the $f _ { n }$ are continuous, the uniform limit theorem implies $\sum f _ { n }$ is also continuous.

## Strategy:

No real place to start, so pick the nicest place: compact intervals. Then bounded intervals, then unbounded sets.

## Solution:

• Set $f _ { N } ( x ) = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } { \frac { x ^ { n } } { n ! } } .$

Then by definition, $f _ { N } ( x )  f ( x )$ pointwise on R.

• Claim: $f _ { N }$ converges on compact intervals

– For any compact interval $[ - M , M ]$ , we have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \| f _ { N } ( x ) - f ( x ) \| _ { \infty } = } & { { \displaystyle \operatorname* { s u p } _ { x \in [ - 4 , d ] } \left. \sum _ { n = N + 1 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { x ^ { n } } { n ! } } \right. } } \\ & { \leq \underbrace { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { x \in [ - 1 , d ] } } _ { x \in [ - 1 , d ] } \left. \frac { \sum _ { n = N + 1 } ^ { \infty } \left. \frac { x ^ { n } } { n ! } \right. } { n = N + 1 } \right. } \\ & { \leq \displaystyle \sum _ { n = N + 1 } ^ { \infty } \frac { \frac { \mathrm { d } } { n ! } } { n ! } } \\ & { \leq \displaystyle \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } \frac { M ^ { n } } { n ! } \left. \mathrm { s i n c e ~ a l l ~ s a d d i t i o n a l ~ t e r m s ~ a r e ~ p o s i t i v e } \right. } \\ & { = \epsilon ^ { M } } \\ & { < \epsilon ^ { M } , } \end{array}
$$

so $f _ { N }  f$ uniformly on $[ - M , M ]$ by the M-test.

$\diamondsuit$ Note: we’ve used that this power series converges to $e ^ { x }$ pointwise everywhere.

• This argument shows that f converges on any bounded set.

• Claim: $f _ { N }$ does not converge uniformly on all of R.

– Uniformly convergent sums have uniformly decaying terms:

$$
\sum _ { n \leq N } g _ { n } \stackrel { N \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } \sum g _ { n } \mathrm { u n i f o r m l y ~ o n ~ } A \implies \| g _ { n } \| _ { \infty , A } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { x \in A } \left| g _ { n } ( x ) \right| \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0 .
$$

– Take $B _ { N }$ a ball of radius N about 0, then for $N > 1$ , note that $x = N$ on the boundary and so

$$
\left\| { \frac { x ^ { k } } { k ! } } \right\| _ { \infty , B _ { N } } = { \frac { N ^ { k } } { k ! } } \stackrel { N \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } \infty .
$$

• Conclusion: $f _ { N }$ converges on any bounded $A \subseteq \mathbb { R }$ but not on all of R.

## 2.3 Fall 2014 # 1

Let $\{ f _ { n } \}$ be a sequence of continuous functions such that $\sum f _ { n }$ converges uniformly.

Prove that $\sum f _ { n }$ is also continuous.

Concepts Used:

• The uniform limit theorem.

$\varepsilon / 3$ trick.

## Solution:

Claim: If $F _ { N }  F$ uniformly with each $F _ { N }$ continuous, then F is continuous.

Proof (of claim).

• Follows from an $\varepsilon / 3$ argument:

$$
| F ( x ) - F ( y | \leq | F ( x ) - F _ { N } ( x ) | + | F _ { N } ( x ) - F _ { N } ( y ) | + | F _ { N } ( y ) - F ( y ) | \leq \varepsilon \to 0 .
$$

– The first and last $\varepsilon / 3$ come from uniform convergence of $F _ { N }  F$

– The middle $\varepsilon / 3$ comes from continuity of each $F _ { N }$

• Now setting $F _ { N } : = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } f _ { n }$ yields a finite sum of continuous functions, which is continuous.

• Each $F _ { N }$ is continuous and $F _ { N }  F$ uniformly, so $F$ is continuous.

## 2.4 Spring 2017 # 4

Let $f ( x , y )$ on $[ - 1 , 1 ] ^ { 2 }$ be defined by

$$
f ( x , y ) = { \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { \displaystyle { \frac { x y } { \left( x ^ { 2 } + y ^ { 2 } \right) ^ { 2 } } } } & { ( x , y ) \neq ( 0 , 0 ) } \\ { 0 } & { ( x , y ) = ( 0 , 0 ) } \end{array} \right. }
$$

Determine if f is integrable.

Concepts Used:

• Just Calculus.

$1 / r$ is not integrable on (0, 1).

## Solution:

Switching to polar coordinates and integrating over the quarter of the unit disc $D \cap Q _ { 1 } \subseteq I ^ { 2 }$

in quadrant 1, we have

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { \int _ { \mathcal { P } } f d A \geq \int _ { 0 } f d A } \\ { = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \pi / 2 } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \frac { r ^ { 2 } \exp ( \theta ) \sin ( \theta ) } { r ^ { 4 } } } r \ d r d \theta } \\ { = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \pi / 2 } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \frac { \exp ( \theta ) \sin ( \theta ) } { r } } d r d \theta } \\ { \quad } & { = \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \frac { 1 } { r } } d r \right) \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { \pi / 2 } \cos ( \theta ) \sin ( \theta ) d \theta \right) } \\ { \quad } & { = \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \frac { 1 } { r } } d r \right) \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } u d u \right) } \\ { \quad } & { = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \frac { 1 } { r } } d r \right) } \\ { \quad } & { \longrightarrow \infty . } \end{array} }
$$

## 2.5 Spring 2015 # 1

Let $( X , d )$ and $( Y , \rho )$ be metric spaces, $f : X \to Y$ , and $x _ { 0 } \in X$

Prove that the following statements are equivalent:

1. For every $\varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists \delta > 0$ such that $\rho ( f ( x ) , f ( x _ { 0 } ) ) < \varepsilon$ whenever $d ( x , x _ { 0 } ) < \delta$

2. The sequence $\{ f ( x _ { n } ) \} _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } \to f ( x _ { 0 } )$ for every sequence $\left\{ x _ { n } \right\} \to x _ { 0 }$ in X .

Concepts Used:

• What it means for a sequence to converge.

• Trading N s for δs.

## Solution:

## Proof $( 1 \implies 2 ) .$

• Let $\{ x _ { n } \} \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } x _ { 0 }$ be arbitrary; we want to show $\{ f ( x _ { n } ) \} \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } f ( x _ { 0 } )$

– We thus want to show that for every $\varepsilon > 0 .$ , there exists an N (ε) such that

$$
n \geq N ( \varepsilon ) \implies \rho ( f ( x _ { n } ) , f ( x _ { 0 } ) ) < \varepsilon .
$$

• Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be arbitrary, then by (1) choose δ such that $\rho ( f ( x ) , f ( x _ { 0 } ) ) < \varepsilon$ when $d ( x , x _ { 0 } ) < \delta .$

• Since $x _ { n } \to x .$ , there is some N such that $n \geq N \implies d ( x _ { n } , x _ { 0 } ) < \delta$

• Then for $n \geq N , d ( x _ { n } , x _ { 0 } ) < \delta$ and thus $\rho ( f ( x _ { n } ) , f ( x _ { 0 } ) ) < \varepsilon , \mathrm { { s o } } f ( x _ { n } )  f ( x _ { 0 } ) \mathrm { { b y } }$ definition.

Proof $( 2 \implies 1 )$

The direct implication is not a good idea here, since you need a handle on all x in a neighborhood of x0, not just a specific sequence.

• By contrapositive, show that $\lambda \implies 2 .$

• Need to show: if f is not $\varepsilon { - } \delta$ continuous at $x _ { 0 } .$ then there exists a sequence $x _ { n } \to x _ { 0 }$ where $f ( x _ { n } ) \not \to f ( x _ { 0 } )$

• Negating 1, we have that there exists an $\varepsilon > 0$ such that for all $\delta ,$ there exists an x with $d ( x , x _ { 0 } ) < \delta$ but $\rho ( f ( x ) , f ( x _ { 0 } ) ) > \varepsilon$

• So take a sequence of deltas $\delta _ { n } = { \frac { 1 } { n } } ;$ apply this to produce a sequence $x _ { n }$ with $d ( x _ { n } , x _ { 0 } ) < \delta _ { n } : = { \frac { 1 } { n } } \longrightarrow 0$ and $\rho ( f ( x _ { n } ) , f ( x _ { 0 } ) ) > \varepsilon$ for all n.

• This yields a sequence $x _ { n } \to x _ { 0 }$ where $f ( x _ { n } ) \not \to f ( x _ { 0 } )$

## 2.6 Fall 2014 # 2

Let I be an index set and $\alpha : I  ( 0 , \infty )$

a. Show that

$$
\sum _ { i \in I } a ( i ) : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { J \subset I \atop J { \mathrm { f i n i t e } } } \sum _ { i \in J } a ( i ) < \infty \implies I { \mathrm { ~ i s ~ c o u n t a b l e . } }
$$

b. Suppose $I = \mathbb { Q }$ and $\sum _ { q \in \mathbb { Q } } a ( q ) < \infty$ . Define

$$
f ( x ) : = \sum _ { { q \in \mathbb { Q } } \atop { q \leq x } } a ( q ) .
$$

Show that f is continuous at $x \iff x \notin \mathbb { Q } .$

## Concepts Used:

• Can always filter sets X with a function $X  \mathbb { R } .$

• Countable union of countable sets is still countable.

• Continuity: $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { y \to x } f ( y ) = f ( x )$ from either side.

• Trick: pick enumerations of countable sets and reindex sums

## Solution:

```latex
Proof $( o f a ) .$
• Set $S : = \sum _ { i \in I } \alpha ( i )$ , we will show that $S < \infty \implies I$ is countable.
• Write
I = [ Sn, S n :=  i ∈ I  α ( i ) ≥ 1n  .
n≥0
– Note that $S _ { n } \subseteq S$ for all n, so $\sum _ { i \in I } \alpha ( i ) \geq \sum _ { i \in S _ { n } } \alpha ( i )$ for all n.
– It suffices to show that $S _ { n }$ nis countable, since I is a countable union of $S _ { n }$
• There is an inequality
$\infty > S : = \sum _ { i \in I } \alpha ( i )$
$\geq \sum _ { i \in S _ { n } } \alpha ( i )$
$\geq \sum _ { i \in S _ { n } } { \frac { 1 } { n } }$
$= \frac { 1 } { n } \sum _ { i \in S _ { n } } 1$
 1n #Sn
=⇒ ∞ > nS ≥ #Sn.
```

## Proof $( o f b )$

• We’ll prove something more general: let $Q = \left\{ q _ { k } \right\}$ be countable and $\{ \alpha _ { k } : = \alpha ( q _ { k } ) \}$ be summable, and define

$$
f ( x ) : = \sum _ { q _ { k } \leq x } \alpha _ { k } .
$$

$f$ is always discontinuous precisely on the countable set $Q$ and continuous on R $\backslash Q .$

$f$ is always left-continuous, is right-continuous at $x \in \mathbb { R } \setminus Q$ , and not rightcontinuous at $x \in Q$

$f$ has jump discontinuities at every $q _ { m }$ , where the jump is precisely $\alpha _ { m }$

• This follows from computing the left and right limits:

$$
f ( x ^ { + } ) = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { h  0 } \sum _ { q _ { k } \leq x + h } \alpha _ { k } = \sum _ { q _ { k } \leq x } \alpha _ { k } = \sum _ { q _ { k } < x } \alpha _ { k } + \sum _ { q _ { k } = x } \alpha _ { k }
$$

$$
f ( x ^ { - } ) = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { h  0 } \sum _ { q _ { k } \leq x - h } \alpha _ { k } = \sum _ { q _ { k } < x } \alpha _ { k } ,
$$

where we’ve used that $\{ q _ { k } \leq x \} = \{ q _ { k } < x \} \amalg \{ x \}$ in the first equality.

• Then if $x = q _ { m }$ for some $m ,$

$$
f ( x ^ { + } ) = f ( q _ { m } ^ { + } ) = \sum _ { q _ { k } < q _ { m } } \alpha _ { k } + \alpha _ { m }
$$

$$
f ( x ^ { - } ) = f ( a _ { m } ^ { - } ) = \sum _ { q _ { k } < q _ { m } } \alpha _ { k } ,
$$

which clearly differ if $\alpha _ { m } \neq 0 .$

• Taking $x \not \in Q$ , we have $\{ q _ { k } \leq x \} = \{ q _ { k } < x \}$ , since $\{ q _ { k } = x \} = \emptyset$ , so

$$
f ( x ^ { + } ) = \sum _ { q _ { k } \leq x } \alpha _ { k } = \sum _ { q _ { k } < x } \alpha _ { k }
$$

$$
f ( x ^ { - } ) = \sum _ { q _ { k } < x } \alpha _ { k } ,
$$

so the limits agree.

• To recover the result in the problem, let $\mathbb { Q } = \{ q _ { k } \}$ be any enumeration of the rationals.

## 3 General Analysis

## 3.1 Spring 2020 # 1

Prove that if $f : [ 0 , 1 ] \to \mathbb { R }$ is continuous then

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } k x ^ { k - 1 } f ( x ) d x = f ( 1 ) .
$$

Concepts Used:

• DCT

• Weierstrass Approximation Theorem

– If $f : [ a , b ]  \mathbb { R }$ is continuous, then for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists a polynomial $p _ { \varepsilon } ( x )$ such that $\| f - p _ { \varepsilon } \| _ { \infty } < \varepsilon .$

## Solution:

• Suppose p is a polynomial, then integrate by parts:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \underset { k  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } k x ^ { k - 1 } p ( x ) d x = \underset { k  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } ( \frac { \partial } { \partial x } x ^ { k } ) p ( x ) d x } & { } \\ { = \underset { k  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \displaystyle [ x ^ { k } p ( x ) | _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } - \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } x ^ { k } ( \frac { \partial p } { \partial x } ( x ) ) d x ] } & { \mathrm { I B P } } \\ { = p ( 1 ) - \underset { k  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } x ^ { k } ( \frac { \partial p } { \partial x } ( x ) ) d x , } \end{array}
$$

• Thus it suffices to show that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } x ^ { k } \left( { \frac { \partial p } { \partial x } } \left( x \right) \right) d x = 0 .
$$

• Integrating by parts a second time yields

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } x ^ { k } \left( \frac { \partial p } { \partial x } \left( x \right) \right) d x = \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \frac { x ^ { k + 1 } } { k + 1 } \frac { \partial p } { \partial x } \left( x \right) \Big | _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } - \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \frac { x ^ { k + 1 } } { k + 1 } \left( \frac { \partial ^ { 2 } p } { \partial x ^ { 2 } } \left( x \right) \right) d x } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \frac { p / \left( 1 \right) } { k + 1 } - \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \frac { x ^ { k + 1 } } { k + 1 } \left( \frac { \partial ^ { 2 } p } { \partial x ^ { 2 } } \left( x \right) \right) d x } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad = - \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \frac { x ^ { k + 1 } } { k + 1 } \left( \frac { \partial ^ { 2 } p } { \partial x ^ { 2 } } \left( x \right) \right) d x } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad = - \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \frac { x ^ { k + 1 } } { k + 1 } \left( \frac { \partial ^ { 2 } p } { \partial x ^ { 2 } } \left( x \right) \right) d x \quad \mathrm { b y ~ \mathrm { D C T } } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad = - \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } 0 \left( \frac { \partial ^ { 2 } p } { \partial x ^ { 2 } } \left( x \right) \right) d x } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad = 0 . } \end{array}
$$

– The DCT can be applied here because polynomials are smooth and [0, 1] is compact, so $\frac { { \partial } ^ { 2 } p } { \partial x ^ { 2 } }$ is bounded on [0, 1] by some constant M and

$$
\int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \left| x ^ { k } \frac { \partial ^ { 2 } p } { \partial x ^ { 2 } } \left( x \right) \right| \le \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } 1 \cdot M = M < \infty .
$$

• So the result holds when f is a polynomial.

• Now use the Weierstrass approximation theorem:

– If $f : [ a , b ]  \mathbb { R }$ is continuous, then for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists a polynomial $p _ { \varepsilon } ( x )$ such that $\| f - p _ { \varepsilon } \| _ { \infty } < \varepsilon$

• Thus

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } k x ^ { k - 1 } p _ { \varepsilon } ( x ) d x - \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } k x ^ { k - 1 } f ( x ) d x \Big \vert = \displaystyle \left. \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } k x ^ { k - 1 } \left( p _ { \varepsilon } ( x ) - f ( x ) \right) d x \right. } & { } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \leq \left. \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } k x ^ { k - 1 } \| p _ { \varepsilon } - f \| _ { \infty } d x \right. } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \| p _ { \varepsilon } - f \| _ { \infty } \cdot \left. \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } k x ^ { k - 1 } d x \right. } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \| p _ { \varepsilon } - f \| _ { \infty } \cdot x ^ { k } \Big \vert _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \| p _ { \varepsilon } - f \| _ { \infty } . } \end{array}
$$

$$
{ \varepsilon } { \to } 0 
$$

and the integrals are equal.

• By the first argument,

$$
\int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } k x ^ { k - 1 } p _ { \varepsilon } ( x ) d x = p _ { \varepsilon } ( 1 ) { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ e a c h ~ } } \varepsilon
$$

• Since uniform convergence implies pointwise convergence, $p _ { \varepsilon } ( 1 ) \stackrel { \varepsilon \to 0 } { \to } f ( 1 )$

## 3.2 Fall 2019 # 1

Let $\{ a _ { n } \} _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty }$ be a sequence of real numbers.

a. Prove that $\operatorname* { i f } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } a _ { n } = 0$ , then

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } { \frac { a _ { 1 } + \cdots + a _ { n } } { n } } = 0
$$

b. Prove that if $\sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { a _ { n } } { n } }$ converges, then

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } { \frac { a _ { 1 } + \cdots + a _ { n } } { n } } = 0
$$

## Solution:

## Concepts Used:

• Cesaro mean/summation.

• Break series apart into pieces that can be handled separately.

• Idea: once N is large enough, $a _ { k } \approx S$ , and all smaller terms will die off as $N  \infty .$

– See this MSE answer.

## Proof $( o f a )$

• Prove a stronger result:

$$
a _ { k } \to S \implies S _ { N } : = \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } a _ { k } \to S .
$$

• For any $\varepsilon > 0$ , use convergence $a _ { k } \to S \colon$ choose (and fix) $M = M ( \varepsilon )$ large enough such that

$$
k \geq M + 1 \implies | a _ { k } - S | < \varepsilon .
$$

With M fixed, choose $N = N ( M , \varepsilon )$ large enough so that $\frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { M } | a _ { k } - S | < \varepsilon .$

• Then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \left( \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \frac { \partial _ { i } } { \partial x _ { i - 1 } ^ { i } } \right) - S _ { i } } & { \operatorname { i } \left( \frac { \partial _ { i } ^ { ( 2 ) } } { \partial x _ { i - 1 } ^ { i } } \right) - S _ { i } } \\ & { = \frac { 1 } { N } \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } \right) - \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } } \\ & { = \frac { 1 } { N } \left( \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } \right) } \\ & { \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } } \\ & { = \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } } \\ & { = \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } } \\ & { \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } \leq \frac { 1 } { N - 1 } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } } \\ & { \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } \leq \frac { 1 } { N - 1 } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } } \\ & { = \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } + ( 1 - S _ { i } ) } \\ & { \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } } \\ & { \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } } \\ & { \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \alpha _ { i } - S _ { i } } \\ &  \leq \frac \end{array}
$$

## Proof (of b).

• Define

$$
\Gamma _ { n } : = \sum _ { k = n } ^ { \infty } \frac { a _ { k } } { k } .
$$

$\Gamma _ { 1 } = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } \frac { a _ { k } } { k }$ is the original series and each $\Gamma _ { n }$ is a tail of $\Gamma _ { 1 }$ , so by assumption $\Gamma _ { n } \stackrel { n  \infty } {  } 0 .$

{ \frac { 1 } { n } } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } a _ { k } = { \frac { 1 } { n } } ( \Gamma _ { 1 } + \Gamma _ { 2 } + \cdots + \Gamma _ { n } { - } \mathbf { I } _ { \mathbf { n } { + } \mathbf { 1 } } )
$$

• This comes from consider the following summation:

$$
\Gamma _ { 1 } :

Also a minor check: $\Gamma _ { n }  0 \implies \frac { 1 } { n } \Gamma _ { n }  0 .$

{ \begin{array} { r l } & { { \frac { 1 } { n } } \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } a _ { k } = { \frac { 1 } { n } } ( \Gamma _ { 1 } + \Gamma _ { 2 } + \cdots + \Gamma _ { n } - \Gamma _ { \mathbf { n } + \mathbf { 1 } } ) } \\ & { \qquad = ( { \frac { 1 } { n } } \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { n } \Gamma _ { k } ) - ( { \frac { 1 } { n } } \Gamma _ { n + 1 } ) } \\ & { \qquad n _ { \underset {  } { \longrightarrow } 0 } ^ { \underset {  } { \longrightarrow } 0 } . } \end{array} }

## 3.3 Fall 2018 # 4

Let $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ . Prove that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) | \sin n x | \ d x = { \frac { 2 } { \pi } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) \ d x
$$

Hint: Begin with the case that f is the characteristic function of an interval.

Ask someone to check the last approximation part.

## Solution:

Concepts Used:

• Converting floor/ceiling functions to inequalities: $x - 1 \leq \lfloor x \rfloor \leq x .$

Case of a characteristic function of an interval $[ a , b ]$

• First suppose $f ( x ) = \chi _ { [ a , b ] } ( x )$

• Note that sin(nx) has a period of $2 \pi / n$ , and thus $\left\lfloor { \frac { ( b - a ) } { ( 2 \pi / n ) } } \right\rfloor = \left\lfloor { \frac { n ( b - a ) } { 2 \pi } } \right\rfloor$ full periods in $[ a , b ]$

• Taking the absolute value yields a new function with half the period

$- \mathrm { \normalfont ~ S o } \mathrm { \normalfont ~ \left| s i n \right|} ( n x ) $ has a period of $\pi / n$ with $\left\lfloor { \frac { n ( b - a ) } { \pi } } \right\rfloor$ full periods in $[ a , b ]$

• We can compute the integral over one full period (which is independent of which period is chosen)

– We can use translation invariance of the integral to compute this over the period 0 to $\pi / n .$

– Since sin(nx) is positive, it equals $| \mathrm { s i n } ( n x ) |$ on its first period, so we have

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \int _ { { \mathrm { O n e ~ P e r i o d } } } | \sin ( n x ) | d x = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \pi / n } \sin ( n x ) d x } \\ { = { \frac { 1 } { n } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \pi } \sin ( u ) d u } & { u = n x } \\ { = { \frac { 1 } { n } } \left( - \cos ( u ) { \Big | } _ { 0 } ^ { \pi } \right) } \\ { = { \frac { 2 } { n } } . } \end{array} }
$$

• Then break the integral up into integrals over full periods $P _ { 1 } , P _ { 2 } , \cdots , P _ { N }$ where $N : =$ $\lfloor n ( b - a ) / \pi \rfloor$

• Noting that each period is of length $\frac { \pi } { n }$ , so letting $L _ { n }$ be the regions falling outside of a full period, we have

• Thus

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \int _ { a } ^ { b } | \sin ( n x ) | d x = } & { \left( \displaystyle \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N } \displaystyle \int _ { P _ { j } } | \sin ( n x ) | d x \right) + \displaystyle \int _ { L _ { n } } | \sin ( n x ) | d x } \\ & { = \left( \displaystyle \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N } { \frac { 2 } { n } } \right) + \displaystyle \int _ { L _ { n } } | \sin ( n x ) | d x } \\ & { = N \left( { \frac { 2 } { n } } \right) + \displaystyle \int _ { L _ { n } } | \sin ( n x ) | d x } \\ & { : = \displaystyle \left\lfloor { \frac { ( b - a ) n } { \pi } } \right\rfloor { \frac { 2 } { n } } + R _ { n } } \\ & { : = ( b - a ) C _ { n } + R _ { n } } \end{array} }
$$

where (claim) ${ C _ { n } } \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } \frac { 2 } \pi$ and $R ( n ) \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } 0$

$C _ { n } \to { \frac { 2 } { \pi } } \colon$

$$
{ \frac { n - 1 } { n } } \left( { \frac { 2 } { \pi } } \right) = { \frac { n - 1 } { \pi } } \left( { \frac { 2 } { n } } \right) \leq \left\lfloor { \frac { n } { \pi } } \right\rfloor \left( { \frac { 2 } { n } } \right) \leq { \frac { n } { \pi } } \left( { \frac { 2 } { n } } \right) = { \frac { 2 } { \pi } } ,
$$

then use the fact that ${ \frac { n - 1 } { n } } \to 1$

– Then equality follows by the Squeeze theorem.

$R _ { n } \to 0 ;$

– We use the fact that $m ( L _ { n } ) \to 0 .$ , then $\int _ { L _ { n } } | \mathrm { s i n } ( n x ) | \leq \int _ { L _ { n } } 1 = m ( L _ { n } )  0 .$

– This follows from the fact that $L _ { n }$ is the complement of $\cup _ { j } P _ { j }$ , the set of full periods, so

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { m ( L _ { n } ) = m ( b - a ) - \displaystyle \sum m ( P _ { j } ) } } \\ { { \mathrm { ~ } = ( b - a ) - \left\lfloor \displaystyle \frac { n ( b - a ) } { \pi } \right\rfloor \left( \displaystyle \frac { \pi } { n } \right) } } \\ { { \mathrm { ~ } \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } ( b - a ) - ( b - a ) } } \\ { { \mathrm { ~ } = 0 . } } \end{array}
$$

where we’ve used the fact that

$$
\begin{array} { c c c } { \left( \displaystyle \frac { \pi } { n } \right) \left( \displaystyle \frac { ( b - a ) n - 1 } { \pi } \right) \leq \left\lfloor \displaystyle \frac { n ( b - a ) } { \pi } \right\rfloor \left( \displaystyle \frac { \pi } { n } \right) } \\ { \leq \left( \displaystyle \frac { \pi } { n } \right) \left( \displaystyle \frac { ( b - a ) n } { \pi } \right) } \\ { = ( b - a ) , } \end{array}
$$

then taking $n \to \infty$ sends the LHS to $b - a$ , forcing the middle term to be $b - a$ by the Squeeze theorem.

General case:

• By linearity of the integral, the result holds for simple functions:

$- \mathrm { ~ I f ~ } f = \sum c _ { j } \chi _ { E _ { j } }$ where $E _ { j } = [ a _ { j } , b _ { j } ]$ , we have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) \| \sin ( n x ) \| d x = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \sum _ { \substack { e _ { j } \chi _ { E _ { j } } ( x ) \| \sin ( n x ) \| \sin ( n x ) \| d x } } } } \\ & { = \sum c _ { j } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \chi _ { E _ { j } } ( x ) \| \sin ( n x ) \| d x } \\ & { = \sum c _ { j } ( b _ { j } - a _ { j } ) \frac { 2 } { \pi } } \\ & { = \frac { 2 } { \pi } \sum c _ { j } ( b _ { j } - a _ { j } ) } \\ & { = \frac { 2 } { \pi } \sum c _ { j } m ( E _ { j } ) } \\ & { : = \frac { 2 } { \pi } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f . } \end{array}
$$

• Since $f \in L ^ { 1 }$ , where simple functions are dense, choose $s _ { n } \nearrow f$ where $\| s _ { N } - f \| _ { 1 } < \varepsilon$ then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \left. \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) | \mathrm { s i n } ( n x ) | d x - \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } s _ { N } ( x ) | \mathrm { s i n } ( n x ) | d x \right. = \displaystyle \left. \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \left( f ( x ) - s _ { N } ( x ) \right) | \mathrm { s i n } ( n x ) | d x \right. } & { } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } | f ( x ) - s _ { N } ( x ) | | \mathrm { s i n } ( n x ) | d x } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = | | ( f - s _ { N } ) | \mathrm { s i n } ( n x ) | | _ { 1 } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \leq | | f - s _ { N } | | _ { 1 } \cdot | | \mathrm { s i n } ( n x ) | | _ { \infty } \quad \mathrm { b y ~ H o l d e r } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \leq \varepsilon \cdot 1 , } \end{array}
$$

• So the integrals involving $s N$ converge to the integral involving $f ,$ and

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int f ( \boldsymbol { x } ) | \mathrm { s i n } ( n \boldsymbol { x } ) | = \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } \int s _ { N } ( \boldsymbol { x } ) | \mathrm { s i n } ( n \boldsymbol { x } ) | } \\ { \displaystyle = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int s _ { N } ( \boldsymbol { x } ) | \mathrm { s i n } ( n \boldsymbol { x } ) | \quad \mathrm { b e c a u s e ~ 7 } } \\ { \displaystyle = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } \frac { 2 } { \pi } \int s _ { N } ( \boldsymbol { x } ) } \\ { \displaystyle = \frac { 2 } { \pi } \int f , } \end{array}
$$

which is the desired result.

## 3.4 Fall 2017 # 4

Let

$$
f _ { n } ( x ) = n x ( 1 - x ) ^ { n } , \quad n \in \mathbb { N } .
$$

a. Show that $f _ { n } \to 0$ pointwise but not uniformly on [0, 1].

b. Show that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } n ( 1 - x ) ^ { n } \sin x d x = 0
$$

Hint for (a): Consider the maximum of $f _ { n } .$

## Solution:

## Concepts Used:

$\sum f _ { n } < \infty \iff \operatorname* { s u p } f _ { n } \to 0 .$

• Negating uniform convergence: $f _ { n } \ \not \to \ f$ uniformly iff $\exists \varepsilon$ such that $\forall N ( \varepsilon )$ there exists an $x _ { N }$ such that $| f ( x _ { N } ) - f ( x ) | > \varepsilon$

• Exponential inequality: $1 + y \le e ^ { y }$ for all $y \in \mathbb { R } .$

a.

$f _ { n } \to 0$ pointwise:

• Finding the maximum: can check that $\frac { \partial f _ { n } } { \partial x } = x ( 1 - x ) ^ { n - 1 } \left( 1 + ( n ^ { 2 } - 1 ) x \right)$

• This has critical points $x = 0 , 1 , { \frac { - 1 } { n ^ { 2 } + 1 } }$ , and the latter is a global max on [0, 1].

• Set $x _ { n } : = { \frac { - 1 } { n ^ { 2 } + 1 } }$

• Compute

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } f _ { n } ( x _ { n } ) = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } { \frac { - n } { n ^ { 2 } + 1 } } ( 1 + x _ { n } ) ^ { n } = 0 \cdot 1 = 0 .
$$

• So sup $f _ { n } \to 0$ , forcing $f _ { n } \to 0$ pointwise.

The convergence is not uniform:

• Let $x _ { n } = { \frac { 1 } { n } }$ and $\varepsilon > e ^ { - 1 }$ , then

$$
\begin{array} { c } { \displaystyle { \| n x ( 1 - x ) ^ { n } - 0 \| _ { \infty } \geq | n x _ { n } ( 1 - x _ { n } ) ^ { n } | } } \\ { \displaystyle { \qquad = \left| \left( 1 - \frac 1 n \right) ^ { n } \right| } } \\ { \displaystyle { \qquad > e ^ { - 1 } } } \\ { \displaystyle { \qquad > \varepsilon . } } \end{array}
$$

– Here we’ve used that $( 1 + { \frac { x } { n } } ) ^ { n } \leq e ^ { x }$ for all $x \in \mathbb { R }$ and all $n .$

– Follows from $1 + y \le e ^ { y }$ applied to $y = x / n$

• Thus $\left\| f _ { n } - 0 \right\| _ { \infty } = \left\| f _ { n } \right\| _ { \infty } > e ^ { - 1 } > 0 .$

b.

## Possible to use part a with sin(x) ≤ x on [0, π/2]?

• Noting that sin $( x ) \leq 1$ , we have

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \left. \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } n ( 1 - x ) ^ { n } \sin ( x ) \right. \le \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \left. n ( 1 - x ) ^ { n } \sin ( x ) \right. } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad \le \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \left. n ( 1 - x ) ^ { n } \right. } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad = n \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } ( 1 - x ) ^ { n } } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad = - \frac { n ( 1 - x ) ^ { n + 1 } } { n + 1 } } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad \xrightarrow { n \to \infty } 0 . } \end{array}
$$

## 3.5 Spring 2017 # 3

Let

$$
f _ { n } ( x ) = a e ^ { - n a x } - b e ^ { - n b x } \quad { \mathrm { ~ w h e r e ~ } } 0 < a < b .
$$

Show that

a. $\sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } \left| f _ { n } \right|$ is not in $L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , \infty ) , m )$

Hint: $f _ { n } ( x )$ has a root $x _ { n } .$

b.

$$
\sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } f _ { n } { \mathrm { ~ i s ~ i n ~ } } L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , \infty ) , m ) \quad { \mathrm { a n d } } \quad \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } f _ { n } ( x ) d m = \ln { \frac { b } { a } }
$$

Not complete.

Add concepts.

Walk through.

## Solution:

## Concepts Used:

a.

$f _ { n }$ has a root:

$$
a e ^ { - n a x } = b e ^ { - n b x } \iff { \frac { 1 } { n } } = e ^ { - n b x } e ^ { n a x } = e ^ { n ( b - a ) x } \iff x = { \frac { \ln \left( { \frac { a } { b } } \right) } { n ( a - b ) } } : = x _ { n } .
$$

• Thus $f _ { n }$ only changes sign at $x _ { n } .$ , and is strictly positive on one side of $x _ { n }$

• Then

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R } \displaystyle \sum _ { n } | f _ { n } ( x ) | d x = \displaystyle \sum _ { n } \int _ { \mathbb R } | f _ { n } ( x ) | d x } \\ { \displaystyle \geq \sum _ { n } \int _ { x _ { n } } ^ { \infty } f _ { n } ( x ) d x } \\ { \displaystyle = \sum _ { n } \frac 1 n \left( e ^ { - b n x } - e ^ { - a n x } \Big | _ { x _ { n } } ^ { \infty } \right) } \\ { \displaystyle = \sum _ { n } \frac 1 n \left( e ^ { - b n x _ { n } } - e ^ { - a n x _ { n } } \right) . } \end{array}
$$

b.

?

## 3.6 Fall 2016 # 1

Define

$$
f ( x ) = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { 1 } { n ^ { x } } } .
$$

Show that $f$ converges to a differentiable function on (1, ∞) and that

$$
f ^ { \prime } ( x ) = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } \left( { \frac { 1 } { n ^ { x } } } \right) ^ { \prime } .
$$

Hint:

$$
\left( { \frac { 1 } { n ^ { x } } } \right) ^ { \prime } = - { \frac { 1 } { n ^ { x } } } \ln n
$$

## Solution:

Concepts Used:

• Set $f _ { N } ( x ) : = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } n ^ { - x }$ , so $f ( x ) = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } f _ { N } ( x )$

• If an interchange of limits is justified, we have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \frac { \partial } { \partial x } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } \frac { 1 } { n - \lambda } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { 0 \le N \to \infty } \frac { 1 } { k } [ ( \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \cdots ) - ( \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \cdots ) ] } & { } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \frac { 1 } { z } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty , i = 1 } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { 0 \le N \to 1 } [ ( \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \cdots ) - ( \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \cdots ( x + x ) ) ] } \\ & { - \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty , i = 1 } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { 0 \le N \to 1 } [ \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \cdots \cdots ( x ^ { - ( i + k ) } ) ] } \\ & { - \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty , i = 1 } \frac { 1 } { k } [ \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \cdots  } \\ & { -  \operatorname* { l i m } _ { 0 \le N \to 1 } \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \frac { 1 } { k } [ \displaystyle \mu ^ { ( 1 - x ) } -  \mathrm { s i n c e } \mathrm { ~ } ] ] \quad \mathrm { s i n c e ~ v i t h ~ i s ~ i s ~ s t ~ f i n i t e s ~ s u r t } } \\ & { = \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty , i = 1 } \displaystyle \frac { \partial } { \partial x } \ \frac { \partial } { \partial x } ( \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { \lambda x ^ { \prime } } ) } \\ & { = \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty , i = 1 } \frac { k } { \lambda x ^ { \prime } } \ \frac { 1 } { \partial x ^ { \prime } } \alpha ( \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { \lambda x ^ { \prime } } ) } \\ &  \displaystyle = \lambda \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { i \in \mathbb { N } , i = 1 } \frac { k }  \lambda x \end{array}
$$

where the combining of sums in (1) is valid because $\sum n ^ { - x }$ is absolutely convergent for $x > 1$ by the p-test.

• Thus it suffices to justify the interchange of limits and show that the last sum converges on $( 1 , \infty )$

• Claim: $\sum n ^ { - x } \ln ( n )$ converges.

– Use the fact that for any fixed $\varepsilon > 0$

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } { \frac { \ln ( n ) } { n ^ { \varepsilon } } } \stackrel { L . H . } { = } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } { \frac { 1 / n } { \varepsilon n ^ { \varepsilon - 1 } } } = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } { \frac { 1 } { \varepsilon n ^ { \varepsilon } } } = 0 ,
$$

– This implies that for a fixed $\varepsilon > 0$ and for any constant $c > 0$ there exists an N large enough such that $n \geq N$ implies ln $\iota ( n ) / n ^ { \varepsilon } < c ,$ i.e. ln $. ( n ) < c n ^ { \varepsilon }$ •

– Taking c = 1, we have $n \geq N \implies \ln ( n ) < n ^ { \varepsilon }$

– We thus break up the sum:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \sum _ { n \in \mathbb { N } } \displaystyle \frac { \ln ( n ) } { n ^ { x } } = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N - 1 } \displaystyle \frac { \ln ( n ) } { n ^ { x } } + \sum _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } \displaystyle \frac { \ln ( n ) } { n ^ { x } } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad \leq \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N - 1 } \displaystyle \frac { \ln ( n ) } { n ^ { x } } + \sum _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } \displaystyle \frac { n ^ { \varepsilon } } { n ^ { x } } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad : = C _ { \varepsilon } + \sum _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } \displaystyle \frac { n ^ { \varepsilon } } { n ^ { x } } \quad \mathrm { w i t h ~ } C _ { \varepsilon } < \infty \mathrm { ~ a ~ c o n s t a n t } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad = C _ { \varepsilon } + \sum _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { n ^ { x - \varepsilon } } , } \end{array}
$$

where the last term converges by the p-test if $x - \varepsilon > 1$

– But ε can depend on x, and if $x \in ( 1 , \infty )$ is fixed we can choose $\varepsilon < | x - 1 |$ to ensure this.

• Claim: the interchange of limits is justified.

## 3.7 Fall 2016 # 5

Let $\varphi \in L ^ { \infty } ( \mathbb { R } )$ . Show that the following limit exists and satisfies the equality

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \left( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } { \frac { | \varphi ( x ) | ^ { n } } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } } d x \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } = \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } .
$$

## Solution:

Concepts Used:

?

Let L be the LHS and R be the RHS.

Claim: $L \leq R . \ - \ \mathrm { S }$ ince $| \varphi | \leq \| \varphi \| _ { \infty }$ a.e., we can write

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { L _ { n } ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } : = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } { \frac { | \varphi ( x ) | ^ { n } } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } } } \\ & { \leq \int _ { \mathbb { R } } { \frac { \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } ^ { n } } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } } } \\ & { = \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } ^ { n } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } { \frac { 1 } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } } } \\ & { = \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } ^ { n } \arctan ( x ) \| _ { - \infty } ^ { \infty } } \\ & { = \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } ^ { n } \left( { \frac { \pi } { 2 } } - { \frac { - \pi } { 2 } } \right) } \\ & { = \pi \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } ^ { n } } \end{array} }
$$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \implies L ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } \leq \sqrt [ n ] { \pi \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } ^ { n } } } \\ & { \implies L \leq \pi ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } } \\ & { \qquad \overset { n \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } , } \end{array}
$$

where we’ve used the fact that $c ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } 1$ for any constant c.

Actually true? Need conditions?

Claim: $R \leq L$

• We will show that $R \leq L + \varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon > 0$

• Set

$$
S _ { \varepsilon } : = \left\{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \mid \left| \varphi ( x ) \right| \geq \left\| \varphi \right\| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon \right\} .
$$

• Then we have

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } & { \int _ { \mathbb { R } } { \frac { | \varphi ( x ) | ^ { n } } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } } d x \geq \int _ { S _ { \varepsilon } } { \frac { | \varphi ( x ) | ^ { n } } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } } d x \quad S _ { \varepsilon } \subset \mathbb { R } } \\ & { \qquad \geq \int _ { S _ { \varepsilon } } { \frac { \left( \left\| \varphi \right\| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon \right) ^ { n } } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } } d x \qquad { \mathrm { b y ~ d e f i n i t i o n ~ o f ~ } } S _ { \varepsilon } } \\ & { \qquad = \left( \left\| \varphi \right\| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon \right) ^ { n } \int _ { S _ { \varepsilon } } { \frac { 1 } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } } d x } \\ & { \qquad = \left( \left\| \varphi \right\| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon \right) ^ { n } C _ { \varepsilon } \qquad { \mathrm { w h e r e ~ } } C _ { \varepsilon } { \mathrm { ~ i s ~ s o m e ~ c o n s t a n t } } } \end{array} }
$$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \implies ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \frac { | \varphi ( x ) | ^ { n } } { 1 + x ^ { 2 } } d x ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } \geq ( \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon ) C _ { \varepsilon } ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } } & { } \\ { \overset { n \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } ( \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon ) \cdot 1 } & { } \\ { \varepsilon \overset {  0 } {  } \| \varphi \| _ { \infty } , } \end{array}
$$

where we’ve again used the fact that $c ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } }  1$ for any constant.

## 3.8 Fall 2016 # 6

Let $f , g \in L ^ { 2 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ . Show that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } f ( x ) g ( x + n ) d x = 0
$$

## Rewrite solution.

## Concepts Used:

• Cauchy Schwarz: $\left\| f g \right\| _ { 1 } \leq \left\| f \right\| _ { 1 } \left\| g \right\| _ { 1 }$

• Small tails in $L ^ { p } .$

## Solution:

• Use the fact that $L ^ { p }$ has small tails: if $h \in L ^ { 2 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ , then for any $\varepsilon > 0$

$$
\forall \varepsilon , \exists N \in \mathbb { N } \quad { \mathrm { s u c h t h a t } } \quad \int _ { | x | \geq N } | h ( x ) | ^ { 2 } d x < \varepsilon .
$$

• So choose N large enough so that

$$
\int _ { \| x \| \geq N } | g ( x ) | ^ { 2 } < \varepsilon
$$

$$
\int _ { \| x \| \geq N } | f ( x ) | ^ { 2 } < \varepsilon
$$

• Then write

$$
\int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { d } } f ( x ) g ( x + n ) d x = \int _ { \| x \| \leq N } f ( x ) g ( x + n ) d x + \int _ { \| x \| \geq N } f ( x ) g ( x + n ) d x .
$$

• Bounding the second term: apply Cauchy-Schwarz

$$
\int _ { \| x \| \geq N } f ( x ) g ( x + n ) d x \leq \left( \int _ { \| x \| \geq N } | f ( x ) | ^ { 2 } \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \cdot \left( \int _ { \| x \| \geq N } | g ( x ) | ^ { 2 } \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \leq \varepsilon ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \cdot \| g \| _ { 2 } .
$$

• Bounding the first term: also Cauchy-Schwarz, after variable changes

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \int _ { \| x \| \leq N } f ( x ) g ( x + n ) d x = \int _ { - N } ^ { N } f ( x ) g ( x + n ) d x } \\ & { \qquad = \int _ { - N + n } ^ { N + n } f ( x - n ) g ( x ) d x } \\ & { \qquad \leq \int _ { - N + n } ^ { \infty } f ( x - n ) g ( x ) d x } \\ & { \qquad \leq \left( \int _ { - N + n } ^ { \infty } \left| f ( x - n ) \right| ^ { 2 } \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \cdot \left( \int _ { - N + n } ^ { \infty } \left| g ( x ) \right| ^ { 2 } \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } \\ & { \qquad \leq \left\| f \right\| _ { 2 } \cdot \varepsilon ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } . } \end{array}
$$

• Then as long as $n \geq 2 N$ , we have

$$
\int | f ( x ) g ( x + n ) | \leq { \big ( } \| f \| _ { 2 } + \| g \| _ { 2 } { \big ) } \cdot \varepsilon ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } .
$$

$$

Show that $e _ { n } < E _ { n }$ , and prove Bernoulli’s inequality:

( 1 + x ) ^ { n } \geq 1 + n x

- 1 < x < \infty , \ n \in \mathbb { N } .

Show that there is a number $x _ { m }$ such that $f ( x _ { m } ) \leq f ( x )$ for all $x \in \mathbb { R }$

## 3.11 Fall 2020 # 1

Show that if $x _ { n }$ is a decreasing sequence of positive real numbers such that $\sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } x _ { n }$ converges, then

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } n x _ { n } = 0 .
$$

## Concepts Used:

• Cauchy criterion for convergence

• Claim: even and odd subsequences converge iff whole sequence converges.

Proof (of claim).

⇐= : clear, since any subsequence of a convergent sequence converges, and to the same limit. =⇒ : Fix ε, choose $N \gg 1$ so that both $| a _ { n } - L | < \varepsilon , | a _ { 2 n } - L | < \varepsilon$ for $n \geq N$ . Then for any $n ,$ it is either even or odd, so one of these bounds applies.

## Solution:

See this MSE post for many solutions: https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/4603/ if-a-n-subset0-infty-is-non-increasing-and-sum-a-n-infty-then-lim

• Since $\sum _ { k \geq 1 } x _ { k } < \infty$ , by the Cauchy criterion for convergent sequences we have

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { M , N \to \infty } \sum _ { M \leq k \leq N } x _ { k } = 0 .
$$

– This still holds if we freely add a constant $C ,$ so $C \sum _ { \ k = 1 } x _ { k }  0$ as well. M≤k≤N

• Trick: $N : = n , M : =$ 2n and take $C : = 2 :$

$$
\begin{array} { c } { { 2 \displaystyle \sum _ { n \leq k \leq 2 n } x _ { k } \geq 2 \displaystyle \sum _ { n \leq k \leq 2 n } x _ { 2 n } } } \\ { { = 2 ( 2 n - n ) x _ { 2 n } } } \\ { { = 2 n x _ { 2 n } , } } \end{array}
$$

$x _ { k }$ are non-increasing

and the upper bound goes to zero as $n  \infty .$

• So the even subsequence $2 n x _ { 2 n } \to 0$ , it now suffices to show the odd subsequence $( 2 n + 1 ) x _ { 2 n + 1 } \to 0 .$

• Write

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { ( 2 n + 1 ) x _ { 2 n + 1 } = 2 n \cdot x _ { 2 n + 1 } + 1 \cdot x _ { 2 n + 1 } } & { { } } \\ { \leq 2 n \cdot x _ { 2 n } + 1 \cdot x _ { 2 n + 1 } } & { { } } \\ { \overset { n \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0 , } \end{array}
$$

$x _ { k }$ are non-increasing

where the first term converges by what we showed above, and the second by assumption.

$$

for any $1 \leq a < b < \infty$

b. Prove that if $f$ satisfies

$$

for all $t \in [ 1 , \infty )$ , then

$$

$$

## 3.14 Spring 2014 # 2

Let $\left\{ a _ { n } \right\}$ be a sequence of real numbers such that

$$
\left\{ b _ { n } \right\} \in \ell ^ { 2 } ( \mathbb { N } ) \implies \sum a _ { n } b _ { n } < \infty .
$$

Show that $\sum a _ { n } ^ { 2 } < \infty$

Note: Assume $a _ { n } , b _ { n }$ are all non-negative.

Have someone check!

## Solution:

• Define a sequence of operators

$$
T _ { N } : \ell ^ { 2 } \to \ell ^ { 1 }
$$

$$
\{ b _ { n } \} \mapsto \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } a _ { n } b _ { n } .
$$

• By assumption, these are well defined: the image is $\ell ^ { 1 }$ since $| T _ { N } ( \{ b _ { n } \} ) | < \infty$ for all N and all $\{ b _ { n } \} \in \ell ^ { 2 }$

• So each $T _ { N } \in \left( \ell ^ { 2 } \right) ^ { \vee }$ is a linear functional on $\ell ^ { 2 } .$

• For each $x \in \ell ^ { 2 }$ , we have $\| T _ { N } ( x ) \| _ { \mathbb { R } } = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } a _ { n } b _ { n } < \infty$ by assumption, so each $T _ { N }$ is pointwise bounded.

• By the Uniform Boundedness Principle, sup N $\| T _ { N } \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } < \infty$

• Define $T = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } T _ { N }$ , then $\| T \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } < \infty$

• By the Riesz Representation theorem,

$$
\sqrt { \sum a _ { n } ^ { 2 } } : = \| \{ a _ { n } \} \| _ { \ell ^ { 2 } } = \| T \| _ { ( \ell ^ { 2 } ) ^ { \vee } } = \| T \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } < \infty .
$$

• So $\sum a _ { n } ^ { 2 } < \infty$

## 4 Measure Theory: Sets

## 4.1 Spring 2020 # 2

Let $m _ { * }$ denote the Lebesgue outer measure on R.

a.. Prove that for every $E \subseteq \mathbb { R }$ there exists a Borel set B containing E such that

$$
m _ { * } ( B ) = m _ { * } ( E ) .
$$

b.. Prove that if $E \subseteq \mathbb { R }$ has the property that

$$
m _ { * } ( A ) = m _ { * } ( A \bigcap E ) + m _ { * } ( A \bigcap E ^ { c } )
$$

for every set $A \subseteq \mathbb { R }$ , then there exists a Borel set $B \subseteq \mathbb { R }$ such that $E = B \setminus N$ with $m _ { * } ( N ) = 0$

Be sure to address the case when $m _ { * } ( E ) = \infty$

Concepts Used:

• Definition of outer measure:

$$
m _ { * } ( E ) = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { \{ Q _ { j } \} \ni E } \sum | Q _ { j } |
$$

where $\{ Q _ { j } \}$ is a countable collection of closed cubes.

• Break R into $\textstyle { \prod _ { n \in \mathbb { Z } } [ n , n + 1 ) }$ , each with finite measure.

• Theorem: $m _ { * } ( Q ) = \lvert Q \rvert$ for Q a closed cube (i.e. the outer measure equals the volume).

## Solution:

Proof .

$m _ { * } ( Q ) \leq | Q | \colon$

• Since $Q \subseteq Q , Q \to Q$ and $m _ { * } ( Q ) \leq | Q |$ since $m _ { * }$ is an infimum over such coverings.

$| Q | \leq m _ { * } ( Q ) ;$

• Fix $\varepsilon > 0 .$

• Let $\{ Q _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } \Rightarrow Q$ be arbitrary, it suffices to show that

$$
\left| Q \right| \leq \left( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } \left| Q _ { i } \right| \right) + \varepsilon .
$$

• Pick open cubes $S _ { i }$ such that $Q _ { i } \subseteq S _ { i }$ and $| Q _ { i } | \leq | S _ { i } | \leq ( 1 + \varepsilon ) | Q _ { i } | ,$

• Then $\{ S _ { i } \} \ni Q ,$ so by compactness of Q pick a finite subcover with N elements.

• Note

$$
Q \subseteq \bigcup _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } S _ { i } \implies | Q | \leq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } | S _ { i } | \leq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } ( 1 + \varepsilon ) | Q _ { j } | \leq ( 1 + \varepsilon ) \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } | Q _ { i } | .
$$

• Taking an infimum over coverings on the RHS preserves the inequality, so

$$
| Q | \leq ( 1 + \varepsilon ) m _ { * } ( Q )
$$

• Take $\varepsilon \to 0$ to obtain final inequality.

a.

• If $m _ { * } ( E ) = \infty .$ then take $B = \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ since $m ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } ) = \infty$

• Suppose $N : = m _ { * } ( E ) < \infty$

• Since $m _ { * } ( E )$ is an infimum, by definition, for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists a covering by closed cubes $\{ Q _ { i } ( \varepsilon ) \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } \Longrightarrow E$ depending on ε such that

$$
\sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } \left| Q _ { i } ( \varepsilon ) \right| < N + \varepsilon .
$$

• For each fixed n, set $\varepsilon _ { n } = { \frac { 1 } { n } }$ to produce such a covering $\{ Q _ { i } ( \varepsilon _ { n } ) \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty }$ and set $B _ { n } : =$ $\bigcup _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } Q _ { i } ( \varepsilon _ { n } ) .$

• The outer measure of cubes is equal to the sum of their volumes, so

$$
m _ { * } ( B _ { n } ) = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } | Q _ { i } ( \varepsilon _ { n } ) | < N + \varepsilon _ { n } = N + \frac { 1 } { n } .
$$

• Now set $B : = \bigcap _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } B _ { n } .$

– Since $E \subseteq B _ { n }$ for every $n , E \subseteq B$

– Since B is a countable intersection of countable unions of closed sets, B is Borel.

– Since $B _ { n } \subseteq B$ for every n, we can apply subadditivity to obtain the inequality

$$
E \subseteq B \subseteq B _ { n } \implies N \leq m _ { * } ( B ) \leq m _ { * } ( B _ { n } ) < N + \frac { 1 } { n } \quad \mathrm { f o r a l l } \quad n \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { \geq 1 } .
$$

• This forces $m _ { * } ( E ) = m _ { * } ( B )$

b.

Suppose $m _ { * } ( E ) < \infty$

• By (a), find a Borel set $B \supset E$ such that $m _ { * } ( B ) = m _ { * } ( E )$

• Note that $E \subseteq B \implies B \bigcap E = E$ and $B \bigcap { E ^ { c } } = B \setminus E .$

• By assumption,

$$
m _ { * } ( B ) = m _ { * } ( B \bigcap E ) + m _ { * } ( B \bigcap E ^ { c } )
$$

$$
m _ { * } ( E ) = m _ { * } ( E ) + m _ { * } ( B \setminus E )
$$

$$
m _ { * } ( E ) - m _ { * } ( E ) = m _ { * } ( B \setminus E ) \qquad \mathrm { s i n c e } \ m _ { * } ( E ) < \infty
$$

$$
\implies m _ { * } ( B \setminus E ) = 0 .
$$

• So take $N = B \setminus E ;$ this shows $m _ { * } ( N ) = 0 { \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } } E = B \setminus ( B \setminus E ) = B \setminus N .$

If $m _ { * } ( E ) = \infty \colon$

• Apply result to $\begin{array} { r } { E _ { R } : = E \bigcap [ R , R + 1 ) ^ { n } \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { n } { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ } } R \in \mathbb { Z } , \operatorname { s o } { \cal E } = \prod _ { R } E _ { R } } \end{array}$

• Obtain $B _ { R } , N _ { R }$ such that $E _ { R } = B _ { R } \setminus N _ { R } , m _ { * } ( E _ { R } ) = m _ { * } ( B _ { R } )$ , and $m _ { * } ( N _ { R } ) = 0$

• Note that

$- \ B : = \bigcup _ { R } B _ { R }$ is a union of Borel sets and thus still Borel

$$
- \ E = \bigcup _ { \mathbf { r } } E _ { R }
$$

$$
- \ N : = \ddot { B } \setminus E
$$

$- \ N ^ { \prime } : = \bigcup _ { R } N _ { R }$ is a union of null sets and thus still null

• Since $E _ { R } \subset B _ { R }$ for every R, we have $E \subset B$

• We can compute

$$
N = B \setminus E = \left( \bigcup _ { R } B _ { R } \right) \setminus \left( \bigcup _ { R } E _ { R } \right) \subseteq \bigcup _ { R } ( B _ { R } \setminus E _ { R } ) = \bigcup _ { R } N _ { R } : = N ^ { \prime }
$$

where $m _ { * } ( N ^ { \prime } ) = 0$ since $N ^ { \prime }$ is null, and thus subadditivity forces $m _ { * } ( N ) = 0 .$

## 4.2 Fall 2019 # 3.

Let $( X , B , \mu )$ be a measure space with $\mu ( X ) = 1$ and $\{ B _ { n } \} _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty }$ be a sequence of B-measurable subsets of X, and

$$
B : = \left\{ x \in X \ \left| \ x \in B _ { n } { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ i n f i n i t e l y ~ m a n y ~ } } n \right. \right\} .
$$

a. Argue that B is also a B-measurable subset of X.

b. Prove that if $\sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mu ( B _ { n } ) < \infty$ then $\mu ( B ) = 0$

c. Prove that if $\sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mu ( B _ { n } ) = \infty$ and the sequence of set complements $\{ B _ { n } ^ { c } \} _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty }$ satisfies

$$
\mu \left( \bigcap _ { n = k } ^ { K } B _ { n } ^ { c } \right) = \prod _ { n = k } ^ { K } \left( 1 - \mu \left( B _ { n } \right) \right)
$$

for all positive integers k and K with $k < K$ , then $\mu ( B ) = 1$

Hint: Use the fact that $1 - x \leq e ^ { - x }$ for al l x.

Concepts Used:

• Borel-Cantelli: for a sequence of sets $X _ { n }$

$$
\{ x \ { | \ x \in X _ { n } { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ i n f i n i t e l y ~ m a n y ~ } } n \} } = \bigcap _ { N \geq 1 } \bigcup _ { n \geq N } X _ { n } = \operatorname* { l i m s u p } _ { n } X _ { n }
$$

$$
\left\{ x \mid x \in X _ { n } { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ a l l ~ b u t ~ f i n i t e l y ~ m a n y ~ } } n \right\} = \bigcup _ { N \geq 1 } \bigcap _ { n \geq N } X _ { n } = \operatorname* { l i m i n f } X _ { n } .
$$

• Properties of logs and exponentials:

$$
\prod _ { n } e ^ { x _ { n } } = e ^ { \Sigma _ { n } x _ { n } } { \mathrm { a n d } } \sum _ { n } \log ( x _ { n } ) = \log \left( \prod _ { n } x _ { n } \right) .
$$

• Tails of convergent sums vanish.

• Continuity of measure: $B _ { n } \ \searrow \ B$ and $\mu ( B _ { 0 } ) < \infty$ implies $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \mu ( B _ { n } ) \ = \ \mu ( B )$ ), and $B _ { n } \nearrow B \implies \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \mu ( B _ { n } ) = \mu ( B )$

## Solution:

Proof (of a).

• The Borel σ-algebra is closed under countable unions/intersections/complements,

• B = lim sup $B _ { n } = \cap _ { N \geq 1 } \cup _ { n \geq N } B _ { n }$ is an intersection of unions of measurable sets. n

Proof (of b).

• Tails of convergent sums vanish, so

$$
\sum _ { n \geq N } \mu ( B _ { n } ) \xrightarrow { N \to \infty } 0 .
$$

• Also,

$$
B _ { M } : = \bigcap _ { N = 1 } ^ { M } \bigcup _ { n \geq N } B _ { n } \searrow _ { \times } B .
$$

• A computation:

$$
\mu ( B ) : = \mu \left( \bigcap _ { N \geq 1 } \bigcup _ { n \geq N } B _ { n } \right)
$$

$$
\leq \mu \left( \bigcup _ { n \geq N } B _ { n } \right)
$$

$$
\leq \sum _ { n \geq N } \mu ( B _ { n } )
$$

∀N

$$
{ \stackrel { N \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } } 0 ,
$$

where we’ve used that we’re intersecting over fewer sets and this can only increase measure.

## 4.3 Spring 2019 # 2

Let B denote the set of all Borel subsets of R and $\mu : B \to [ 0 , \infty )$ denote a finite Borel measure on R.

a. Prove that if $\left\{ F _ { k } \right\}$ is a sequence of Borel sets for which $F _ { k } \supseteq F _ { k + 1 }$ for all $k ,$ then

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \mu \left( F _ { k } \right) = \mu \left( \bigcap _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } F _ { k } \right)
$$

b. Suppose $\mu$ has the property that $\mu ( E ) = 0$ for every $E \in B$ with Lebesgue measure $m ( E ) = 0$ Prove that for every $\epsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ so that if $E \in B$ with $m ( E ) < \delta _ { \mathrm { { f } } }$ then $\mu ( E ) < \varepsilon$

## Concepts Used:

• Proof of continuity of measure.

• Using limsup/liminf sets (intersections of unions and vice-versa) and (sub)additivity to bound measures.

– Control over lower bound: use tails of convergent sums

– Control over upper bound: use rapidly converging coefficients like $\sum 1 / 2 ^ { n }$

• Convergent sums have vanishing tails.

• Intersecting over more sets can only lose measure, taking a union over more can only gain measure.

• Similarly intersecting over fewer sets can only gain measure, and taking a union over fewer sets can only lose measure.

## Strategy:

Use a limsup or liminf of sets and continuity of measure. Note that choosing a limsup vs a liminf is fiddly – for one choice, you can only get one of the bounds you need, for the other choice you can get both.

## Solution:

Proof $( o f a ) .$ • Observation: $\mu$ finite means $\mu ( E ) < \infty$ for all $E \in B ,$ , which we’ll need in several places.

• Prove a more general statement: for any measure $\mu ,$

$$
\mu ( F _ { 1 } ) < \infty , F _ { k } \searrow F \implies \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \mu ( F _ { k } ) = \mu ( F ) ,
$$

where $F _ { k } \searrow F$ means $F _ { 1 } \supseteq F _ { 2 } \supseteq \cdots { \mathrm { w i t h } } \bigcap _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } F _ { k } = F .$

– Note that $\mu ( F )$ makes sense: each $F _ { k } \in B .$ , which is a σ-algebra and closed under countable intersections.

• Take disjoint annuli by setting $E _ { k } : = F _ { k } \setminus F _ { k + 1 }$

• Funny step: write

$$
F _ { 1 } = F \mathrm { I I } \prod _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } E _ { k } .
$$

– This is because $x \in F _ { 1 }$ iff x is in every $F _ { k } .$ , so in $F ,$ or

$- \ x \notin F _ { 1 }$ but $x \in F _ { 2 }$ , noting incidentally $x \in F _ { 3 } , F _ { 4 } , \cdot \cdot \cdot , \mathbf { o r } .$

$- \ x \notin F _ { 2 }$ but $x \in F _ { 3 }$ , and so on.

• Now take measures, and note that we get a telescoping sum:

$$
 \begin{array} { r l } & { \| A ( F _ { i } ) - \mu ( F ) + \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mu ( E _ { k } ) \| } \\ & { \qquad \leq \operatorname* { i n f } ( F _ { i } ) + \ \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mu ( E _ { k } ) } \\ & { \qquad - \mu ( F ^ { 0 } ) + \underbrace { { \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { 3 } } } \mu ( E _ { k } ) } _ { \mathrm { K \setminus { S } \setminus \infty } } \ } \\ & { \qquad = \mu ( F ) + \ \underbrace { { \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { 3 } } } \mu ( E _ { k } ) } _ { \mathrm { K \setminus { S } \setminus \infty } } \ \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } ( F ( E _ { k } ) \ \xi _ { j + 1 } ) } \\ & { \qquad = \mu ( F ) + \ \underbrace { { \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { 3 } } } \mu ( E _ { k } ) } _ { \mathrm { K \setminus { S } \setminus \infty } } \ \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } ( F ( E _ { k } ) \ \xi _ { j } ) \qquad \qquad { \mathrm { t o b s i n t i t i c d } } } \\ & { \qquad \leq \mu ( F ) + \ \underbrace { { \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { 3 } } } \mu ( E _ { k } ) } _ { \mathrm { K \setminus { S } \setminus \infty } } \ \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } ( F ( E _ { k } ) \ \xi _ { j } ) \qquad \mathrm { t r o b \ c i p t i t i c d } } \\ & { \qquad \leq \mu ( F ) + \ \underbrace { { \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { 3 } } } \mu ( E _ { k } ) } _ { \mathrm { K \setminus { S } \setminus \infty } } \ ( \mu ( F ) ) - \  ( F ) \} + \ \mu ( F ) \ \xi _ { j } ) \ \qquad \cdots \qquad } \\ & { \qquad + \ ( \mu ( F ) - \mu ( F ) ) - \ \mu ( F ) \xi _ { j } ) \qquad \mathrm { t a n d } \ ( \mu ( F _ { k } ) ) - \ \mu ( F _ { k } ) ) } \\ &  \qquad = \mu ( F ) + \ \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mu ( F _ { k } ) - \exp \end{array}
$$

• Justifying the measure subtraction: the general statement is that for any pair of sets $A \subseteq X , \mu ( X \setminus A ) = \mu ( X ) - \mu ( A )$ when $\mu ( A ) < \infty { : }$

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l r l } & { X = A [ \operatorname { I } ( X \setminus A ) } \\ & { \implies \mu ( X ) = \mu ( A ) + \mu ( X \setminus A ) } \\ & { \implies \mu ( X ) - \mu ( A ) = \mu ( X \setminus A ) } & & { { \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } } \mu ( A ) < \infty . } \end{array} }
$$

4.3 Spring 2019 $\# 2 \mathrm { ~ }$

• Now use that $\mu ( F _ { 1 } ) < \infty$ to justify subtracting it from both sides:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mu ( F _ { 1 } ) = \mu ( F ) + \mu ( F _ { 1 } ) - \underset { N \to \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \mu ( F _ { N + 1 } ) } \\ & { \implies 0 = \mu ( F _ { 1 } ) - \underset { N \to \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \ \mu ( F _ { N + 1 } ) } \end{array}
$$

## Proof $( o f b )$

• Toward a contradiction, negate the implication: there exists an $\varepsilon > 0$ such that for all δ, there exists an $E \in B$

$$
m ( E ) < \delta
$$

but

$$
\mu ( E ) > \varepsilon .
$$

– Goal: produce a set A with $m ( A ) = 0$ but $\mu ( A ) \neq 0 .$

• Take a sequence $\delta _ { n } = \alpha ( n )$ , some function to be determined later, produce sets $E _ { n }$ with

$$
m ( E _ { n } ) < \delta _ { n }
$$

but

• Set

$$
A _ { M } : = \bigcap _ { N = 1 } ^ { M } \bigcup _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } E _ { n } : = \bigcap _ { N = 1 } ^ { M } F _ { N } \qquad F _ { N } : = \bigcup _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } E _ { n } .
$$

– Observation: $F _ { N } \supseteq F _ { N + 1 }$ for all $N _ { ; }$ , since the right-hand side involves taking a union over fewer sets.

– Notation: define

$$
A _ { \infty } : = \bigcap _ { N = 1 } ^ { \infty } { \bigcup _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } E _ { n } } .
$$

• Bounding the Lebesgue measure m from above:

$$
m ( A _ { \infty } ) : = m \left( \bigcap _ { N = 1 } ^ { \infty } { \bigcup ^ { \infty } { E _ { n } } } \right)
$$

$$
\leq m \left( \bigcup _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } E _ { n } \right)
$$

$$
\leq \sum _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } m ( E _ { n } )
$$

by countable subadditivity

$$
\leq \sum _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } \alpha ( n )
$$

$$
\stackrel { N  \infty } {  } 0 ,
$$

where we’ve used that intersecting over fewer sets (i.e. none) can only increase measure in the first bound.

– We have control over the sequence $\alpha ( n )$ , so we can choose it to be summable so that the tails converge to zero as rapidly as we’d like.

– So e.g. for any $\varepsilon _ { 1 } > 0$ , we can choose $\alpha ( n ) : = \varepsilon _ { 1 } / 2 ^ { n }$ , then

$$
\sum _ { n = N } ^ { \infty } \alpha ( n ) \leq \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { \varepsilon _ { 1 } } { 2 ^ { n } } } = \varepsilon _ { 1 } \to 0 .
$$

Bounding the µ measure from below: 4.3 Spring 2019 # 2

$$
\mu ( A _ { \infty } ) : = \mu \left( \bigcap _ { N = 1 } ^ { \infty } F _ { N } \right)
$$

$$
= \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } \mu ( F _ { N } )
$$

by part (1)

## 4.4 Fall 2018 # 2

Let $E \subset \mathbb { R }$ be a Lebesgue measurable set. Show that there is a Borel set $B \subset E$ such that $m ( E \setminus B ) = 0$

Move this to review notes to clean things up.

What a mess, redo!!

## Concepts Used:

• Definition of measurability: there exists an open $O \supset E$ such that $m _ { * } ( O \setminus E ) < \varepsilon$ for all $\varepsilon > 0 .$

• Theorem: E is Lebesgue measurable iff there exists a closed set $F \subseteq E$ such that $m _ { * } ( E \setminus F ) < \varepsilon$ for all $\varepsilon > 0$ .

• Every $F _ { \sigma } , G _ { \delta }$ is Borel.

• Claim: E is measurable $\Longleftrightarrow$ for every ε there exist $F _ { \varepsilon } \subset E \subset G _ { \varepsilon }$ with $F _ { \varepsilon }$ closed and $G _ { \varepsilon }$ open and $m ( G _ { \varepsilon } \setminus E ) < \varepsilon$ and $m ( E \setminus F _ { \varepsilon } ) < \varepsilon .$

– Proof: existence of $G _ { \varepsilon }$ is the definition of measurability.

– Existence of $F _ { \varepsilon } \colon ?$

• Claim: E is measurable =⇒ there exists an open $O \supseteq E$ such that $m ( O \backslash E ) = 0 .$

– Since E is measurable, for each $n \in \mathbb { N }$ choose $G _ { n } \supseteq E$ such that $m _ { * } ( G _ { n } \backslash E ) < \frac { 1 } { n } .$ – Set $O _ { N } : = \bigcap _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } G _ { n }$ and $O : = \bigcap _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } G _ { n } .$

– Suppose E is bounded.

♦ Note $O _ { N } \searrow O$ and $m _ { * } ( O _ { 1 } ) < \infty { \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } } E$ is bounded, since in this case

$$
m _ { * } ( G _ { n } \setminus E ) = m _ { * } ( G _ { 1 } ) - m _ { * } ( E ) < 1 \iff m _ { * } ( G _ { 1 } ) < m _ { * } ( E ) + \frac { 1 } { n } < \infty .
$$

♦ Note $O _ { N } \setminus E \setminus O \setminus E$ since $O _ { N } \setminus E : = O _ { N } \bigcap E ^ { c } \supseteq O _ { N + 1 } \bigcap E ^ { c }$ for all N , and again $m _ { * } ( O _ { 1 } \setminus E ) < \infty$

♦ So it’s valid to apply continuity of measure from above:

$$
\begin{array} { l } { m _ { * } ( O \setminus E ) = \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } m _ { * } ( O _ { N } \setminus E ) } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad \leq \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } m _ { * } ( G _ { N } \setminus E ) } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad = \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } \frac { 1 } { N } = 0 , } \end{array}
$$

where the inequality uses subadditivity on $\bigcap _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } G _ { n } \subseteq G _ { N }$ – Suppose E is unbounded.

♦ Write $E ^ { k } = E \bigcap [ k , k + 1 ] ^ { d } \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ as the intersection of E with an annulus, and note that $\begin{array} { r } { E = \dot { \mathrm { L I } } _ { k \in \mathbb { N } } E _ { k } . } \end{array}$

♦ Each $E _ { k }$ is bounded, so apply the previous case to obtain $O _ { k } \supseteq E _ { k }$ with $m ( O _ { k } \setminus E _ { k } ) = 0 .$

♦ So write $O _ { k } = E _ { k } [ \mathrm { I } N _ { k }$ where $N _ { k } : = O _ { k } \setminus E _ { k }$ is a null set.

♦ Define $O = \bigcup O _ { k }$ , note that $E \subseteq O$

♦ Now note

$$
\begin{array} { c } { { \cal { O } \backslash E = ( \coprod _ { k } { \cal O } _ { k } ) \setminus ( \coprod _ { K } E _ { k } ) } } \\ { { \mathrm { } \subseteq \coprod _ { k } \big ( { \cal O } _ { k } \setminus { \cal E } _ { k } \big ) } } \\ { { \Longrightarrow m _ { * } ( { \cal O } \setminus { \cal E } ) \le m _ { * } \left( \coprod _ { k } \big \setminus { \cal E } _ { k } \right) \big ) = 0 , } } \end{array}
$$

since any countable union of null sets is again null.

– So $O \supseteq E$ with $m ( O \backslash E ) = 0$

## • Theorem: since E is measurable, $E ^ { c }$ is measurable

– Proof: It suffices to write $E ^ { c }$ as the union of two measurable sets, $E ^ { c } = S \bigcup E ^ { c } - S )$ where S is to be determined.

– We’ll produce an S such that $m _ { * } ( E ^ { c } - S ) = 0$ and use the fact that any subset of a null set is measurable.

– Since E is measurable, for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists an open $\mathcal { O } _ { \varepsilon } \supseteq E$ such that $m _ { * } ( { \mathcal { O } } _ { \varepsilon } \setminus E ) < \varepsilon$

– Take the sequence $\left\{ \varepsilon _ { n } : = { \frac { 1 } { n } } \right\}$ to produce a sequence of sets ${ \mathcal { O } } _ { n }$

– Note that each $\mathcal { O } _ { n } ^ { c }$ is closed and

$$
{ \mathcal { O } } _ { n } \supseteq E \iff { \mathcal { O } } _ { n } ^ { c } \subseteq E ^ { c } .
$$

– Set $S : = \bigcup \mathcal { O } _ { n } ^ { c }$ , which is a union of closed sets, thus an $F _ { \sigma }$ set, thus Borel, thus n measurable.

– Note that $S \subseteq E ^ { c }$ since each $\mathcal { O } _ { n } \subseteq E ^ { c }$

– Note that

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { E ^ { c } \setminus S : = E ^ { c } \setminus \left( \displaystyle \bigcup _ { u = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mathcal { O } _ { u } ^ { c } \right) } \\ { } & { = E ^ { c } \bigcap \left( \displaystyle \bigcup _ { u = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mathcal { O } _ { u } ^ { c } \right) ^ { c } \quad \mathrm { d e f i n i t i o n ~ o f ~ s e t ~ m i m a t } } \\ { } & { = E ^ { c } \bigcap \left( \displaystyle \bigcap _ { u = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mathcal { O } _ { u } \right) ^ { c } \quad \mathrm { D e ~ M o r g a n i t ~ l a x } } \\ { } & { = E ^ { c } \bigcup \left( \displaystyle \bigcap _ { u = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mathcal { O } _ { u } \right) } \\ { } & { = E ^ { c } \bigcup \left( \displaystyle \bigcap _ { u = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mathcal { O } _ { u } \right) } \\ { } & { = \left( \displaystyle \bigcap _ { u = 1 } ^ { \infty } \mathcal { O } _ { u } \right) \setminus E } \\ { } & { \leq \mathcal { O } _ { u } \setminus E \mathrm { ~ f o r e v e r ~ } N \in \mathbb { N } . } \end{array}
$$

Then by subadditivity,

$$
m _ { * } ( E ^ { c } \setminus S ) \leq m _ { * } ( \mathcal { O } _ { N } \setminus E ) \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \quad \forall N \implies m _ { * } ( E ^ { c } \setminus S ) = 0 .
$$

– Thus $E ^ { c } \backslash S$ is measurable.

## Solution:

• Since E is measurable, $E ^ { c }$ is measurable.

• Since $E ^ { c }$ is measurable exists an open $O \supseteq E ^ { c }$ such that $m ( O \setminus E ^ { c } ) = 0$

• Set $B : = O ^ { c }$ , then $O \supseteq E ^ { c } \iff { \mathcal { O } } ^ { c } \subseteq E \iff B \subseteq E .$

• Computing measures yields

$$
E \setminus B : = E \setminus \mathcal { O } ^ { c } : = E \bigcap ( \mathcal { O } ^ { c } ) ^ { c } = E \bigcap \mathcal { O } = \mathcal { O } \bigcap ( E ^ { c } ) ^ { c } : = \mathcal { O } \setminus E ^ { c } ,
$$

thus $m ( E \setminus B ) = m ( \mathcal { O } \setminus E ^ { c } ) = 0 .$

• Since O is open, B is closed and thus Borel.

## 4.5 Spring 2018 # 1

Define

$$
E : = \left\{ x \in \mathbb { R } : \left| x - { \frac { p } { q } } \right| < q ^ { - 3 } { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ i n f i n i t e l y ~ m a n y ~ } } p , q \in \mathbb { N } \right\} .
$$

Prove that $m ( E ) = 0 $

## Concepts Used:

• Borel-Cantelli: If $\{ E _ { k } \} _ { k \in \mathbb { Z } } \subset 2 ^ { \mathbb { R } }$ is a countable collection of Lebesgue measurable sets with $\sum _ { k \in \mathbb { Z } } m ( E _ { k } ) < \infty ,$ then almost every $x \in \mathbb { R }$ is in at most finitely many $E _ { k }$

– Equivalently (?), m $( \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } E _ { k } ) = 0$ , where lim sup $E _ { k } = \bigcap _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } \bigcup _ { j \geq k } E _ { j }$ , the elements k→∞

which are in $E _ { k }$ for infinitely many k.

## Solution:

• Strategy: Borel-Cantelli.

• We’ll show that $m ( E ) \bigcap [ n , n + 1 ] = 0$ for all $n \in \mathbb { Z } ;$ then the result follows from

$$
m ( E ) = m \left( \bigcup _ { n \in \mathbb { Z } } E { \bigcap } [ n , n + 1 ] \right) \leq \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } m ( E { \bigcap } [ n , n + 1 ] ) = 0 .
$$

• By translation invariance of measure, it suffices to show $m ( E \bigcap [ 0 , 1 ] ) = 0 .$

– So WLOG, replace E with $E \bigcap [ 0 , 1 ]$

• Define

$$
E _ { j } : = \left\{ x \in [ 0 , 1 ] \Big \vert \exists p \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { \geq 0 } \mathrm { ~ s . t . ~ } \Bigg | x - \frac { p } { j } \Bigg | < \frac { 1 } { j ^ { 3 } } \right\} .
$$

– Note that $\begin{array} { r } { E _ { j } \subseteq \prod _ { p \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { \geq 0 } } B _ { j ^ { - 3 } } \left( \frac { p } { j } \right) } \end{array}$ , i.e. a union over integers p of intervals of radius $1 / j ^ { 3 }$ around the points $p / j$ . Since $1 / j ^ { 3 } < 1 / j$ , this union is in fact disjoint.

• Importantly, note that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { j \to \infty } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \cal E } _ { j } : = \bigcap _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } \bigcup _ { j = n } ^ { \infty } E _ { j } = E
$$

since

$x \in$ lim sup $E _ { j } \iff x \in E _ { j }$ for infinitely many j j

⇐⇒ there are infinitely many j for which there exist a p such that $\left| x - { \frac { p } { j } } \right| < j ^ { - 3 }$

⇐⇒ there are infinitely many such pairs $p , j$

$$
\iff x \in E .
$$

• Intersecting with [0, 1], we can write $E _ { j }$ as a union of intervals:

$$
E _ { j } = \left( 0 , j ^ { - 3 } \right) \quad \mathrm { U } \quad B _ { j ^ { - 3 } } \left( \frac { 1 } { j } \right) \amalg B _ { j ^ { - 3 } } \left( \frac { 2 } { j } \right) \amalg \cdot \cdot \cdot \amalg B _ { j ^ { - 3 } } \left( \frac { j - 1 } { j } \right) \quad \mathrm { U } \quad ( 1 - j ^ { - 3 } , 1 ) ,
$$

where we’ve separated out the “boundary” terms to emphasize that they are balls about 0 and 1 intersected with [0, 1].

• Since $E _ { j }$ is a union of open sets, it is Borel and thus Lebesgue measurable.

• Computing the measure of $E _ { j }$ :

– For a fixed j, there are exactly $j + 1$ possible choices for a numerator $( 0 , 1 , \cdots , j )$ thus there are exactly $j + 1$ sets appearing in the above decomposition.

– The first and last intervals are length $\textstyle { \frac { 1 } { j ^ { 3 } } }$

– The remaining $( j + 1 ) - 2 = j - 1$ intervals are twice this length, $\frac { 2 } { j ^ { 3 } }$ – Thus

$$
m ( E _ { j } ) = 2 \left( { \frac { 1 } { j ^ { 3 } } } \right) + ( j - 1 ) \left( { \frac { 2 } { j ^ { 3 } } } \right) = { \frac { 2 } { j ^ { 2 } } }
$$

• Note that

$$
\sum _ { j \in \mathbb { N } } m ( E _ { j } ) = 2 \sum _ { j \in \mathbb { N } } \frac { 1 } { j ^ { 2 } } < \infty ,
$$

which converges by the p-test for sums.

• But then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { m ( E ) = m ( \operatorname* { l i m s u p } _ { j } ) } & { } \\ & { = m ( \displaystyle \bigcap _ { n \in \mathbb { N } / \geq n } E _ { j } ) } \\ & { \leq m ( \displaystyle \bigcup _ { j \geq N } E _ { j } ) \quad \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ e v e r y ~ } N } \\ & { \leq \displaystyle \sum _ { j \geq N } m ( E _ { j } ) } \\ & { \overset { N \to \infty } { \to } 0 \quad . } \end{array}
$$

• Thus E is measurable as a subset of a null set and $m ( E ) = 0$

$$

is a bijection from the class of Lebesgue measurable sets of $[ 0 , \infty )$ to itself.

## 4.7 Spring 2017 # 2

a. Let $\mu$ be a measure on a measurable space $( X , M )$ and f a positive measurable function.

Define a measure λ by

$$
\lambda ( E ) : = \int _ { E } f \ d \mu , \quad E \in { \mathcal { M } }
$$

Show that for g any positive measurable function,

$$
\int _ { X } g \ d \lambda = \int _ { X } f g \ d \mu
$$

b. Let $E \subset \mathbb { R }$ be a measurable set such that

$$
\int _ { E } x ^ { 2 } \ d m = 0 .
$$

Show that $m ( E ) = 0$

Concepts Used:

• Absolute continuity of measures: $\lambda \ll \mu \iff E \in { \mathcal { M } } , \mu ( E ) = 0 \implies \lambda ( E ) = 0 .$

• Radon-Nikodym: if $\lambda \ll \mu ,$ then there exists a measurable function $\frac { \partial \lambda } { \partial \mu } : = f$ where $\lambda ( E ) = \int _ { E } f d \mu .$

• Chebyshev’s inequality:

$$
A _ { c } : = \Big \{ x \in X \ \Big | \ | f ( x ) | \geq c \Big \} \implies \mu ( A _ { c } ) \leq c ^ { - p } \int _ { A _ { c } } | f | ^ { p } d \mu \quad \forall 0 < p < \infty .
$$

## Solution:

a.

• Strategy: use approximation by simple functions to show absolute continuity and apply Radon-Nikodym

• Claim: $\lambda \ll \mu ,$ i.e. $\mu ( E ) = 0 \Longrightarrow \lambda ( E ) = 0 .$

– Note that if this holds, by Radon-Nikodym, $f = { \frac { \partial \lambda } { \partial \mu } } \implies d \lambda = f d \mu$ , which would yield

$$
\int g \ d \lambda = \int g f \ d \mu .
$$

• So let E be measurable and suppose $\mu ( E ) = 0$

• Then

$$
\lambda ( E ) : = \int _ { E } f \ d \mu = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \left\{ \int _ { E } s _ { n } d \mu \Bigm | \ s _ { n } : = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } c _ { j } \mu ( E _ { j } ) , \ : s _ { n } \ : \nearrow \ f \right\}
$$

where we take a sequence of simple functions increasing to $f .$

• But since each $E _ { j } \subseteq E _ { \mathrm { : } }$ we must have $\mu ( E _ { j } ) = 0$ for any such $E _ { j }$ , so every such $s _ { n }$ must be zero and thus $\lambda ( E ) = 0$

## What is the final step in this approximation?

b.

• Set $g ( x ) = x ^ { 2 }$ , note that g is positive and measurable.

• By part (a), there exists a positive f such that for any $E \subseteq \mathbb { R }$

$$
\int _ { E } g \ d m = \int _ { E } g f \ d \mu
$$

– The LHS is zero by assumption and thus so is the RHS.

$m \ll \mu$ by construction.

– Note that $g f$ is positive.

• Define $A _ { k } = \left\{ x \in X { \Big | } g f \cdot \chi _ { E } > { \frac { 1 } { k } } \right\}$ , for $k \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { \geq 0 }$

• Then by Chebyshev with $p = 1$ , for every k we have

$$
\mu ( A _ { k } ) \leq k \int _ { E } g f \ d \mu = 0
$$

• Then noting that $A _ { k } \setminus A : = { \Bigl \{ } x \in X { \Big | } g f \cdot \chi _ { E } ( x ) > 0 { \Bigr \} }$ , we have $\mu ( A ) = 0$

• Since $g f$ is positive, we have

$$
x \in E \iff g f \chi _ { E } ( x ) > 0 \iff x \in A
$$

so $E = A$ and $\mu ( E ) = \mu ( A )$

• But $m \ll \mu$ and $\mu ( E ) = 0$ , so we can conclude that $m ( E ) = 0 $
