## 4.15 Spring 2017 # 1

Let K be the set of numbers in [0, 1] whose decimal expansions do not use the digit 4.

We use the convention that when a decimal number ends with 4 but all other digits are different from 4, we replace the digit 4 with 399 · · ·. For example, 0.8754 = 0.8753999 · · ·.

Show that K is a compact, nowhere dense set without isolated points, and find the Lebesgue measure $m ( K )$ .

Concepts Used:

• Definition: A is nowhere dense ⇐⇒ every interval I contains a subinterval $S \subseteq A ^ { c }$

– Equivalently, the interior of the closure is empty, $\left( { \overline { { K } } } \right) ^ { \circ } = \varnothing .$

## Solution:

Claim: K is compact.

• It suffices to show that $K ^ { c } : = [ 0 , 1 ] \backslash K$ is open; Then K will be a closed and bounded subset of R and thus compact by Heine-Borel.

• Strategy: write $K ^ { c }$ as the union of open balls (since these form a basis for the Euclidean topology on R).

Do this by showing every point $x \in K ^ { c }$ is an interior point, i.e. x admits a neighborhood $N _ { x }$ such that $N _ { x } \subseteq K ^ { c }$

• Identify $K ^ { c }$ as the set of real numbers in [0, 1] whose decimal expansion does contain a 4.

– We will show that there exists a neighborhood small enough such that all points in it contain a 4 in their decimal expansions.

• Let $x \in K ^ { c }$ , suppose a 4 occurs as the kth digit, and write

$$
x = 0 . d _ { 1 } d _ { 2 } \cdots d _ { k - 1 } 4 d _ { k + 1 } \cdots = \left( \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { k } d _ { j } 1 0 ^ { - j } \right) + \left( 4 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - k } \right) + \left( \sum _ { j = k + 1 } ^ { \infty } d _ { j } 1 0 ^ { - j } \right) .
$$

• Set $r _ { x } < 1 0 ^ { - k }$ and let $y \in [ 0 , 1 ] \bigcap B _ { r _ { x } } ( x )$ be arbitrary and write

$$
y = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } c _ { j } 1 0 ^ { - j } .
$$

• Thus $| x - y | < r _ { x } < 1 0 ^ { - k }$ , and the first k digits of x and y must agree:

– We first compute the difference:

$$
x - y = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } d _ { j } 1 0 ^ { - j } - \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } c _ { j } 1 0 ^ { - j } = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } \left( d _ { j } - c _ { j } \right) 1 0 ^ { - j }
$$

– Thus (claim)

$$
| x - y | \leq \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } | d _ { j } - c _ { j } | 1 0 ^ { j } < 1 0 ^ { - k } \iff | d _ { j } - c _ { j } | = 0 \quad \forall j \leq k .
$$

– Otherwise we can note that any term $| d _ { j } - c _ { j } | \geq 1$ and there is a contribution to $| x - y |$ of at least 1 · 10−j for some $j < k$ , whereas

$$
j < k \iff 1 0 ^ { - j } > 1 0 ^ { - k } ,
$$

a contradiction.

• This means that for all $j \le k$ we have $d _ { j } = c _ { j }$ , and in particular $d _ { k } = 4 = c _ { k }$ , so y has a 4 in its decimal expansion.

• But then $K ^ { c } = \bigcup _ { x } B _ { r _ { x } } ( x )$ is a union of open sets and thus open.

Claim: K is nowhere dense and $m ( K ) = 0 { : }$

• Strategy: Show $\left( { \overline { { K } } } \right) ^ { \circ } = \varnothing .$

• Since K is closed, ${ \overline { { K } } } = K ,$ so it suffices to show that K does not properly contain any interval.

• It suffices to show $m ( K ^ { c } ) = 1$ , since this implies $m ( K ) = 0$ and since any interval has strictly positive measure, this will mean K can not contain an interval.

• As in the construction of the Cantor set, let

$- \ K _ { 0 }$ denote [0, 1] with 1 interval $\left( \frac { 4 } { 1 0 } , \frac { 5 } { 1 0 } \right)$ of length $\textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 1 0 } }$ deleted, so

$$
m ( K _ { 0 } ^ { c } ) = \frac { 1 } { 1 0 } .
$$

$- \ K _ { 1 }$ denote $K _ { 0 }$ with 9 intervals $\left( { \frac { 1 } { 1 0 0 } } , { \frac { 5 } { 1 0 0 } } \right) , \left( { \frac { 1 4 } { 1 0 0 } } , { \frac { 1 5 } { 1 0 0 } } \right) , \cdots \left( { \frac { 9 4 } { 1 0 0 } } , { \frac { 9 5 } { 1 0 0 } } \right)$ of length $\frac { 1 } { 1 0 0 }$ deleted, so

$$
m ( K _ { 1 } ^ { c } ) = \frac { 1 } { 1 0 } + \frac { 9 } { 1 0 0 } .
$$

$\mathbf { \Sigma } - \ K _ { n }$ denote $K _ { n - 1 }$ with $9 ^ { n }$ such intervals of length $\frac { 1 } { 1 0 ^ { n + 1 } }$ deleted, so

$$
m ( K _ { n } ^ { c } ) = \frac { 1 } { 1 0 } + \frac { 9 } { 1 0 0 } + \cdots + \frac { 9 ^ { n } } { 1 0 ^ { n + 1 } } .
$$

• Then compute

$$
m ( K ^ { c } ) = \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { 9 ^ { n } } { 1 0 ^ { n + 1 } } } = { \frac { 1 } { 1 0 } } \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { \infty } \left( { \frac { 9 } { 1 0 } } \right) ^ { n } = { \frac { 1 } { 1 0 } } \left( { \frac { 1 } { 1 - { \frac { 9 } { 1 0 } } } } \right) = 1 .
$$

Claim: K has no isolated points:

• A point $x \in K$ is isolated iff there there is an open ball $B _ { r } ( x )$ containing x such that $B _ { r } ( x ) \subsetneq K ^ { c }$

– So every point in this ball should have a 4 in its decimal expansion.

• Strategy: show that if $x \in K$ , every neighborhood of x intersects $K .$

• Note that $m ( K _ { n } ) = \left( \frac { 9 } { 1 0 } \right) ^ { n } { \overset { n \to \infty } { \to } } 0$

• Also note that we deleted open intervals, and the endpoints of these intervals are never deleted.

– Thus endpoints of deleted intervals are elements of K.

• Fix x. Then for every ε, by the Archimedean property of R, choose n such that $\left( { \frac { 9 } { 1 0 } } \right) ^ { n } <$ ε.

• Then there is an endpoint $x _ { n }$ of some deleted interval $I _ { n }$ satisfying

$$
| x - x _ { n } | \leq { \bigg ( } { \frac { 9 } { 1 0 } } { \bigg ) } ^ { n } < \varepsilon .
$$

• So every ball containing x contains some endpoint of a removed interval, and thus an element of K.

## 4.16 Spring 2016 # 2

Let $0 < \lambda < 1$ and construct a Cantor set $C _ { \lambda }$ by successively removing middle intervals of length λ.

Prove that $m ( C _ { \lambda } ) = 0$

## 5 Measure Theory: Functions

## 5.1 Fall 2016 # 2

Let $f , g : [ a , b ] \to \mathbb { R }$ be measurable with

$$
\int _ { a } ^ { b } f ( x ) \ d x = \int _ { a } ^ { b } g ( x ) \ d x .
$$

Show that either

1. $f ( x ) = g ( x )$ almost everywhere, or

2. There exists a measurable set $E \subset [ a , b ]$ such that

$$
\int _ { E } f ( x ) d x > \int _ { E } g ( x ) d x
$$

Concepts Used:

• Monotonicity of the Lebesgue integral: $f \leq g$ on $A \implies \int _ { A } f \leq \int _ { A } g$

## Strategy:

Take the assumption and the negation of (1) and show (2). The obvious move: define the set A where they differ. The non-obvious move: split A itself up to get a strict inequality.

## Solution:

• Write $X : = [ a , b ] ;$

• Suppose it is not the case that $\textit { f } = \textit { g }$ almost everywhere; then letting $A : =$ $\left\{ x \in X \mid f ( x ) \neq g ( x ) \right\}$ , we have $m ( A ) > 0$

• Write

$$
{ \cal A } = { \cal A } _ { 1 } [ [ { \cal A } _ { 2 } : = \{ f > g \} ] ] \{ f < g \} .
$$

• Both $A _ { i }$ are measurable:

– Since $f , g$ are measurable functions, so is $h : = f - g$

– We can write

$$
A _ { 1 } : = \Big \{ x \in X \Big | h > 0 \Big \} = h ^ { - 1 } ( ( 0 , \infty ) )
$$

$$
A _ { 2 } : = \Big \{ x \in X \Big | h < 0 \Big \} = h ^ { - 1 } ( ( - \infty , 0 ) ) ,
$$

and pullbacks of Borel sets by measurable functions are measurable.

• Then on $E ,$ we have $f ( x ) > g ( x )$ pointwise. This is preserved by monotonicity of the integral, thus

$$
f ( x ) > g ( x ) { \mathrm { ~ o n ~ } } E \implies \int _ { E } f ( x ) d x > \int _ { E } g ( x ) d x .
$$

Show that

1. $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$

2. $f$ is uniformly continuous.

3. $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { | x | \to \infty } f ( x ) = 0 .$

$$
\chi _ { E \cap ( E + x ) } ( y ) = \chi _ { E } ( y ) \chi _ { E } ( y - x )
$$

## 5.3 Spring 2021 # 1

Problem 5.3.1 (Spring 2021, 1)   
Let $( X , { \mathcal { M } } , \mu )$ be a measure space and let $E _ { n } \in { \mathcal { M } }$ be a measurable set for $n \geq 1$ . Let   
$f _ { n } : = \chi _ { E _ { n } }$ be the indicator function of the set E and show that   
a. ${ f _ { n } } \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to }$ 1 uniformly ⇐⇒ there exists $N \in  { \mathbb { N } }$ such that $E _ { n } = X$ for all $n \geq N .$   
b. $f _ { n } ( x ) \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } 1$ for almost every $x \iff$   
$\mu \left( \bigcap _ { n \geq 0 } { \bigsqcup } \left( X \setminus E _ { k } \right) \right) = 0 .$

Solution:   
Part a:   
=⇒ :   
• Suppose $\chi _ { E _ { n } }  1$ uniformly, we want to produce an N such that $n \geq N \implies x \in E _ { n }$   
for all $x \in X$   
• Take $\varepsilon : = 1 / 2 .$ By uniform convergence, for N large enough,   
$\forall n \geq N \quad | \chi _ { E _ { n } } ( x ) - 1 | < 1 / 2$ $\forall x \in X$   
$\iff \forall n \geq N \quad \chi _ { E n } ( x ) = 1$ $\forall x \in X$   
$\iff \forall n \geq N \quad x \in E n$ $\forall x \in X$ $\iff \forall n \geq N \quad E _ { n } = X ,$   
where we’ve used that $E _ { n } \subseteq X$ by definition and this shows $X \subseteq E _ { n }$ . So this N suffices.   
⇐= :   
• Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be arbitrary.   
• Choose N such that $n \geq N \implies X = E _ { n }$ . Then   
$\forall n \geq N x \in E _ { n }$ $\forall x \in X$   
$\forall n \geq N \quad \chi _ { E _ { n } } ( x ) = 1$ $\forall x \in X$   
$\forall n \geq N \quad | \chi _ { E _ { n } } ( x ) - 1 | = 0 < \varepsilon$ $\forall x \in X ,$   
so $\chi _ { E _ { n } }  1$ uniformly.   
Part b:

• Define

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { S : = \{ x \in X \Bigm \vert \chi _ { E _ { k } } ( x )  1 \} } \\ & { \quad : = \{ x \in X \Bigm \vert \forall \varepsilon , \exists N \mathrm { ~ s . t . ~ }  \chi _ { E _ { k } } ( x ) - 1  < \varepsilon , \forall k \geq N \} } \\ & { L : = \displaystyle \bigcap _ { n \geq 0 } \bigcup _ { k \geq n } ( X \setminus E _ { k } ) , } \end{array}
$$

so $S$ is the set where $f _ { n }  f$ and $X \backslash S$ is the exceptional set where $f _ { n } \ \not \to \ f$ doesn’t converge pointwise.

• Claim: $L = X \backslash S _ { : }$ , so if $x \in S \iff x \in X \setminus L$

• Proof of claim: Suppose there exists an N such that the first line below is true. Then for a fixed x, there are equivalent statements:

$$
x \in S
$$

$$
\iff \exists N \mathrm { ~ s . t . ~ } \forall \varepsilon > 0 , \quad | \chi _ { E _ { k } } ( x ) - 1 | < \varepsilon
$$

$$
\forall k \geq N
$$

$$
\iff \exists N \mathrm { ~ s . t . ~ } | \chi _ { E _ { k } } ( x ) - 1 | = 0
$$

$$
\forall k \geq N
$$

$$
\iff \exists N \ \mathrm { s . t . } \ \chi _ { E _ { k } } ( x ) = 1
$$

$$
\forall k \geq N
$$

$$
\iff \exists N { \mathrm { ~ s . t . ~ } } x \in E _ { k }
$$

$$
\forall k \geq N
$$

$$
\iff \exists N { \mathrm { ~ s . t . ~ } } x \notin X \setminus E _ { k }
$$

$$
\forall k \geq N
$$

$$
\iff \exists N \ { \mathrm { s . t . } } \ x \ \not \in \bigcup _ { k \geq N } X \setminus E _ { k }
$$

$$
\Longleftrightarrow x \not \in \bigcap _ { n \geq 0 } \bigcup X \setminus E _ { k }
$$

$$
\iff x \notin L
$$

$$
\iff { x \in X \setminus L } .
$$

• Proving the iff: $f _ { n }  f$ almost everywhere $\iff \mu ( X \setminus S ) = 0 \iff \mu ( L ) = 0 .$

## 6.1 Fall 2019 # 2

Prove that

$$
\left| { \frac { d ^ { n } } { d x ^ { n } } } { \frac { \sin x } { x } } \right| \leq { \frac { 1 } { n } }
$$

for all $x \neq 0$ and positive integers n.

$$
H i n t : \ C o n s i d e r \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \cos ( t x ) d t
$$

Solution:

Concepts Used:

• DCT

• Bounding in the right place. Don’t evaluate the actual integral!

• By induction on the number of limits we can pass through the integral.

• For $n = 1$ we first pass one derivative into the integral: let $x _ { n } \to x$ be any sequence converging to x, then

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { { \frac { \partial } { \partial x } } } & { { \frac { \partial | \mathbf { x } | ( \mathbf { x } ) } { \partial x } } = { \frac { \partial } { \partial x } } \ \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \cos ( | x | ) d t } \\ & { = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \in \mathbf { x } } { \frac { 1 } { x _ { n } - x } } \cdot { \frac { 1 } { x _ { n } - x } } \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \cos ( | x _ { n } | ) d t - \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \cos ( | x | ) d t \right) } \\ & { = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \in \mathbf { x } } \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \cos ( | x _ { n } | ) - \cos ( | x | ) \ d t \right) } \\ & { = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \in \mathbf { x } } \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \left( \sin ( | x | ) \left| x _ { n } - x \right| \ \right) d t \right) \qquad { \mathrm { w h e r e } } \quad \xi _ { n } \in [ x _ { n } , x ] { \mathrm { ~ i p ~ } } \mathbb { M } \mathbb { Y } , \xi _ { n } \to x \qquad } \\ & { = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \in \mathbf { x } } \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t \sin ( | \xi _ { n } | ) d t \right) } \\ & { = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \in \mathbf { x } } \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t \sin ( | \xi _ { n } | ) d t \right) } \\ & { = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \in \mathbf { x } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t \in \mathbf { x } } \mathbb { x i n } ( | \xi _ { n } | ) d t } \\ & { = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t \sin ( | x | ) d t } \end{array} }
$$

• Taking absolute values we obtain an upper bound

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { | \frac { \partial } { \partial x } \frac { \sin ( x ) } { x } | = | \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t \sin ( t x ) d t | } } \\ & { } & { \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } | t \sin ( t x ) | d t } \\ & { } & { \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } 1 d t = 1 , } \end{array}
$$

since $t \in [ 0 , 1 ] \implies | t | < 1$ , and $| \mathrm { s i n } ( x t ) | \le 1$ for any x and t.

• Note that this bound also justifies the DCT, since the functions $f _ { n } ( t ) = t \sin ( t \xi _ { n } )$ are uniformly dominated by $g ( t ) = 1$ on $L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ .

Note: integrating by parts here yields the actual formula:

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t \sin ( t x ) d t = _ { I B P } \left( \frac { - t \cos ( t x ) } { x } \right) \Big | _ { t = 0 } ^ { t = 1 } - \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \frac { \cos ( t x ) } { x } d t } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad = \frac { - \cos ( x ) } { x } - \frac { \sin ( x ) } { x ^ { 2 } } } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad = \frac { x \cos ( x ) - \sin ( x ) } { x ^ { 2 } } . } \end{array}
$$

• For the inductive step, we assume that we can pass n − 1 limits through the integral and

show we can pass the nth through as well.

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { { \frac { \partial ^ { n } } { \partial x ^ { n } } } { \frac { \sin ( x ) } { x } } = { \frac { \partial ^ { n } } { \partial x ^ { n } } } \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \cos ( t x ) d t } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad = { \frac { \partial } { \partial x } } \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \frac { \partial ^ { n - 1 } } { \partial x ^ { n - 1 } } } \cos ( t x ) d t } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \qquad = { \frac { \partial } { \partial x } } \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } t ^ { n - 1 } f _ { n - 1 } ( x , t ) d t } \end{array} }
$$

– Note that $f _ { n } ( x , t ) = \pm \sin ( t x )$ when n is odd and $f _ { n } ( x , t ) = \pm \cos ( t x )$ when n is even, and a constant factor of t is multiplied when each derivative is taken.

• We continue as in the base case:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \frac { \partial } { \partial x } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \ell ^ { n - 1 } f _ { n - 1 } ( x , \ell ) d \ell = } & { \underset { x _ { k } \to x } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \ell ^ { n - 1 } \left( \frac { f _ { n - 1 } ( x _ { n } , \ell ) - f _ { n - 1 } ( x , \ell ) } { x _ { n } - x } \right) d \ell } \\ & { = \mathrm { { l i v } } _ { \mathrm { T } } \underset { x _ { k } \to x } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \ell ^ { n - 1 } \frac { \partial f _ { n - 1 } } { \partial x } ( \xi _ { k } , \ell ) d \ell \mathrm { ~ w h e r e ~ } \xi _ { k } \in [ x _ { k } , x ] , \xi _ { k } \to x } \\ & { = \mathrm { { l i m } } _ { \mathrm { ~ } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \frac { { \mathrm { l i m } } } { x { { \kappa } } \cdot { \mathrm { s } } ^ { _ { 1 } } } \ell ^ { n - 1 } \frac { \partial f _ { n - 1 } } { \partial x } ( \xi _ { k } , \ell ) d \ell } \\ & { : = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \underset { x _ { k } \to x } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \ell ^ { n } f _ { n } ( \xi _ { k } , \ell ) d \ell } \\ & { = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \ell ^ { n } f _ { n } ( x , \ell ) d \ell . } \end{array}
$$

– We’ve used the fact that $f _ { 0 } ( x ) = \cos ( t x )$ is smooth as a function of $x ,$ and in particular continuous The DCT is justified because the functions $h _ { n , k } ( x , t ) = t ^ { n } f _ { n } ( \xi _ { k } , t )$ are again uniformly (in k) bounded by 1 since $t \leq 1 \implies t ^ { n } \leq 1$ and each $f _ { n }$ is a sin or cosine.

• Now take absolute values

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { \left| { \frac { \partial ^ { n } } { \partial \varphi ^ { n } } } \ { \frac { \sin ( x ) } { x } } \right| = \left| \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } - t ^ { n } f _ { n } ( x , t ) \ d t \right| } \\ & { \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \left| t ^ { n } f _ { n } ( x , t ) \right| \ d t } \\ & { \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \left| t ^ { n } \right| \left| f _ { n } ( x , t ) \right| \ d t } \\ & { \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \left| t ^ { n } \right| \cdot 1 \ d t } \\ & { \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \sqrt { 1 ^ { n } \cdot d t } } \quad { \mathrm { ~ i n ~ } } } \\ & { = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \sqrt { \textrm { s } } } \ d t \quad { \mathrm { ~ s i n c e ~ } } \ l { \mathrm { ~ i n ~ p o s i i i t y } } } \\ & { = { \frac { 1 } { n + 1 } } } \\ & { < { \frac { 1 } { n } } . } \end{array} }
$$

– We’ve again used the fact that $f _ { n } ( x , t )$ is of the form $\pm \cos ( t x ) \ \mathrm { o r } \pm \sin ( t x )$ , both of which are bounded by 1.

## 6.2 Spring 2020 # 5

Compute the following limit and justify your calculations:

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { n } \left( 1 + { \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } } \right) ^ { - ( n + 1 ) } d x .
$$

Not finished, flesh out.

Walk through.

Solution:

Concepts Used:

$\mathrm { D C T }$

• Passing limits through products and quotients

Note that

$$
\begin{array} { c c l } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \left( 1 + \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } \right) ^ { - ( n + 1 ) } = \frac { 1 } { \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \left( 1 + \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } \right) ^ { 1 } \left( 1 + \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } \right) ^ { n } } } \\ { \displaystyle = \frac { 1 } { 1 \cdot e ^ { x ^ { 2 } } } } \\ { \displaystyle = e ^ { - x ^ { 2 } } . } \end{array}
$$

If passing the limit through the integral is justified, we will have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { n } \left( 1 + \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } \right) ^ { - ( n + 1 ) } d x = } & { \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \chi _ { [ 0 , n ] } \left( 1 + \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } \right) ^ { - ( n + 1 ) } d x } \\ & { \quad \quad = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { n + \infty } } \chi _ { [ 0 , n ] } \left( 1 + \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } \right) ^ { - ( n + 1 ) } d x \quad \mathrm { b y t h e D C T } } \\ & { \quad = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { n + \infty } } \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \left( 1 + \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } \right) ^ { - ( n + 1 ) } d x } \\ & { \quad = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } e ^ { - x ^ { 2 } } } \\ & { \quad = \frac { \sqrt { \pi } } { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

Computing the last integral:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } e ^ { - x ^ { 2 } } d x ) ^ { 2 } = ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } e ^ { - x ^ { 2 } } d x ) ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } e ^ { - y ^ { 2 } } d x ) } } \\ & { = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } e ^ { - ( x + y ) ^ { 2 } } d x } \\ & { = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 2 \pi } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } e ^ { - r ^ { 2 } } r d r d \theta \quad \quad u = r ^ { 2 } } \\ & { = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 2 \pi } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } e ^ { - u } d u d \theta } \\ & { = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 2 \pi } 1 } \\ & { = \pi . } \end{array}
$$

and now use the fact that the function is even so $\int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } f = { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } f .$ Justifying the DCT:

• Apply Bernoulli’s inequality:

$$
1 + \frac { { { x ^ { 2 } } ^ { n + 1 } } } { n } \geq 1 + \frac { { { x ^ { 2 } } } } { n } \left( 1 + { { x ^ { 2 } } } \right) \geq 1 + { { x ^ { 2 } } } ,
$$

where the last inequality follows from the fact that $1 + { \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { n } } \geq 1$

## 6.3 Spring 2019 # 3

Let $\{ f _ { k } \}$ be any sequence of functions in $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ satisfying $\| f _ { k } \| _ { 2 } \leq M$ for all $k \in \mathbb N$

Prove that if $f _ { k }  f$ almost everywhere, then $f \in L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ with $\| f \| _ { 2 } \leq M$ and

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f _ { k } ( x ) d x = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) d x
$$

Hint: Try using Fatou’s Lemma to show that $\Vert f \Vert _ { 2 } \leq$ M and then try applying Egorov’s Theorem.

Solution:

## Concepts Used:

• Definition of $L ^ { + }$ : space of measurable function $X  [ 0 , \infty ]$

• Fatou: For any sequence of $L ^ { + }$ functions, Z lim inf $f _ { n } \leq$ lim inf $\int f _ { n }$

• Egorov’s Theorem: If $E \subseteq \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ is measurable, $m ( E ) > 0 , f _ { k } : E \overset { \left. } { \right. }$ R a sequence of measurable functions where $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } f _ { n } ( x )$ exists and is finite $\mathrm { a . e . , }$ , then $f _ { n }  f$ almost uniformly: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists a closed subset $F _ { \varepsilon } \subseteq E$ with $m ( E \setminus F ) < \varepsilon$ and $f _ { n }  f$ uniformly on $F .$ .

## $L ^ { 2 }$ bound:

• Since $f _ { k }  f$ almost everywhere, lim inf $f _ { n } ( x ) = f ( x )$ a.e. n

$\| f _ { n } \| _ { 2 } < \infty$ implies each $f _ { n }$ is measurable and thus $\left| f _ { n } \right| ^ { 2 } \in L ^ { + }$ , so we can apply Fatou:

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle | | f | | _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } = \int | f ( x ) | ^ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle = \int \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \operatorname* { i n f } | f _ { n } ( x ) | ^ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle \underset { \mathrm { F a t u m } } { \overset { \leq } { \prod } } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \int \Big | f _ { n } ( x ) \Big | ^ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle \leq \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \operatorname* { i n f } M } \\ { \displaystyle = M . } \end{array}
$$

• Thus $\| f \| _ { 2 } \leq \sqrt { M } < \infty$ implying $f \in L ^ { 2 }$

## What is the "right" proof here that uses the first part?

Equality of Integrals:

• Take the sequence $\varepsilon _ { n } = { \frac { 1 } { n } }$

• Apply Egorov’s theorem: obtain a set $F _ { \varepsilon }$ such that $f _ { n }  f$ uniformly on $F _ { \varepsilon }$ and m $( I \setminus F _ { \varepsilon } ) < \varepsilon$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \underset { n  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } }  \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f _ { n } - f  \leq \underset { n  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \vert f _ { n } - f \vert } & { } \\ { = \underset { n  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } ( \int _ { F _ { \varepsilon } } \vert f _ { n } - f \vert + \int _ { I \setminus F _ { \varepsilon } } \vert f _ { n } - f \vert ) } & { } \\ { = \underset { f _ { \varepsilon } , n  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \underset { | f _ { n } - f | + } { \operatorname* { l i m } } + \underset { n  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \int _ { I \setminus F _ { \varepsilon } } \vert f _ { n } - f \vert } & { \mathrm { b y ~ u n i f o r m ~ c o n v e r g e n c e } } \\ { = 0 + \underset { n  \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \int _ { I \setminus F _ { \varepsilon } } \vert f _ { n } - f \vert , } & { } \end{array}
$$

so it suffices to show $\int _ { I \backslash F _ { \varepsilon } } | f _ { n } - f | \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } 0 ,$

• We can obtain a bound using Holder’s inequality with $p = q = 2 \colon$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \int _ { I \backslash F _ { \varepsilon } } | f _ { n } - f | \le ( \int _ { I \backslash F _ { \varepsilon } } | f _ { n } - f | ^ { 2 } ) ( \int _ { I \backslash F _ { \varepsilon } } | 1 | ^ { 2 } ) } } \\ & { = ( \int _ { I \backslash F _ { \varepsilon } } | f _ { n } - f | ^ { 2 } ) \mu ( F _ { \varepsilon } ) } \\ & { \le \| f _ { n } - f \| _ { 2 } \mu ( F _ { \varepsilon } ) } \\ & { \le ( \| f _ { n } \| _ { 2 } + \| f \| _ { 2 } ) \mu ( F _ { \varepsilon } ) } \\ & { \le 2 M \cdot \mu ( F _ { \varepsilon } ) } \end{array}
$$

where M is now a constant not depending on $\varepsilon \textnormal { o r } n$

• Now take a nested sequence of sets $F _ { \varepsilon }$ with $\mu ( F _ { \varepsilon } ) \to 0$ and applying continuity of measure yields the desired statement.

## 6.4 Fall 2018 # 6

Compute the following limit and justify your calculations:

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { 1 } ^ { n } { \frac { d x } { \left( 1 + { \frac { x } { n } } \right) ^ { n } { \sqrt [ n ] { x } } } }
$$

Add concepts.

Solution:

Concepts Used:

?

• Note that $x ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to }$ 1 for any $0 < x < \infty$

• Thus the integrand converges to $\frac { 1 } { e ^ { x } }$ , which is integrable on $( 0 , \infty )$ and integrates to 1.

• Break the integrand up:

$$
\int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { 1 } { \left( 1 + { \frac { x } { n } } \right) ^ { n } x ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } } } d x = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } { \frac { 1 } { \left( 1 + { \frac { x } { n } } \right) ^ { n } x ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } } } d x = \int _ { 1 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { 1 } { \left( 1 + { \frac { x } { n } } \right) ^ { n } x ^ { \frac { 1 } { n } } } } d x .
$$

## 6.5 Fall 2018 # 3

Suppose f(x) and $x f ( x )$ are integrable on R. Define F by

$$
F ( t ) : = \int _ { - \infty } ^ { \infty } f ( x ) \cos ( x t ) d x
$$

Show that

$$
F ^ { \prime } ( t ) = - \int _ { - \infty } ^ { \infty } x f ( x ) \sin ( x t ) d x .
$$

Walk through.

Solution:

Concepts Used:

• Mean Value Theorem

• DCT

$$
\begin{array} { c } { { \displaystyle { \frac { \partial } { \partial t } } F ( t ) = \frac { \partial } { \partial t } \int _ { \mathbb R } f ( x ) \cos ( x t ) \ d x } } \\ { { \displaystyle { \phantom { \frac { \partial } { \partial t } } } _ { = } ^ { D C T } \int _ { \mathbb R } f ( x ) \frac { \partial } { \partial t } \cos ( x t ) \ d x } } \\ { { \displaystyle { \phantom { \frac { \partial } { \partial t } } } = \int _ { \mathbb R } x f ( x ) \cos ( x t ) \ d x , } } \end{array}
$$

so it only remains to justify the DCT.

• Fix t, then let $t _ { n } \to t$ be arbitrary.

• Define

$$
h _ { n } ( x , t ) = f ( x ) \left( { \frac { \cos ( t x ) - \cos ( t _ { n } x ) } { t _ { n } - t } } \right) \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } { \frac { \partial } { \partial t } } \left( f ( x ) \cos ( x t ) \right)
$$

since cos(tx) is differentiable in t and this is the limit definition of differentiability.

• Note that

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { { \frac { \partial } { \partial t } } \cos ( t x ) : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t _ { n } \to t } { \frac { \cos ( t x ) - \cos ( t _ { n } x ) } { t _ { n } - t } } } \\ { { \overset { M V T } { = } } } & { { \frac { \partial } { \partial t } } \cos ( t x ) \ \Big \vert _ { t = \xi _ { n } } } \\ & { = x \sin ( \xi _ { n } x ) } \end{array} }
$$

$$
\mathrm { f o r ~ s o m e } ~ \xi _ { n } \in [ t , t _ { n } ] ~ \mathrm { o r } ~ [ t _ { n } , t ]
$$

where $\xi _ { n } \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to }$ t since wlog $t _ { n } \leq \xi _ { n } \leq t$ and $t _ { n } \nearrow t$

• We then have

$$
\begin{array} { r } { | h _ { n } ( x ) | = | f ( x ) x \sin ( \xi _ { n } x ) | \leq | x f ( x ) | \quad \mathrm { s i n c e } | \mathrm { s i n } ( \xi _ { n } x ) | \leq 1 } \end{array}
$$

for every x and every n.

• Since $x f ( x ) \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ by assumption, the DCT applies.

## 6.6 Spring 2018 # 5

Suppose that

$f _ { n } , f \in L ^ { 1 }$

$f _ { n }  f$ almost everywhere, and

$\int | f _ { n } | \to \int | f | .$

Show that $\int f _ { n } \to \int f .$

Solution:

Concepts Used:

$$
\bullet \ \int | f _ { n } - f |  \iff \int f _ { n } = \int f .
$$

• Fatou:

$$
\mathrm { ~ l i m } \operatorname* { i n f } f _ { n } \leq \operatorname* { l i m } \operatorname* { i n f } f _ { n }
$$

$$
\int \operatorname* { l i m } \operatorname* { s u p } f _ { n } \geq \operatorname* { l i m } \operatorname* { s u p } \int f _ { n } .
$$

• Since $\int | f _ { n } | \stackrel { n  \infty } {  } \int | f |$ , define

$$
h _ { n } = | f _ { n } - f |
$$

$$
{ \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } } 0 \ a . e .
$$

$$
g _ { n } = | f _ { n } | + | f |
$$

$$
\stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } 2 | f | \ a . e .
$$

– Note that $g _ { n } - { h _ { n } } \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } 2 | f | - 0 = 2 | f |$

• Then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \int 2 | { \boldsymbol f } | = \int \operatorname* { l i m i n f } _ { n } ( g _ { n } - h _ { n } ) } } \\ & { = \int \operatorname* { l i m i n f } _ { n } ( g _ { n } ) + \int \operatorname* { l i m i n f } _ { n } ( - h _ { n } ) } \\ & { = \int \operatorname* { l i m i n f } _ { n } ( g _ { n } ) - \int \operatorname* { l i m s u p } _ { n } ( h _ { n } ) } \\ & { = \int 2 | { \boldsymbol f } | - \int \operatorname* { l i m s u p } _ { n } ( h _ { n } ) } \\ & { \leq \int 2 | { \boldsymbol f } | - \operatorname* { l i m s u p } _ { n } \int h _ { n } ~ \mathrm { b y ~ F a t o u } , } \end{array}
$$

• Since $f \in L ^ { 1 } , \int 2 | f | = 2 \| f \| _ { 1 } < \infty$ and it makes sense to subtract it from both sides, thus

$$
\begin{array} { l } { 0 \leq - \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { } \int h _ { n } } \\ { : = - \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { } \int | f _ { n } - f | . } \end{array}
$$

which forces $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { } \int | f _ { n } - f | = 0 .$ since

– The integral of a nonnegative function is nonnegative, so $\int | f _ { n } - f | \geq 0 .$

$$
- \mathrm { ~ S o ~ } \biggl ( - \int | f _ { n } - f | \biggr ) \leq 0 .
$$

– But the above inequality shows $\left( - \int | f _ { n } - f | \right) \geq 0$ as well.

• Since lim $\operatorname* { i n f } _ { n } \int h _ { n } \leq \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { } \int h _ { n } = 0 ;$ , lim $\int h _ { n }$ exists and is equal to zero. n

• But then

$$
\left| \int f _ { n } - \int f \right| = \left| \int f _ { n } - f \right| \leq \int | f _ { n } - f | ,
$$

and taking $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty }$ on both sides yields

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \left| \int f _ { n } - \int f \right| \leq \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int | f _ { n } - f | = 0 ,
$$

so lim $\int f _ { n } = \int f .$ n→∞

## 6.7 Spring 2018 # 2

Let

$$
f _ { n } ( x ) : = { \frac { x } { 1 + x ^ { n } } } , \quad x \geq 0 .
$$

a. Show that this sequence converges pointwise and find its limit. Is the convergence uniform on $\lbrack 0 , \infty ) ?$

b. Compute

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n  \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } f _ { n } ( x ) d x
$$

Add concepts.

Solution:

Concepts Used:

## 6.7.1 a

Claim: $f _ { n }$ does not converge uniformly to its limit.

• Note each $f _ { n } ( x )$ is clearly continuous on $( 0 , \infty )$ , since it is a quotient of continuous functions where the denominator is never zero.

• Note

$$
x < 1 \implies x ^ { n } { \overset { n \to \infty } { \to } } 0 \quad { \mathrm { a n d } } \quad x > 1 \implies x ^ { n } { \overset { n \to \infty } { \to } } \infty .
$$

• Thus

$$
f _ { n } ( x ) = { \frac { x } { 1 + x ^ { n } } } \ { \overset { n \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } } \ f ( x ) : = { \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { x , } & { 0 \leq x < 1 } \\ { 1 } & { x = 1 } \\ { 2 } & { x > 1 } \end{array} \right. } .
$$

• If $f _ { n }  f$ uniformly on $[ 0 , \infty )$ , it would converge uniformly on every subset and thus uniformly on $( 0 , \infty )$

– Then f would be a uniform limit of continuous functions on $( 0 , \infty )$ and thus continuous on $( 0 , \infty )$ •

– By uniqueness of limits, $f _ { n }$ would converge to the pointwise limit f above, which is not continuous at $x = 1$ , a contradiction.

## 6.7.2 b

• If the DCT applies, interchange the limit and integral:

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } f _ { n } ( x ) d x = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } f _ { n } ( x ) d x } & { { \mathrm { D C T } } } \\ & { = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } f ( x ) d x } \\ & { = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } x d x + \int _ { 1 } ^ { \infty } 0 d x } \\ & { = \left. { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } x ^ { 2 } \right| _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } } \\ & { = { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } . } \end{array} }
$$

• To justify the DCT, write

$$
\int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } f _ { n } ( x ) = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f _ { n } ( x ) + \int _ { 1 } ^ { \infty } f _ { n } ( x ) .
$$

$f _ { n }$ restricted to (0, 1) is uniformly bounded by $g _ { 0 } ( x ) = 1$ in the first integral, since

$$
x \in [ 0 , 1 ] \implies \frac { x } { 1 + x ^ { n } } < \frac { 1 } { 1 + x ^ { n } } < 1 : = g ( x )
$$

so

$$
\int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f _ { n } ( x ) d x \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } 1 d x = 1 < \infty .
$$

Also note that $g _ { 0 } \cdot \chi _ { ( 0 , 1 ) } \in L ^ { 1 } ( ( 0 , \infty ) )$

• The $f _ { n }$ restricted to $( 1 , \infty )$ are uniformly bounded by $g _ { 1 } ( x ) = \frac { 1 } { x ^ { 2 } } \mathrm { ~ o n ~ } [ 1 , \infty )$ , since

$$
x \in ( 1 , \infty ) \implies \frac { x } { 1 + x ^ { n } } \leq \frac { x } { x ^ { n } } = \frac { 1 } { x ^ { n - 1 } } \leq \frac { 1 } { x ^ { 2 } } \in L ^ { 1 } ( [ 1 , \infty ) \mathrm { ~ w h e n ~ } n \geq 3 ,
$$

by the p-test for integrals.

• So set

$$
g : = g _ { 0 } \cdot \chi _ { ( 0 , 1 ) } + g _ { 1 } \cdot \chi _ { [ 1 , \infty ) } ,
$$

then by the above arguments $g \in L ^ { 1 } ( ( 0 , \infty ) )$ and $f _ { n } \leq g$ everywhere, so the DCT applies.

## 6.8 Fall 2016 # 3

Let $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ . Show that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \to 0 } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } | f ( y - x ) - f ( y ) | d y = 0
$$

Missing some stuff.

Solution:

Concepts Used:

$C _ { c } ^ { \infty } \hookrightarrow L ^ { p }$ is dense.

$\operatorname { I f } f \ldots \mathit { 2 }$

• Fixing notation, set $\tau _ { x } f ( y ) : = f ( y - x )$ ; we then want to show

$$
\| \tau _ { x } f - f \| _ { L ^ { 1 } } \stackrel { x \to 0 } { \to } 0 .
$$

• Claim: by an $\varepsilon / 3$ argument, it suffices to show this for compactly supported functions: – Since $f \in L ^ { 1 }$ , choose $g _ { n } \subset C _ { c } ^ { \infty } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { 1 } )$ smooth and compactly supported so that

$$
\| f - g \| _ { L ^ { 1 } } < \varepsilon .
$$

– Claim: $\| { \tau } _ { x } f - { \tau } _ { x } g \| < \varepsilon .$

♦ Proof 1: translation invariance of the integral.

♦ Proof 2: Apply a change of variables:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \| \tau _ { x } f - \tau _ { x } g \| _ { 1 } : = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R } | \tau _ { x } f ( y ) - \tau _ { x } g ( y ) | d y } \\ & { \qquad = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R } | f ( y - x ) - g ( y - x ) | d y } \\ & { \qquad = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R } | f ( u ) - g ( u ) | d u \qquad ( u = y - x , d u = d y ) } \\ & { \qquad = \| f - g \| _ { 1 } } \\ & { \qquad < \varepsilon . } \end{array}
$$

– Then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \left\| { \tau _ { x } f - f } \right\| _ { 1 } = \left\| { \tau _ { x } f - \tau _ { x } g + \tau _ { x } g - g + g - f } \right\| _ { 1 } } \\ & { \qquad \leq \left\| { \tau _ { x } f - \tau _ { x } g } \right\| _ { 1 } + \left\| { \tau _ { x } g - g } \right\| _ { 1 } + \left\| { g - f } \right\| _ { 1 } } \\ & { \qquad \leq 2 \varepsilon + \left\| { \tau _ { x } g - g } \right\| _ { 1 } . } \end{array}
$$

• To show this for compactly supported functions:

– Let $g \in C _ { c } ^ { \infty } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { 1 } )$ , let $E = \operatorname { s u p p } ( g )$ , and write

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \| \tau _ { x } g - g \| _ { 1 } = \int _ { \mathbb R } | g ( y - x ) - g ( y ) | d y } \\ { \displaystyle = \int _ { E } | g ( y - x ) - g ( y ) | d y + \int _ { E ^ { c } } | g ( y - x ) - g ( y ) | d y } \\ { \displaystyle = \int _ { E } | g ( y - x ) - g ( y ) | d y . } \end{array}
$$

– But $g$ is smooth and compactly supported on $E ,$ and thus uniformly continuous on $E ,$ so

$$
\begin{array} { l l } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \to 0 } \int _ { E } \left| g ( y - x ) - g ( y ) \right| d y = \int _ { E } \operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \to 0 } \left| g ( y - x ) - g ( y ) \right| d y } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad = \int _ { E } 0 d y } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad = 0 . } \end{array}
$$

Let $f : [ 1 , \infty ) \to { \mathbb R }$ such that $f ( 1 ) = 1$ and

## 6.11 Spring 2021 # 2

Problem 6.11.1 (?)

Calculate the following limit, justifying each step of your calculation:

$$
L : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { n } { \frac { \cos \left( { \frac { x } { n } } \right) } { x ^ { 2 } + \cos \left( { \frac { x } { n } } \right) } } d x .
$$

Solution: • If interchanging a limit and integral is justified, we have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \lambda = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left. \mathbf { n } _ { f } \right. _ { \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } } ^ { \otimes } \left. \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \right. _ { \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } } ^ { \otimes } } & { \mathrm { ~ i ~ n ~ } } \\ & { = \frac { \mathbf { n } _ { f } } { 2 } \left. \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \right. _ { \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } } ^ { \otimes } \frac { \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } } { \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + 2 \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + 2 \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + 2 \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + 2 \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } } } \\ & { = \frac { \mathbf { n } _ { f } } { 2 } \int _ { \left( \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } \frac { \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } } { \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + 2 \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + 2 \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + 2 \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + 2 \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } } } \\ &  = \sum _ { \left( \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } \frac  \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } + \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } ^ { \otimes } \left. \mathbf { n } _ { f } ^ { \star \star } \right. _ { \mathbf { n } _ { 0 } } ^ { \otimes } \end{array}
$$

where we’ve used that $\cos ( \theta )$ is continuous on $\mathbb { R }$ to pass a limit inside, noting that x is fixed in the integrand.

• Justifying the interchange: DCT. Write $f _ { n } ( x ) : = \cos ( x / n ) / ( x ^ { 2 } + \cos ( x / n ) )$

On $( \alpha , \infty )$ for any $\alpha > 1$

– We have

$$
| f _ { n } ( x ) | \leq \left| { \frac { 1 } { x ^ { 2 } + \cos ( x / n ) } } \right| \leq { \frac { 1 } { x ^ { 2 } - 1 } } ,
$$

where we’ve used that $- 1 \leq \cos ( x / n ) \leq 1$ for every $x ,$ and so the denominator is minimized when $\cos ( x / n ) = - 1$ , and this maximizes the quantity.

– Setting $g ( x ) : = 1 / ( x ^ { 2 } - 1 )$ , we have $g \in L ^ { 1 } ( \alpha , \infty )$ by the limit comparison test with $h ( x ) : = x ^ { 2 }  \colon$

$$
{ \frac { g ( x ) } { h ( x ) } } : = { \frac { x ^ { 2 } - 1 } { x ^ { 2 } } } = 1 - { \frac { 1 } { x ^ { 2 } } } \stackrel { x \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 1 < \infty ,
$$

and so g, h either both converge or both diverge. But $\int _ { \alpha } ^ { \infty } { \frac { 1 } { x ^ { 2 } } } d x <$ ∞ by the p-test for integrals since $\alpha > 1$

• On (0, α):

– Just use that $f _ { n } ( x )$ is bounded by a constant:

$$
| f _ { n } ( x ) | = \left| { \frac { \cos ( x / n ) } { x ^ { 2 } + \cos ( x / n ) } } \right| \leq \left| { \frac { \cos ( x / n ) } { \cos ( x / n ) } } \right| = 1 ,
$$

where we’ve used that $x ^ { 2 }$ is positive, and removing it from the denominator only makes the quantity larger.

– Then check that $\int _ { 0 } ^ { \alpha } 1 d x = \alpha < \infty , \mathrm { s o } 1 \in L ^ { 1 } ( 0 , \alpha ) .$

## 6.12 Spring 2021 # 5

Let $f _ { n } \in L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ for $n \in \mathbb { N }$ , and assume that

$\| f _ { n } \| _ { 2 } \leq n ^ { \frac { - 5 1 } { 1 0 0 } }$ for all $n \in \mathbb { N } .$

${ \widehat { f } } _ { n }$ is supported in the interval $[ 2 ^ { n } , 2 ^ { n + 1 } ]$ , so

$$
{ \widehat { f } } _ { n } ( \xi ) : = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f _ { n } ( x ) e ^ { 2 \pi i \xi \cdot x } d x = 0
$$

for $\xi \not \in [ 2 ^ { n } , 2 ^ { n + 1 } ]$

Prove that $\sum _ { n \in \mathbb { N } } f _ { n }$ converges in the Hilbert space $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

Hint: Plancherel’s identity may be helpful.

## Integrals: Approximation

## 7.1 Spring 2018 # 3

Let f be a non-negative measurable function on [0, 1].

Show that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { p \to \infty } { \left( \int _ { [ 0 , 1 ] } f ( x ) ^ { p } d x \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { p } } } = \| f \| _ { \infty } .
$$

## Concepts Used:

$\| f \| _ { \infty } : = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { t } \left\{ t { \Big | } m \left( \left\{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \ \Big | \ f ( x ) > t \right\} \right) = 0 \right\}$ , i.e. this is the lowest upper bound that holds almost everywhere.

## Solution:

$\| f \| _ { p } \leq \| f \| _ { \infty } \mathrm { : }$   
– Note $| f ( x ) | \leq \| f \| _ { \infty }$ almost everywhere and taking pth powers preserves this in  
equality.   
– Thus   
$| f ( x ) | \leq \| f \| _ { \infty }$ a.e. by definition   
=⇒ |f (x)|p ≤ kf kp for p ≥ 0   
$\implies \| f \| _ { p } ^ { p } = \int _ { X } | f ( x ) | ^ { p } ~ d x$   
≤ $\int _ { X } \| f \| _ { \infty } ^ { p } d x$   
= $\| f \| _ { \infty } ^ { p } \int _ { X } 1 d x$   
$= \| f \| _ { \infty } ^ { p } \cdot m ( X )$ since the norm doesn’t depend on x   
= kf kp∞ since m(X) = 1.

♦ Thus $\| f \| _ { p } \leq \| f \| _ { \infty }$ for all p and taking $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { p \to \infty }$ preserves this inequality.

$\| f \| _ { p } \geq \| f \| _ { \infty }$

– Fix ε > 0.

– Define

$$
S _ { \varepsilon } : = \left\{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \Big | | f ( x ) | \geq \| f \| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon \right\} .
$$

♦ Note that $m ( S _ { \varepsilon } ) > 0 ;$ otherwise if $m ( S _ { \varepsilon } ) = 0 ,$ , then $t : = \left\| f \right\| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon < \left\| f \right\| _ { \varepsilon }$ . But this produces a smaller upper bound almost everywhere than $\| f \| _ { \varepsilon } ,$ , contradicting the definition of $\| f \| _ { \ell }$ as an infimum over such bounds.

– Then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \| f \| _ { p } ^ { p } = \int _ { X } | f ( x ) | ^ { p } d x } } \\ & { \geq \int _ { S _ { \varepsilon } } | f ( x ) | ^ { p } d x \quad \mathrm { s i n c e ~ } S _ { \varepsilon } \subseteq X } \\ & { \geq \int _ { S _ { \varepsilon } } \| f \| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon | ^ { p } d x \quad \mathrm { s i n c e ~ o n ~ } S _ { \varepsilon } , | f | \geq \| f \| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon } \\ & { = | \| f \| _ { \infty } - \varepsilon | ^ { p } \cdot m ( S _ { \varepsilon } ) \quad \mathrm { s i n c e ~ t h e ~ i n t e g r a n d ~ i s ~ i n d e p e n d e n t ~ o f ~ } x } \\ & { \geq 0 \quad \mathrm { s i n c e ~ } m ( S _ { \varepsilon } ) > 0 } \end{array}
$$

– Taking pth roots for $p \geq 1$ preserves the inequality, so

$$
\implies \lVert f \rVert _ { p } \ge | \lVert f \rVert _ { \infty } - \varepsilon | \cdot m ( S _ { \varepsilon } ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { p } } \xrightarrow { p \to \infty } | \lVert f \rVert _ { \infty } - \varepsilon | \xrightarrow { \varepsilon \to 0 } | \lVert f \rVert _ { \infty }
$$

where we’ve used the fact that above arguments work

– Thus $\| f \| _ { p } \geq \| f \| _ { \infty } .$

## 7.2 Spring 2018 # 4

Let $f \in L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ and suppose

$$
\int _ { [ 0 , 1 ] } f ( x ) x ^ { n } d x = 0 { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ a l l ~ i n t e g e r s ~ } } n \geq 0 .
$$

Show that $f = 0$ almost everywhere.

## Concepts Used:

• Weierstrass Approximation: A continuous function on a compact set can be uniformly approximated by polynomials.

$C ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ is dense in $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

• Polynomials are dense in $L ^ { p } ( X , { \mathcal { M } } , \mu )$ for any $X \subseteq \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ compact and µ a finite measure, for all $1 \leq p < \infty$ .

– Use Weierstrass Approximation, then uniform convergence implies $L ^ { p } ( \mu )$ convergence by DCT.

## Solution:

## Proof (using Fourier transforms).

• Fix $k \in \mathbb { Z } .$

• Since $ e ^ { 2 \pi i k x }$ is continuous on the compact interval [0, 1], it is uniformly continuous.

• Thus there is a sequence of polynomials $P _ { \ell }$ such that

$$
P _ { \ell , k } \stackrel { \ell  \infty } { \longrightarrow } e ^ { 2 \pi i k x } \mathrm { ~ u n i f o r m l y ~ o n ~ } [ 0 , 1 ] .
$$

• Note applying linearity to the assumption $\int f ( x ) x ^ { n }$ , we have

$$
\int f ( x ) x ^ { n } d x = 0 \forall n \implies \int f ( x ) p ( x ) d x = 0
$$

for any polynomial $p ( x )$ , and in particular for $P _ { \ell , k } ( x )$ for every \` and every k.

• But then

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { \langle f , \ e _ { k } \rangle = \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) e ^ { - 2 \pi i k x } \ d x } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) \ { \underset { \ell \to \infty } { \mathrm { l i m } } } \ P _ { \ell } ( x ) } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { \ell \to \infty } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) P _ { \ell } ( x ) { \mathrm { b y ~ u n i f o r m ~ c o n v e r g e n c e ~ o n ~ a ~ c o m p a c t ~ i n t e r v a l } } } \\ & { = \displaystyle { \operatorname* { l i m } _ { \ell \to \infty } 0 } { \mathrm { b y ~ a s s u m p t i o n } } } \\ & { = 0 \ \forall k \in \mathbb { Z } , } \end{array} }
$$

so f is orthogonal to every $e _ { k }$

• Thus $\begin{array} { r } { f \in S ^ { \perp } : = \mathrm { s p a n } _ { \mathbb { C } } \left\{ e _ { k } \right\} _ { k \in \mathbb { Z } } ^ { \perp } \subseteq L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] ) } \end{array}$ , but since this is a basis, S is dense and thus $S ^ { \perp } = \{ 0 \}$ in $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

• Thus $f \equiv 0$ in $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ , which implies that f is zero almost everywhere.

Proof (Alternative).

• By density of polynomials, for $f \in L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ choose $p _ { \varepsilon } ( x )$ such that $\| f - p _ { \varepsilon } \| < \varepsilon$ by Weierstrass approximation.

• Then on one hand,

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } & { \left\| f ( f - p _ { \varepsilon } ) \right\| _ { 1 } = \left\| f ^ { 2 } \right\| _ { 1 } - \left\| f \cdot p _ { \varepsilon } \right\| _ { 1 } } \\ & { \qquad = \left\| f ^ { 2 } \right\| _ { 1 } - 0 \quad { \mathrm { b y ~ a s s u m p t i o n } } } \\ & { \qquad = \left\| f \right\| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } . } \end{array} }
$$

Where we’ve used that $\left\| f ^ { 2 } \right\| _ { 1 } = \int \left| f ^ { 2 } \right| = \int | f | ^ { 2 } = \| f \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } .$

• On the other hand

$$
{ \begin{array} { r l } { \| f ( f - p _ { \varepsilon } ) \| \leq \| f \| _ { 1 } \| f - p _ { \varepsilon } \| _ { \infty } } & { { \mathrm { b y ~ H o l d e r } } } \\ { \leq \varepsilon \| f \| _ { 1 } } \\ { \leq \varepsilon \| f \| _ { 2 } { \sqrt { m ( X ) } } } \\ { = \varepsilon \| f \| _ { 2 } } & { { \mathrm { s i n c e ~ } } m ( X ) = 1 . } \end{array} }
$$

– Where we’ve used that $\| f g \| _ { 1 } = \int | f g | = \int | f | | g | \leq \int \| f \| _ { \infty } | g | = \| f \| _ { \infty } \| g \| _ { 1 } .$

• Combining these,

$$
\| f \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \leq \| f \| _ { 2 } \varepsilon \implies \| f \| _ { 2 } < \varepsilon  0 , .
$$

so $\| f \| _ { 2 } = 0$ , which implies f = 0 almost everywhere.

## Concepts Used:

• Limits

• Cauchy Criterion for Integrals: $\int _ { a } ^ { \infty } f ( x )$ dx converges iff for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists an $M _ { 0 }$ such that $A , B \geq M _ { 0 }$ implies $\left| \int _ { A } ^ { B } f \right| < \varepsilon , { \mathrm { i . e . } } \left| \int _ { A } ^ { B } f \right| { \overset { A \to \infty } { \to } } 0 .$

• Integrals of $L ^ { 1 }$ functions have vanishing tails: $\int _ { N } ^ { \infty } | f | \stackrel { N  \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0 .$

• Mean Value Theorem for Integrals: $\int _ { a } ^ { b } f ( t ) d t = ( b - a ) f ( c )$ for some $c \in [ a , b ]$

Solution (of a):

Stated integral equality:

$\mathrm { L e t } \ \varepsilon > 0$

$C _ { c } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } ) \hookrightarrow L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ is dense so choose $\left\{ f _ { n } \right\} \to f$ with $\| f _ { n } - f \| _ { 1 } \to 0$

• Since $\left\{ f _ { n } \right\}$ are compactly supported, choose $N _ { 0 } \gg 1$ such that $f _ { n }$ is zero outside of $B _ { N _ { 0 } } ( { \mathbf 0 } )$

• Then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { N \geq \mathbb { N } _ { 0 } \implies \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } | t | = \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } | t | - \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } | t | } & { } \\ & { \leq \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } | t | - \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } | t | } \\ & { = \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } | t - \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } | t | } \\ & { \leq \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } | t | - \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times 1 } | t | } \\ & { - \left[ \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } \cdot e \right] | t | - \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } 1 \Big ) } \\ & { \xrightarrow [ t ] { u _ { 0 } } \Phi \Big ( \int _ { \left[ t \right] \times N } 1 \Big ) } \\ & { = 0 } \\ & { \xrightarrow [ t ] { u _ { 0 } } } \\ & { \xrightarrow [ t ] { u _ { 0 } } \Phi _ { 0 } , } \end{array}
$$

To see that this doesn’t force $f ( x )  0 { \mathrm { ~ a s ~ } } | x |  \infty { : }$

• Take $f ( x )$ to be a train of rectangles of height 1 and area $1 / 2 ^ { j }$ centered on even integers.

• Then

$$
\int _ { | x | > N } | f | = \sum _ { j = N } ^ { \infty } 1 / 2 ^ { j } \stackrel { N \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0
$$

as the tail of a convergent sum.

• However $f ( x ) = 1$ for infinitely many even integers $x > N$ , so $f ( x ) \neq 0$ as $| x | \to \infty$

## Solution (of b):

Proof (Solution 1, a slight trick).

• Since f is decreasing on $[ 1 , \infty )$ , for any $t \in [ x - n , x ]$ we have

$$
x - n \leq t \leq x \implies f ( x ) \leq f ( t ) \leq f ( x - n ) .
$$

• Integrate over [x, 2x], using monotonicity of the integral:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( x ) d t \leq \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( t ) d t \leq \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( x - n ) d t } \\ { \implies f ( x ) \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } d t \leq \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( t ) d t \leq f ( x - n ) \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } d t } \\ { \implies x f ( x ) \leq \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( t ) d t \leq x f ( x - n ) . } \end{array}
$$

By the Cauchy Criterion for integrals, lim $\int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( t ) \ d t = 0 .$ x→∞

• So the LHS term $x f ( x ) \stackrel { x \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0$

• Since $x > 1 , | f ( x ) | \leq | x f ( x ) |$

• Thus $f ( x ) \stackrel { x \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0$ as well.

## Proof (Solution 2: Variation on the trick).

• Use mean value theorem for integrals:

$$
\int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( t ) d t = x f ( c _ { x } ) \quad { \mathrm { f o r ~ s o m e ~ } } c _ { x } \in [ x , 2 x ] { \mathrm { ~ d e p e n d i n g ~ o n ~ } } x .
$$

• Since f is decreasing,

$$
\begin{array} { r l l } { x \leq c _ { x } \leq 2 x \implies f ( 2 x ) \leq f ( c _ { x } ) \leq f ( x ) } \\ { \implies \displaystyle 2 x f ( 2 x ) \leq 2 x f ( c _ { x } ) \leq 2 x f ( x ) } \\ { \implies \displaystyle 2 x f ( 2 x ) \leq 2 x \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( t ) d t \leq 2 x f ( x ) } \end{array}
$$

• By Cauchy Criterion, $\int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f  0 .$

• So $2 x f ( 2 x )  0$ , which by a change of variables gives $u f ( u ) \to 0$

• Since $u \geq 1 , f ( u ) \leq u f ( u )$ so $f ( u )  0$ as well.

then

Showing $x f ( x ) \stackrel { x \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0 .$

• If $x f ( x )  L \neq 0$ for $0 < L < \infty \colon$   
Fix $\varepsilon > 0 ,$ choose an infinite sequence $\{ x _ { i } \}$ such that $L - \varepsilon \leq x _ { i } f ( x _ { i } ) \leq L$ for

Proof (Solution 4: Akos’ suggestion).

For $x \geq 1$

$$
| x f ( x ) | = \left| \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } f ( x ) d t \right| \leq \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } | f ( x ) | d t \leq \int _ { x } ^ { 2 x } | f ( t ) | d t \leq \int _ { x } ^ { \infty } | f ( t ) | d t { \overset { x \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } } 0
$$

where we’ve used

• Since f is decreasing and $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { x \to \infty } f ( x ) = 0$ from part (a), f is non-negative.

• Since f is positive and decreasing, for every $t \in [ a , b ]$ we have $| f ( a ) | \leq | f ( t ) |$ .

• By part (a), the last integral goes to zero.

## Proof (Solution 5: Peter’s).

• Toward a contradiction, produce a sequence $x _ { i }  \infty$ with $x _ { i } f ( x _ { i } )  \infty$ and $x _ { i } f ( x _ { i } ) > \varepsilon > 0$ , then

$$
\int f ( x ) d x \geq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } \int _ { x _ { i } } ^ { x _ { i + 1 } } f ( x ) d x
$$

$$
\geq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } \int _ { x _ { i } } ^ { x _ { i + 1 } } f ( x _ { i + 1 } ) d x
$$

$$
= \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } f ( x _ { i + 1 } ) \int _ { x _ { i } } ^ { x _ { i + 1 } } d x
$$

$$
\geq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } ( x _ { i + 1 } - x _ { i } ) f ( x _ { i + 1 } )
$$

$$
\geq \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } ( x _ { i + 1 } - x _ { i } ) \frac { \varepsilon } { x _ { i + 1 } }
$$

$$
= \varepsilon \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { \infty } \left( 1 - { \frac { x _ { i - 1 } } { x _ { i } } } \right) \to \infty
$$

which can be ensured by passing to a subsequence where $\sum { \frac { x _ { i - 1 } } { x _ { i } } } < \infty .$

## Solution (of c):

• No: take $f ( x ) = { \frac { 1 } { x \ln x } }$

• Then by a u-substitution,

$$
\int _ { 0 } ^ { x } f = \ln \left( \ln ( x ) \right) { \overset { x \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } } \infty
$$

is unbounded, so $f \not \in L ^ { 1 } ( [ 1 , \infty ) )$ .

• But

$$
x f ( x ) = { \frac { 1 } { \ln ( x ) } } \stackrel { x \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } 0 .
$$

## 8.2 Fall 2019 # 5.

a. Show that if f is continuous with compact support on R, then

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { y \to 0 } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } | f ( x - y ) - f ( x ) | d x = 0
$$

b. Let $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ and for each $h > 0$ let

$$
A _ { h } f ( x ) : = \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \int _ { | y | \leq h } f ( x - y ) d y
$$

• Prove that $\| \mathcal { A } _ { h } f \| _ { 1 } \leq \| f \| _ { 1 }$ for all $h > 0$

• Prove that $\mathcal { A } _ { h } f  f$ in $L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ as $h \to 0 ^ { + }$

## Walk through.

## Concepts Used:

• Continuity in $L ^ { 1 }$ (recall that DCT won’t work! Notes 19.4, prove it for a dense subset first).

• Lebesgue differentiation in 1-dimensional case. See HW 5.6.

## Solution:

Proof $( o f a ) . \mathrm { ~ \ } \bullet \mathrm { ~ \ F i x ~ } \varepsilon > 0$ . If we can find a set A such that the following calculation holds for h small enough, we’re done:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R } \left| f ( x - h ) - f ( x ) \right| d x = \displaystyle \int _ { A } \left| f ( x - h ) - f ( x ) \right| d x } & { } \\ { \leq \displaystyle \int _ { A } \varepsilon } \\ { } & { = \varepsilon \mu ( A ) \longrightarrow 0 , } \end{array}
$$

provided $h  0$ as $\varepsilon \to 0$ , which we can arrange if $| h | < \varepsilon .$

• Choose $A \supseteq \operatorname { s u p p } f$ compact such that supp $f \pm 1 \subseteq A$

Why this can be done: suppf is compact, so closed and bounded, and contained in some compact interval $[ - M , M ]$ . So e.g. $A : = [ - M - 1 , M + 1 ]$ suffices.

• Note that f is still continuous on A, since it is zero on A\suppf , and thus uniformly continuous (by Heine-Cantor, for example).

• We can rephrase the usual definition of uniform continuity:

$$
\forall \varepsilon \exists \delta = \delta ( \varepsilon ) { \mathrm { ~ s u c h ~ t h a t ~ } } | x - y | < \delta \implies | f ( x ) - f ( y ) | < \varepsilon \quad \forall x , y \in A
$$

as

$\forall \varepsilon \exists \delta = \delta ( \varepsilon )$ such that $| h | < \delta \implies | f ( x - h ) - f ( x ) | < \varepsilon$ ∀x such that $x , x \pm h \in A$

• So fix ε and choose such a δ for A, and choose h such that $| h | < \operatorname* { m i n } ( 1 , \delta )$ Then the desired computation goes through by uniform continuity of f on A.

Proof (of b).

We have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb { R } } | A _ { h } ( f ) ( x ) | ~ d x = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \left| \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \int _ { x - h } ^ { x + h } f ( y ) ~ d y \right| ~ d x } & { } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \int _ { x - h } ^ { x + h } | f ( y ) | ~ d y ~ d x } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = { _ { F T } } \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \int _ { y - h } ^ { y + h } | f ( y ) | ~ \mathrm { d } \mathbf { x } ~ \mathrm { d } \mathbf { y } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } | f ( y ) | ~ d y } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \| f \| _ { 1 } , } \end{array}
$$

and (rough sketch)

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb { R } } | A _ { h } ( f ) ( x ) - f ( x ) | ~ d x = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \left| \left( \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \int _ { B ( h , x ) } f ( y ) ~ d y \right) - f ( x ) \right| d x } & { } \\ & { ~ = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \left| \left( \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \int _ { B ( h , x ) } f ( y ) ~ d y \right) - \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \int _ { B ( h , x ) } f ( x ) ~ d y \right| d x } \\ & { ~ \leq { r r } \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \int _ { B ( h , x ) } | f ( y - x ) - f ( x ) | ~ \mathrm { d } { \mathbf x } ~ \mathrm { d } { \mathbf y } } \\ & { ~ \leq \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { 2 h } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \| { \boldsymbol { r } } _ { \kappa } f - f \| _ { 1 } ~ d y } \\ & { ~ \to 0 ~ { \mathbf b } ~ \mathrm { ~ b } ~ \mathrm { ~ o } \boldsymbol { \mathrm { ~ } } . } \end{array}
$$

Remark 8.2.1: This works for arbitrary $f \in L ^ { 1 }$ , using approximation by continuous functions with compact support:

• Choose $g \in C _ { c } ^ { 0 }$ such that $\| f - g \| _ { 1 } \to 0$

• By translation invariance, $\| \tau _ { h } f - \tau _ { h } g \| _ { 1 } \to 0$

• Write

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \| \tau f - f \| _ { 1 } = \| \tau _ { h } f - g + g - \tau _ { h } g + \tau _ { h } g - f \| _ { 1 } } \\ & { \qquad \leq \| \tau _ { h } f - \tau _ { h } g \| + \| g - f \| + \| \tau _ { h } g - g \| } \\ & { \qquad \| \tau _ { h } g - g \| , } \end{array}
$$

so it suffices to show that $\| \tau _ { h } g - g \| \to 0$

## 8.3 Fall 2017 # 3

Let

$$
S = \mathrm { s p a n } _ { \mathbb { C } } \left\{ \chi _ { ( a , b ) } \ \middle | \ a , b \in \mathbb { R } \right\} ,
$$

the complex linear span of characteristic functions of intervals of the form $( a , b )$

Show that for every $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ , there exists a sequence of functions $\{ f _ { n } \} \subset S$ such that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \| f _ { n } - f \| _ { 1 } = 0
$$

## Walk through.

## Concepts Used:

• From homework: E is Lebesgue measurable iff there exists a finite union of closed cubes A such that $\iota ( E \Delta A ) < \varepsilon$

## Solution:

• It suffices to show that S is dense in simple functions, and since simple functions are finite linear combinations of characteristic functions, it suffices to show this for $\chi _ { A }$ for A a measurable set.

• Let $s = \chi _ { A }$

• By regularity of the Lebesgue measure, choose an open set $O \supseteq A$ such that $m ( O \backslash A ) < \varepsilon$

• O is an open subset of R, and thus $O = \amalg _ { j \in \mathbb { N } } I _ { j }$ is a disjoint union of countably many open intervals.

• Now choose N large enough such that $m ( O \Delta I _ { N , n } ) < \varepsilon = \frac { 1 } { n }$ where we define $I _ { N , n } : =$ $\begin{array} { r } { \bigamalg _ { j = 1 } ^ { N } I _ { j } } \end{array}$

• Now define $f _ { n } = \chi _ { I _ { N , n } }$ , then

$$
\| s - f _ { n } \| _ { 1 } = \int \left| \chi _ { A } - \chi _ { I _ { N , n } } \right| = m ( A \Delta I _ { N , n } ) \ { \overset { n \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } } \ 0 .
$$

• Since any simple function is a finite linear combination of $\chi A _ { i }$ , we can do this for each i to extend this result to all simple functions.

• But simple functions are dense in $L ^ { 1 }$ , so S is dense in $L ^ { 1 }$

## 8.4 Spring 2015 # 4

Define

$$
f ( x , y ) : = { \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { \displaystyle { \frac { x ^ { 1 / 3 } } { ( 1 + x y ) ^ { 3 / 2 } } } } & { { \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } } 0 \leq x \leq y } \\ { 0 } & { { \mathrm { ~ o t h e r w i s e } } } \end{array} \right. }
$$

Carefully show that $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 } )$

$$

## 8.7 Spring 2021 # 4

Let $f , g$ be Lebesgue integrable on R and let $g _ { n } ( x ) : = g ( x - n )$ . Prove that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \| f + g _ { n } \| _ { 1 } = \| f \| _ { 1 } + \| g \| _ { 1 } .
$$

## Concepts Used:

• For $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( X ) , \| f \| _ { 1 } : = \int _ { X } | f ( x ) | d x < \infty .$

• Small tails in $L _ { 1 } \colon \mathrm { i f ~ } f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ , then for every $\varepsilon > 0$ exists some radius R such that

$$
\| f \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( B _ { R } ^ { c } ) } < \varepsilon .
$$

• Shift g to the right far enough so that the two densities are mostly disjoint:

<!-- image-->  
Figure 1: Shifting density

• Any integral $\int _ { a } ^ { b } f$ can be written as $\| f \| _ { 1 } - O ( \operatorname { e r r } )$

• Bounding technique:

$$
a - \varepsilon \leq b \leq a + \varepsilon \implies b = a .
$$

## Solution:

• Fix ε.

• Using small tails for $f , g \in L ^ { 1 }$ , choose $R _ { 1 } , R _ { 2 } \gg 0$ so that

$$
\int _ { B _ { R _ { 1 } } ( 0 ) ^ { c } } | f | < \varepsilon
$$

$$
\int _ { B _ { R _ { 2 } } ( 0 ) ^ { c } } \left| g \right| < \varepsilon .
$$

– Note that this implies

$$
\int _ { - R _ { 1 } } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f | = \| f \| _ { 1 } - 2 \varepsilon
$$

$$
\int _ { - R _ { 2 } } ^ { R _ { 2 } } \left| g _ { N } \right| = \left\| g _ { N } \right\| - 2 \varepsilon .
$$

– Also note that by translation invariance of the Lebesgue integral, $\lVert g \rVert _ { 1 } = \lVert g _ { N } \rVert _ { 1 }$

• Now use N to make the densities almost disjoint: choose $N \gg 1$ so that $N - R _ { 2 } > R _ { 1 }$

<!-- image-->  
Figure 2: Shifting density

• Consider the change of variables $x \mapsto x - N$

$$
\int _ { - R _ { 2 } } ^ { R _ { 2 } } | g ( x ) | d x = \int _ { N - R _ { 2 } } ^ { N + R _ { 2 } } | g ( x - N ) | d x : = \int _ { N - R _ { 2 } } ^ { N + R _ { 2 } } | g _ { N } ( x ) | d x .
$$

– Use this to conclude that

$$
\int _ { N - R _ { 2 } } ^ { N + R _ { 2 } } | g _ { N } | = \| g _ { N } \| - 2 \varepsilon .
$$

• Now split the integral in the problem statement at $R _ { 1 }$

$$
\| f + g _ { N } \| _ { 1 } = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } | f + g _ { N } | = \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f + g _ { N } | + \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | f + g _ { N } | : = I _ { 1 } + I _ { 2 } .
$$

• Idea: from the picture,

– On $I _ { 1 } , f$ is big and $g N$ is small

On $I _ { 2 } , f$ is small and $g _ { N }$ is big

• Casework: estimate $I _ { 1 } , I _ { 2 }$ separately, bounding from above and below.

$I _ { 1 }$ upper bound:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { I _ { 1 } \coloneq \int _ { \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f + g x | } \\ & { \leq \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f | + | g x | , } \\ & { = \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f | + \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | g x | } \\ & { \leq \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f | + \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 2 } } | g x | } \\ & { = \| f \| _ { 1 } - \int _ { \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f | + \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R ^ { - 1 } R _ { 2 } } | g x | } \\ & { \leq \| f \| _ { 1 } - \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { R _ { 2 } } | f | + \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R ^ { - 1 } R _ { 2 } } | g x | } \\ & { \leq \| f \| _ { 1 } - \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f | + \varepsilon } \\ & { \leq \| f \| _ { 1 } + \varepsilon . } \end{array} \qquad \Large ,
$$

– In the last step we’ve used that we’re subtracting off a positive number, so forgetting it only makes things larger.

– We’ve also used monotonicity of the Lebesgue integral: if $A \leq B$ , then $( c , A ) \subseteq ( c , B )$ and $\int _ { c } ^ { A } | f | \leq \int _ { c } ^ { B } | f |$ since $| f |$ is positive.

$I _ { 1 }$ lower bound:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { I _ { 1 } \coloneq \int _ { - \infty } ^ { I _ { 1 } } | f + g x | } \\ & { \phantom { = } \geq \int _ { - \infty } ^ { I _ { 1 } } | f | - | g x | } \\ & { \phantom { = } = \int _ { - \infty } ^ { I _ { 1 } } | f | - \int _ { - \infty } ^ { I _ { 1 } } | g x | } \\ & { \phantom { = } \geq \int _ { - \infty } ^ { I _ { 1 } } | f | - \int _ { - \infty } ^ { N - R _ { 2 } } | g x | } \\ & { \phantom { = } = \| f \| _ { 1 } - \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | f | - \int _ { - \infty } ^ { N - R _ { 2 } } | g x | } \\ & { \phantom { = } = \| f \| _ { 1 } - \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | f | - \int _ { - \infty } ^ { N - R _ { 2 } } | g x | } \\ & { \phantom { = } \geq \| f \| _ { 1 } - \varepsilon - \varepsilon } \\ & { = \| f \| _ { 1 } - 2 \varepsilon . } \end{array} \qquad \begin{array} { r l } & { \qquad } \\ & { \qquad } \end{array}
$$

– Now we’ve used that the integral with $g _ { N }$ comes in with a negative sign, so extending the range of integration only makes things smaller. We’ve also used the $\varepsilon$ bound on both $f$ and $g _ { N }$ here, and both are tail estimates.

• Taken together we conclude

$$
\left\| f \right\| _ { 1 } - 2 \varepsilon \leq I _ { 1 } \leq \left\| f \right\| _ { 1 }
$$

$$
\varepsilon \to 0 \implies I _ { 1 } = \| f \| _ { 1 } .
$$

$I _ { 2 }$ lower bound:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { I _ { 2 } : = \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } \vert f + g _ { N } \vert } } \\ & { \le \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } \vert f \vert + \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } g _ { N } } \\ & { \le \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } \vert f \vert + \Vert g _ { N } \Vert _ { 1 } - \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } \vert g _ { N } \vert } \\ & { \le \varepsilon + \| g _ { N } \Vert _ { 1 } - \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } \vert g _ { N } \vert } \\ & { \le \varepsilon + \Vert g _ { N } \Vert _ { 1 } } \\ & { = \varepsilon + \Vert g _ { N } \Vert _ { 1 } . } \end{array}
$$

Here we’ve again thrown away negative terms, only increasing the bound, and used the tail estimate on $f .$

$I _ { 2 }$ upper bound:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { I _ { 2 } : = \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | f + g _ { N } | } } \\ & { = \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | g _ { N } + f | } \\ & { \geq \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | g _ { N } | - \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | f | } \\ & { = \| g _ { N } \| - \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | g _ { N } | - \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | f | } \\ & { \geq \| g _ { N } \| - 2 \varepsilon . } \end{array}
$$

• Here we’ve swapped the order under the absolute value, and used the tail estimates on both g and $f .$

• Taken together:

$$
\| g \| _ { 1 } - \varepsilon \le I _ { 2 } \le \| g \| _ { 1 } + 2 \varepsilon .
$$

• Note that we have two inequalities:

$$
\| f \| _ { 1 } - 2 \varepsilon \le \int _ { - \infty } ^ { R _ { 1 } } | f - g _ { N } | \le \| f \| _ { 1 } + \varepsilon
$$

$$
\| g \| _ { 1 } - 2 \varepsilon \le \int _ { R _ { 1 } } ^ { \infty } | f - g _ { N } | \le \| g \| _ { 1 } + \varepsilon .
$$

• Add these to obtain

$$
\| f \| _ { 1 } + \| g \| _ { 1 } - 4 \varepsilon \leq I _ { 1 } + I _ { 2 } : = \| f - g _ { N } \| _ { 1 } \leq \| f \| + \| g \| _ { 1 } + 2 \varepsilon .
$$

• Check that as $N \to \infty$ as $\varepsilon \to 0$ to yield the result.
