## 9 Fubini-Tonelli

## 9.1 Spring 2020 # 4

Let $f , g \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ . Argue that $H ( x , y ) : = f ( y ) g ( x - y )$ defines a function in $L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 } )$ and deduce from this fact that

$$
( f * g ) ( x ) : = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } f ( y ) g ( x - y ) d y
$$

defines a function in $L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ that satisfies

$$
\left\| f \ast g \right\| _ { 1 } \leq \left\| f \right\| _ { 1 } \left\| g \right\| _ { 1 } .
$$

Strategy:

Just do it! Sort out the justification afterward. Use Tonelli.

## Concepts Used:

• Tonelli: non-negative and measurable yields measurability of slices and equality of iterated integrals

• Fubini: $f ( x , y ) \in L ^ { 1 }$ yields integrable slices and equality of iterated integrals

• F/T: apply Tonelli to $| f | ;$ if finite, $f \in L ^ { 1 }$ and apply Fubini to f

• See Folland’s Real Analysis II, p. 68 for a discussion of using Fubini and Tonelli.

Solution: • If these norms can be computed via iterated integrals, we have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \| f * g \| _ { 1 } : = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } | ( f * g ) ( x ) | d x } \\ & { \quad = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \bigg | \int _ { \mathbb { R } } H ( x , y ) d y \bigg | d x } \\ & { \quad = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \bigg | \int _ { \mathbb { R } } f ( y ) g ( x - y ) d y \bigg | d x } \\ & { \quad \le \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \bigg | \int _ { \mathbb { R } } f ( y ) g ( x - y ) \big | d x d y } \\ & { \quad = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \bigg | \int _ { \mathbb { R } }  H ( x , y )  d x d y } \\ & { \quad = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \bigg | H ( x , y ) \big | d x d y } \\ & { \quad = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 } } | H \big | d y _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 } } } \\ & { \quad = \| H \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 } ) } . } \end{array}
$$

So it suffices to show $\| H \| _ { 1 } < \infty$

• A preliminary computation, the validity of which we will show afterward:

$$
\begin{array} { r l r l r l } {  { \frac { d H \| _ { 1 } } { d t } \| _ { \infty } = | H \| _ { 1 } \sin _ { \phi } z _ { \phi } } } \\ & { } & & { = \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ ( y ( y ) ) d y ] \ \alpha ( x - y ) \ d y ) \ d x } & & { \quad \mathrm { T o r c h } } \\ & { } & & { - \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ ( y ) \partial ( y ( x - y ) ) \ d x ] ) \ d y } & & { \quad \mathrm { T o r c h } \ } \\ & { } & & { - \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ ( y ) \partial ( y ( x - y ) ) \ d x ] ) \ d y } & & { \quad \mathrm { s u r i t i n g } \ t - x - y \ \delta - d x } \\ & { } & & { - \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ ( y ) \partial ( y ) ] \ \delta ( y ) \ d y ) \ d y } \\ & { } & & { - \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ( \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ ( y ) \delta ( y ) ] \ \cdot \ \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ y ( y ) \ d y ] \ \delta y ) } \\ & { } & & { = \int _ { t _ { 0 } } | t ( y ) | \ \cdot \ \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ y ( y ) \ \delta ( y ) \ d y ] \ \delta y } \\ & { } & & { = \int _ { t _ { 0 } } ^ { t } | t ( y ) | \ \cdot | y | \ \delta y } \\ & { } & & { = | \delta | \int _ { t _ { 0 } } | t ( y ) \ d y } & & { \quad \mathrm { t h e ~ n e r m ~ i s t ~ c o n n e r s t o r s t o r s } } \\ & { } & & { = | \delta | \int _ { t _ { 0 } } | t | \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ \int _ { \mathbb { R } } [ ( y ) \delta ( y ) ] \ \delta y } \\ & { } & & { \quad \mathrm { s u r s u r s t o r s t o r s t o r s } . } \end{array}
$$

• We’ve used Tonelli twice: to equate the integral to the iterated integral, and to switch the order of integration, so it remains to show the hypothesis of Tonelli are fulfilled.

Claim: H is measurable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$

## Proof (?).

• It suffices to show $\tilde { f } ( x , y ) : = f ( y )$ and $\tilde { g } ( x , y ) : = g ( x - y )$ are both measurable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$

Then use that products of measurable functions are measurable.

$f \in L ^ { 1 }$ by assumption, and $L ^ { 1 }$ functions are measurable by definition.

• The function $( x , y ) \mapsto g ( x - y )$ is measurable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 } \colon$

$g$ is measurable on R by assumption, so the cylinder function $G ( x , y ) : = g ( x )$ on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$ is measurable (result from course).

– Define a linear transformation

$$
T : = { \left[ \begin{array} { l l } { 1 } & { - 1 } \\ { 0 } & { 1 } \end{array} \right] } \in { \mathrm { G L } } _ { 2 } ( \mathbb { R } )
$$

$$
\implies  T [ \frac { x } { y } ] = [ \begin{array} { c c } { x - y } \\ { y } \end{array} ] ,
$$

and linear functions are measurable.

– Write

$$
\tilde { g } ( x - y ) : = G ( x - y , y ) : = ( G \circ T ) ( x , y ) ,
$$

and compositions of measurable functions are measurable.

## • Apply Tonelli to $| H |$

– H measurable implies $| H |$ is measurable.

$| H |$ is non-negative.

– So the iterated integrals are equal in the extended sense

– The calculation shows the iterated integral is finite, so $\int | H |$ is finite and H is thus integrable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$

Note: Fubini is not needed, since we’re not calculating the actual integral, just showing H is integrable.

## 9.2 Spring 2019 # 4

Let $f$ be a non-negative function on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ and $\mathcal { A } = \{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R } : 0 \leq t \leq f ( x ) \}$

Prove the validity of the following two statements:

a. $f$ is a Lebesgue measurable function on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } \iff A$ is a Lebesgue measurable subset of $\mathbb { R } ^ { n + 1 }$

b. If $f$ is a Lebesgue measurable function on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ , then

$$
m ( A ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { n } } f ( x ) d x = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } m \left( \{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } : f ( x ) \geq t \} \right) d t
$$

Concepts Used:

• See Stein and Shakarchi p.82 corollary 3.3.

• Tonelli

• Important trick! $\left\{ ( x , t ) \ { \Big | } \ 0 \leq t \leq f ( x ) \right\} = \{ f ( x ) \geq t \} \cap \{ t \geq 0 \}$

## Solution:

Proof $( a , \implies )$

• Suppose $f : \mathbb { R } ^ { n }  \mathbb { R }$ is a measurable function.

• Rewrite A:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { A = \Big \{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times \mathbb { R } \Big | 0 \leq t \leq f ( x ) \Big \} } \\ & { \quad = \Big \{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times \mathbb { R } \Big | 0 \leq t < \infty \Big \} \cap \Big \{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times \mathbb { R } \Big | t \leq f ( x ) \Big \} } \\ & { \quad = \Big ( \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times [ 0 , \infty ) \Big ) \cap \Big \{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times \mathbb { R } \Big | f ( x ) - t \geq 0 \Big \} } \\ & { \quad : = \Big ( \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times [ 0 , \infty ) \Big ) \cap H ^ { - 1 } \left( [ 0 , \infty ) \right) , } \end{array}
$$

where we define

$$
\begin{array} { c } { { H : \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times \mathbb { R }  \mathbb { R } } } \\ { { ( x , t ) \mapsto f ( x ) - t . } } \end{array}
$$

– Note: this is “clearly” measurable!

• If we can show both sets are measurable, we’re done, since σ-algebras are closed under countable intersections.

• The first set is measurable since it is a Borel set in $\mathbb { R } ^ { d + 1 }$ .

• For the same reason, it suffices to show H is a measurable function.

• Define cylinder functions

$$
\begin{array} { c } { F : \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times \mathbb { R }  \mathbb { R } } \\ { ( x , t ) \mapsto f ( x ) } \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array} { r } { G : \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times \mathbb { R }  \mathbb { R } } \\ { ( x , t ) \mapsto t } \end{array}
$$

$F$ is a cylinder of $f ,$ and since $f$ is measurable by assumption, $F$ is measurable.

– G is a cylinder on the identity for R, which is measurable, so G is measurable.

• Define

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { H : \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \to \mathbb { R } } \\ & { \quad ( x , t ) \mapsto F ( x , t ) - G ( x , t ) : = f ( x ) - t , } \end{array}
$$

which are linear combinations of measurable functions and thus measurable.

## Proof $( a , \iff  $

⇐ = :

• Suppose A is a measurable set.

• A corollary of Tonelli applied to $\chi _ { X }$ : if E is measurable, then for a.e. t the following slice is measurable:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathcal { A } _ { t } : = \Big \{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \Bigm | ( x , t ) \in \mathcal { A } \Big \} = \Big \{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \Bigm | f ( x ) \geq t \geq 0 \Big \} } \\ & { \qquad = f ^ { - 1 } \left( [ t , \infty ) \right) . } \end{array}
$$

– But maybe this isn’t enough, because we need $f ^ { - 1 } \left( \left[ \alpha , \infty \right) \right)$ for al l α

• But the other slice is also measurable for a.e. x:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathcal { A } _ { x } : = \left\{ t \in \mathbb { R } ~ \middle | ~ ( x , t ) \in \mathcal { A } \right\} } \\ & { \quad = \left\{ t \in \mathbb { R } ~ \middle | ~ 0 \leq t \leq f ( x ) \right\} } \\ & { \quad = \left\{ t \in \mathbb { R } ~ \middle | ~ t \in [ 0 , f ( x ) ] \right\} } \\ & { \quad = [ 0 , f ( x ) ] . } \end{array}
$$

• Moreover the function $x \mapsto m ( \mathcal { A } _ { x } )$ is a measurable function of x

• Now note $m ( \mathcal { A } _ { x } ) = f ( x ) - 0 = f ( x )$ , so f must be measurable.

Proof $( o f b )$

• Writing down what the slices are

$$
\mathcal { A } = \left\{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R } \ \Big | \ 0 \leq t \leq f ( x ) \right\}
$$

$$
\mathcal { A } _ { t } = \left\{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \Big | t \leq f ( x ) \right\} .
$$

• Then

$$
\begin{array} { l l l } { \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n } } f ( x ) \ d x = \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { f ( x ) } 1 \ d t \ d x } \\ { \displaystyle \quad \quad = \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } \chi _ { A } \ d t \ d x } \\ { \displaystyle \quad \stackrel { F , T } { = } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n } } \chi _ { A } \ d x \ d t } \\ { \displaystyle \quad \quad = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } m ( { \cal A } _ { t } ) \ d t , } \end{array}
$$

where we just use that $\int \int \chi _ { A } = m ( { \mathcal { A } } )$

$\mathrm { B y }$ Tonelli, all of these integrals are equal.

This is justified because $f$ was assumed measurable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ , thus by (a) A is a measurable set and thus $\chi _ { A }$ is a measurable function on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R }$

## 9.3 Fall 2018 # 5

Let $f \geq 0$ be a measurable function on R. Show that

$$
\int _ { \mathbb { R } } f = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } m ( \{ x : f ( x ) > t \} ) d t
$$

## Concepts Used:

• Claim: If $E \subseteq \mathbb { R } ^ { a } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { b }$ is a measurable set, then for almost every $\boldsymbol { y } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { b }$ , the slice $E ^ { y }$ is measurable and

$$
m ( E ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { b } } m ( E ^ { y } ) d y .
$$

– Set $g = \chi _ { E }$ , which is non-negative and measurable, so apply Tonelli.

– Conclude that $g ^ { y } = \chi _ { E ^ { y } }$ is measurable, the function $y \mapsto \int g ^ { y } ( x )$ dx is measurable,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { { } } & { { \mathrm { a n d } \displaystyle \int \int g ^ { y } ( x ) d x d y = \int g . } } \\ { { } } & { { - \mathrm { \normalfont ~ B u t } \displaystyle \int g = m ( E ) \mathrm { \ a n d } \displaystyle \int \int g ^ { y } ( x ) d x d y = \int m ( E ^ { y } ) d y . } } \end{array}
$$

## Solution:

Note: f is a function R → R in the original problem, but here I’ve assumed $f : \mathbb { R } ^ { n }  \mathbb { R } .$

• Since $f \geq 0$ , set

$$
\begin{array} { r } { E : = \Big \{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R } \Big | f ( x ) > t \Big \} = \Big \{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R } \Big | 0 \leq t < f ( x ) \Big \} . } \end{array}
$$

• Claim: since f is measurable, E is measurable and thus $m ( E )$ makes sense.

– Since f is measurable, $F ( x , t ) : = t - f ( x )$ is measurable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R }$ – Then write $E = \{ F < 0 \} \cap \{ t \geq 0 \}$ as an intersection of measurable sets.

• We have slices

$$
E ^ { t } : = \left\{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \Big | ( x , t ) \in E \right\} = \left\{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \Big | 0 \leq t < f ( x ) \right\}
$$

$$
E ^ { x } : = \left\{ t \in \mathbb { R } \Big | ( x , t ) \in E \right\} = \left\{ t \in \mathbb { R } \Big | 0 \leq t \leq f ( x ) \right\} = [ 0 , f ( x ) ] .
$$

$- \ E _ { t }$ is precisely the set that appears in the original RHS integrand.

$$
- \ m ( E ^ { x } ) = f ( x ) .
$$

• Claim: $\chi _ { E }$ satisfies the conditions of Tonelli, and thus $m ( E ) = \int \chi _ { E }$ is equal to any iterated integral.

– Non-negative: clear since $0 \leq \chi _ { E } \leq 1$

– Measurable: characteristic functions of measurable sets are measurable.

• Conclude:

1. For almost every $x , E ^ { x }$ is a measurable set, $x \mapsto m ( E ^ { x } )$ is a measurable function, and $m ( E ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { n } } m ( E ^ { x } )$ dx

2. For almost every t, Et is a measurable set, $t \mapsto m ( E ^ { t } )$ is a measurable function, and $m ( E ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } m ( E ^ { t } )$ dt

• On one hand,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { m ( E ) = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n + 1 } } \chi _ { E } ( x , t ) } \\ { = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R } \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n } } \chi _ { E } ( x , t ) d t d x ~ } & { \mathrm { b y ~ T o n e l l i } } \\ { = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n } } m ( E ^ { x } ) d x ~ } & { \mathrm { f i r s t ~ c o n c l u s i o n } } \\ { = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n } } f ( x ) d x . } \end{array}
$$

• On the other hand,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { m ( E ) = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n + 1 } } \chi _ { E } ( x , t ) } \\ { = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R } \int _ { \mathbb R ^ { n } } \chi _ { E } ( x , t ) d x d t } & { \mathrm { b y ~ T o n e l l i } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \displaystyle \int _ { \mathbb R } m ( E ^ { t } ) d t \quad \mathrm { s e c o n d ~ c o n c l u s i o n } . } \end{array}
$$

• Thus

$$
\int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { n } } f d x = m ( E ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } m ( E ^ { t } ) d t = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } m \left( { \left\{ { x } \mid f ( x ) > t \right\} } \right) .
$$

## 10 $L ^ { 2 }$ and Fourier Analysis

## 10.1 Spring 2020 # 6

## 10.1.1 a

Show that

$$
L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] ) \subseteq L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] ) \quad { \mathrm { a n d } } \quad \ell ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { Z } ) \subseteq \ell ^ { 2 } ( \mathbb { Z } ) .
$$

10.1.2 b

For $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ define

$$
{ \widehat { f } } ( n ) : = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) e ^ { - 2 \pi i n x } d x .
$$

Prove that if $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ and $\left\{ { \widehat { f } } ( n ) \right\} \in \ell ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { Z } )$ then

$$
S _ { N } f ( x ) : = \sum _ { | n | \leq N } { \widehat { f } } ( n ) e ^ { 2 \pi i n x } .
$$

converges uniformly on [0, 1] to a continuous function g such that $g = f$ almost everywhere.

Hint: One approach is to argue that if $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ with $\left\{ { \widehat { f } } ( n ) \right\} \in \ell ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { Z } )$ then $f \in L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

## Solution:

## Concepts Used:

• For $e _ { n } ( x ) : = e ^ { 2 \pi i n x }$ , the set $\{ e _ { n } \}$ is an orthonormal basis for $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

• For any orthonormal sequence in a Hilbert space, we have Bessel’s inequality:

$$
\sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } | \langle x , e _ { k } \rangle | ^ { 2 } \leq \| x \| ^ { 2 } .
$$

• When $\{ e _ { n } \}$ is a basis, the above is an equality (Parseval)

• Arguing uniform convergence: since $\left\{ { \widehat { f } } ( n ) \right\} \in \ell ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { Z } )$ , we should be able to apply the M test.

## 10.1.3 a

Claim: $\ell ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { Z } ) \subseteq \ell ^ { 2 } ( \mathbb { Z } )$

• Set $\mathbf { c } = \left\{ c _ { k } \mid k \in \mathbb { Z } \right\} \in \ell ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { Z } )$

• It suffices to show that if $\sum _ { k \in \mathbb { Z } } \left. c _ { k } \right. < \infty$ then $\sum _ { k \in \mathbb { Z } } \left. c _ { k } \right. ^ { 2 } < \infty$

• Let $S = \left\{ c _ { k } \Big | | c _ { k } | \leq 1 \right\}$ , then $c _ { k } \in S \implies \left| c _ { k } \right| ^ { 2 } \leq \left| c _ { k } \right|$

• Claim: $S ^ { c }$ can only contain finitely many elements, all of which are finite.

– If not, either $S ^ { c } : = \{ c _ { j } \} _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty }$ is infinite with every $| c _ { j } | > 1$ , which forces

$$
\sum _ { c _ { k } \in S ^ { c } } | c _ { k } | = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } | c _ { j } | > \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { \infty } 1 = \infty .
$$

– If any $c _ { j } = \infty$ , then $\sum _ { k \in \mathbb { Z } } \left. c _ { k } \right. \geq c _ { j } = \infty .$

• So $S ^ { c }$ is a finite set of finite integers, let $N = \operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ | c _ { j } | ^ { 2 } \ : \Big | \ : c _ { j } \in S ^ { c } \right\} < \infty .$

• Rewrite the sum

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle \sum _ { k \in \mathbb { Z } } \left| c _ { k } \right| ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { \alpha _ { k } \in S } \left| c _ { k } \right| ^ { 2 } + \sum _ { \alpha _ { k } \in S ^ { c } } \left| c _ { k } \right| ^ { 2 } } & { } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \leq \sum _ { \alpha _ { k } \in S } \left| c _ { k } \right| + \sum _ { \alpha _ { k } \in S ^ { c } } \left| c _ { k } \right| ^ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \leq \sum _ { k \in \mathbb { Z } } \left| c _ { k } \right| + \sum _ { \alpha _ { k } \in S ^ { c } } \left| c _ { k } \right| ^ { 2 } \quad \mathrm { s i n c e ~ t h e ~ | \it ~ c _ k | ~ a r e ~ a l l ~ p o s i t i v e } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = \left\| c \right\| _ { \alpha _ { k } } + \sum _ { \alpha _ { k } \in S ^ { c } } \left| c _ { k } \right| ^ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \leq \left\| c \right\| _ { \alpha _ { k } } + \left| S ^ { c } \right| \cdot N } \\ { \displaystyle } & { \ll \infty . } \end{array}
$$

Claim: $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] ) \subseteq L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

• It suffices to show that $\int | f | ^ { 2 } < \infty \implies \int | f | < \infty$

• Define $S = \left\{ x \in [ 0 , 1 ] \ \left| \ | f ( x ) | \leq 1 \right. \right\}$ , then $x \in S ^ { c } \implies | f ( x ) | ^ { 2 } \geq | f ( x ) |$

• Break up the integral:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \int _ { \mathbb { S } } | f | = \int _ { S } | f | + \int _ { S ^ { \varepsilon } } | f | } } \\ & { \le \int _ { S } | f | + \int _ { S ^ { \varepsilon } } | f | ^ { 2 } } \\ & { \le \int _ { S } | f | + \| f \| _ { 2 } } \\ & { \le \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \varepsilon \in S } \{ | f ( x ) | \} \cdot \mu ( S ) + \| f \| _ { 2 } } \\ & { = 1 \cdot \mu ( S ) + \| f \| _ { 2 } \quad \mathrm { b y ~ d e f i n i t i o n ~ o f ~ } S } \\ & { \le 1 \cdot \mu ( [ 0 , 1 ] ) + \| f \| _ { 2 } \quad \mathrm { s i n e ~ } S \le [ 0 , 1 ] } \\ & { = 1 + \| f \| _ { 2 } } \\ & { < \infty . } \end{array}
$$

Note: this proof shows $L ^ { 2 } ( X ) \subseteq L ^ { 1 } ( X )$ whenever $\mu ( X ) < \infty$

## 10.2 Fall 2017 # 5

Let $\varphi$ be a compactly supported smooth function that vanishes outside of an interval $[ - N , N ]$ such that $\int _ { \mathbb R } \varphi ( x ) d x = 1$

For $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } )$ , define

$$
K _ { j } ( x ) : = j \varphi ( j x ) , \qquad f \ast K _ { j } ( x ) : = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } f ( x - y ) K _ { j } ( y ) d y
$$

and prove the following:

1. Each $f * K _ { j }$ is smooth and compactly supported.

2.

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { j \to \infty } \| f * K _ { j } - f \| _ { 1 } = 0
$$

Hint:

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { y \to 0 } \int _ { \mathbb { R } } | f ( x - y ) - f ( x ) | d y = 0
$$

Add concepts.

Solution:

Concepts Used:

## 10.2.1 a

Lemma: If $\varphi \in C _ { c } ^ { 1 }$ , then $( f * \varphi ) ^ { \prime } = f * \varphi ^ { \prime }$ almost everywhere. Sil ly Proof:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { { \mathcal F } ( ( f * \varphi ) ^ { \prime } ) = 2 \pi i \xi { \mathcal F } ( f * \varphi ) } \\ & { \qquad = 2 \pi i \xi { \mathcal F } ( f ) { \mathcal F } ( \varphi ) } \\ & { \qquad = { \mathcal F } ( f ) \cdot ( 2 \pi i \xi { \mathcal F } ( \varphi ) ) } \\ & { \qquad = { \mathcal F } ( f ) \cdot { \mathcal F } ( \varphi ^ { \prime } ) } \\ & { \qquad = { \mathcal F } ( f * \varphi ^ { \prime } ) . } \end{array}
$$

Actual proof :

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { ( f * \varphi ) ^ { \prime } ( x ) = ( \varphi * f ) ^ { \prime } ( x ) } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { \displaystyle = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { h  0 } \frac { ( \varphi * f ) ^ { \prime } ( x + h ) - ( \varphi * f ) ^ { \prime } ( x ) } { h } } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { \displaystyle = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { h  0 } \int \frac { \varphi ( x + h - y ) - \varphi ( x - y ) } { h } f ( y ) } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { \displaystyle \overset { D \geq T } { = } \int _ { \operatorname* { h }  0 } \frac { \varphi ( x + h - y ) - \varphi ( x - y ) } { h } f ( y ) } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { \displaystyle = \int \varphi ^ { \prime } ( x - y ) f ( y ) } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { \displaystyle = ( \varphi ^ { \prime } * f ) ( x ) } } \\ { { \ } } \end{array}
$$

To see that the DCT is justified, we can apply the MVT on the interval [0, h] to f to obtain

$$
{ \frac { \varphi ( x + h - y ) - \varphi ( x - y ) } { h } } = \varphi ^ { \prime } ( c ) \quad c \in [ 0 , h ] ,
$$

and since $\varphi ^ { \prime }$ is continuous and compactly supported, $\varphi ^ { \prime }$ is bounded by some $M < \infty$ by the extreme value theorem and thus

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \int  \frac { \varphi ( x + h - y ) - \varphi ( x - y ) } { h } f ( y )  = \int  \varphi ^ { \prime } ( c ) f ( y )  } } \\ & { } & { \leq \int  M  \vert f \vert } \\ & { } & { =  M  \int  f  < \infty , } \end{array}
$$

since $f \in L ^ { 1 }$ by assumption, so we can take $g : = | M | | f |$ as the dominating function.

Applying this theorem infinitely many times shows that $f * \varphi$ is smooth.

To see that $f * \varphi$ is compactly supported, approximate f by a continuous compactly supported function h, so $\| h - f \| _ { 1 } \overset { L ^ { 1 } } { \to } 0 .$

Now let $g _ { x } ( y ) = \varphi ( x - y )$ , and note that su $\operatorname { \mathfrak { p p } } ( g ) = x - \operatorname { \mathrm { s u p p } } ( \varphi )$ which is still compact.

But since supp(h) is bounded, there is some N such that

$$
| x | > N \implies A _ { x } : = \operatorname { s u p p } ( h ) \cap \operatorname { s u p p } ( g _ { x } ) = \emptyset
$$

and thus

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \displaystyle ( h * \varphi ) ( x ) = \int _ { \mathbb R } \varphi ( x - y ) h ( y ) \ d y } & { } \\ { \displaystyle = \int _ { A _ { x } } g _ { x } ( y ) h ( y ) } & { } \\ { \displaystyle } & { = 0 , } \end{array}
$$

so $\left\{ x \mid f * g ( x ) = 0 \right\}$ is open, and its complement is closed and bounded and thus compact.

10.2.2 b

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \| \int \langle K _ { \cdot } \rangle - f \| _ { 1 } = \int | \int f ( \alpha - y ) K _ { \cdot } ( y ) \ d y - \int ( z ) | d x } } \\ & { = \int | \int f ( \alpha - y ) K _ { \cdot } ( y ) \ d y - \int f ( x ) K _ { \cdot } ( y ) \ d y | d x } \\ & { = \int | \int ( f ( \alpha - y ) - f ( x ) ) K _ { \cdot } ( y ) \ d y | d x } \\ & { \leq \int \int | | G ( x - y ) - f ( x ) | | \cdot | K _ { \cdot } ( y ) | \ d y \ d x } \\ & { \stackrel { \exp } { = } \int \int | ( | G ( x - y ) - f ( x ) | ) \cdot | K _ { \cdot } ( y ) | \ d x \ \mathrm { d y } } \\ & { = \int | K _ { \cdot } ( y ) | ( \int | ( f ( x - y ) - f ( x ) ) | \ d x ) \ d y } \\ & { = \int | | K _ { \cdot } ( y ) | \ \Big ( \int | ( f ( x - y ) - f ( x ) | \ d x \Big ) \ d y \ } \\ & { = \int | K _ { \cdot } ( y ) | \ \cdot | | f - v _ { y } | | \ . d y . } \end{array}
$$

We now split the integral up into pieces.

1. Chose δ small enough such that $| y | < \delta \implies \| f - \tau _ { y } f \| _ { 1 } < \varepsilon$ by continuity of translation in $L ^ { 1 }$ , and

2. Since $\varphi$ is compactly supported, choose J large enough such that

$$
j > J \implies \int _ { | y | \geq \delta } | K _ { j } ( y ) | \ d y = \int _ { | y | \geq \delta } | j \varphi ( j y ) | = 0
$$

Then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \left. { \| f * K _ { j } - f \| _ { 1 } \leq \int | K _ { j } ( y ) | \cdot \| f - \tau _ { y } f \| _ { 1 } \ d y } } \\ & { = \int _ { | y | < \delta } | K _ { j } ( y ) | \cdot \| f - \tau _ { y } f \| _ { 1 } \ d y + \int _ { | y | \geq \delta } | K _ { j } ( y ) | \cdot \| f - \tau _ { y } f \| _ { 1 } \ d y } \\ & { = \varepsilon \int _ { | y | \geq \delta } | K _ { j } ( y ) | + 0 } \\ & { \leq \varepsilon ( 1 ) \right. 0 . } \end{array}
$$

# 11 Functional Analysis: General

<!-- image-->

## 11.1 Fall 2019 # 4

<!-- image-->

Let $\{ u _ { n } \} _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty }$ be an orthonormal sequence in a Hilbert space H.

a. Prove that for every $x \in \mathcal H$ one has

$$
\sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } | \langle x , u _ { n } \rangle | ^ { 2 } \leq \| x \| ^ { 2 }
$$

b. Prove that for any sequence $\{ a _ { n } \} _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } \in \ell ^ { 2 } ( \mathbb { N } )$ there exists an element $x \in \mathcal H$ such that

$$
a _ { n } = \left. x , \ u _ { n } \right. { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ a l l ~ } } n \in \mathbb { N }
$$

and

$$
\| x \| ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } \left| \langle x , u _ { n } \rangle \right| ^ { 2 }
$$

Concepts Used:

• Bessel’s Inequality

• Pythagoras

• Surjectivity of the Riesz map

• Parseval’s Identity

• Trick – remember to write out finite sum $S _ { N }$ , and consider $\| x - S _ { N } \|$

## Solution:

Proof (of a).

• Equivalently, we can show

$$
\| x \| ^ { 2 } - \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } | \langle x , \ u _ { n } \rangle | ^ { 2 } \geq 0 .
$$

• Claim: the LHS is the norm of an element in H, and thus non-negative. More precisely, set $S _ { N } : = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } \langle x , \ u _ { n } \rangle u _ { n }$ , then the above is equal to

$$
\left\| x - \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } S _ { N } \right\| ^ { 2 } .
$$

Note that if this is true, we’re done.

• To see this, expand the norm in terms of inner products:

$$
\begin{array} { r l }  \| \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot \nabla _ { + } \cdot  \end{array}
$$

• Now take $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty }$ and use that k−k is continuous.

Proof (of b).

• Set

$$
x : = \sum _ { n \in \mathbb { N } } a _ { n } u _ { n } .
$$

• Checking the first desired property:

$$
\begin{array} { c l } { \displaystyle \langle x , ~ u _ { m } \rangle = \left. \sum _ { n \geq 1 } a _ { n } u _ { n } , ~ u _ { m } \right. } \\ { \displaystyle = \sum _ { n \geq 1 } a _ { n } \langle u _ { n } , ~ u _ { m } \rangle } \\ { \displaystyle = \sum _ { n \geq 1 } a _ { n } \delta _ { m n } } \\ { \displaystyle = a _ { m } . } \end{array}
$$

• That $x \in H \colon$ : this would follow from

$$
\left\| x \right\| ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { n } \left| \langle x , \ u _ { n } \rangle \right| ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { n } \left| a _ { n } \right| ^ { 2 } < \infty .
$$

The inequality holds by assumption since $\{ a _ { n } \} \in \ell ^ { 2 }$ , so it suffices to show the first equality:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \| x \| ^ { 2 } : = ( x , x ) } \\ & { = \left. \displaystyle \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } a _ { n } {  \boldsymbol { u } } _ { n } , \sum _ { m = 1 } ^ { \infty } a _ { m } {  \boldsymbol { u } } _ { m } \right. } \\ & { = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } a _ { n } a _ { m } ( \delta _ { n } ,  { \boldsymbol { u } } _ { m } ) } \\ & { = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } a _ { n } \overline { { a _ { n } } } b _ { m + 1 } } \\ & { = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } a _ { n } \overline { { a _ { n } } } b _ { m + 1 } } \\ & { = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } a _ { n } \overline { { a _ { n } } } } \\ & { = \sum _ { n } ^ { \infty } | a _ { n } | ^ { 2 } } \\ & { = \sum _ { n } ^ { \infty } | \langle \delta _ { 1 } ,  { \mathrm { \Pi } } _ { a n } \rangle | ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

## 11.2 Spring 2019 # 5

a. Show that $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] ) \subseteq L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ and argue that $L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ in fact forms a dense subset of $L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ .

b. Let Λ be a continuous linear functional on $L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

Prove the Riesz Representation Theorem for $L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ by following the steps below:

$$
\Lambda ( f ) = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) { \overline { { g ( x ) } } } d x \quad { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ a l l ~ } } f \in L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )
$$

with

$$

$$

## Concepts Used:

• Holders’ inequality: $\| f g \| _ { 1 } \leq \| f \| _ { p } \| f \| _ { q }$

• Riesz Representation for $L ^ { 2 } \colon \mathrm { I f } \ \Lambda \in ( L ^ { 2 } ) ^ { \vee }$ then there exists a unique $g \in L ^ { 2 }$ such that $\Lambda ( f ) = \int f g .$

• kf kL∞(X) := inf nt ≥ 0  |f (x)| ≤ t almost everywhereo.

• Lemma: $m ( X ) < \infty \implies L ^ { p } ( X ) \subset L ^ { 2 } ( X )$

## Proof .

Write Holder’s inequality as $\| f g \| _ { 1 } \leq \| f \| _ { a } \| g \| _ { b }$ where ${ \frac { 1 } { a } } + { \frac { 1 } { b } } = 1$ , then

$$
\left\| f \right\| _ { p } ^ { p } = \left\| | f | ^ { p } \right\| _ { 1 } \leq \left\| | f | ^ { p } \right\| _ { a } \| 1 \| _ { b } .
$$

– Now take $a = { \frac { 2 } { p } }$ and this reduces to

$$
\| f \| _ { p } ^ { p } \leq \| f \| _ { 2 } ^ { p } m ( X ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { b } }
$$

$$
\implies \lVert f \rVert _ { p } \leq \lVert f \rVert _ { 2 } \cdot O ( m ( X ) ) < \infty .
$$

## Solution:

Proof (of a).

• Note $X = [ 0 , 1 ] \implies m ( X ) = 1$

• By Holder’s inequality with $p = q = 2 ,$

$$
\| f \| _ { 1 } = \| f \cdot 1 \| _ { 1 } \leq \| f \| _ { 2 } \cdot \| 1 \| _ { 2 } = \| f \| _ { 2 } \cdot m ( X ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } = \| f \| _ { 2 } ,
$$

• Thus $L ^ { 2 } ( X ) \subseteq L ^ { 1 } ( X )$

• Since they share a common dense subset (simple functions), $L ^ { 2 }$ is dense in $L ^ { 1 }$

Let $\boldsymbol { \Lambda } \in { L } ^ { 1 } ( \boldsymbol { X } ) ^ { \vee }$ be arbitrary.

Proof (of b, Existence of g representing $\Lambda ) .$

$$
f \in L ^ { 2 } \subseteq L ^ { 1 }
$$

Claim: $\Lambda \in L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } \implies \Lambda \in L ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } .$

• Suffices to show that $\| \Gamma \| _ { L ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| f \| _ { 2 } = 1 } | \Gamma ( f ) | < \infty$ , since bounded implies continuous.

• By the lemma, $\left\| f \right\| _ { 1 } \leq C \| f \| _ { 2 }$ for some constant $C \approx m ( X )$

• Note

$$
\| \Lambda \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| f \| _ { 1 } = 1 } | \Lambda ( f ) |
$$

• Define ${ \widehat { f } } = { \frac { f } { \left\| f \right\| _ { 1 } } } \operatorname { s o } \left\| { \widehat { f } } \right\| _ { 1 } = 1$

• Since $\| \Lambda \| _ { 1 ^ { \vee } }$ is a supremum over all $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( X )$ with $\| f \| _ { 1 } = 1$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left| \Lambda ( \widehat { f } ) \right| \leq \| \Lambda \| _ { ( L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ) ^ { \vee } } , } \end{array}
$$

• Then

$$
{ \frac { | \Lambda ( f ) | } { \| f \| _ { 1 } } } = \left| \Lambda ( { \widehat { f } } ) \right| \leq \| \Lambda \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } }
$$

$$
\implies | \Lambda ( f ) | \leq \| \Lambda \| _ { 1 ^ { \vee } } \cdot \| f \| _ { 1 }
$$

$$
\leq \| \Lambda \| _ { 1 ^ { \vee } } \cdot C \| f \| _ { 2 } < \infty
$$

So $\Lambda \in ( L ^ { 2 } ) ^ { \vee } .$

Now apply Riesz Representation for $L ^ { 2 } { : }$ there is a $g \in L ^ { 2 }$ such that

$$
f \in L ^ { 2 } \implies \Lambda ( f ) = \langle f , \ g \rangle : = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } f ( x ) { \overline { { g ( x ) } } } d x .
$$

Proof $( o f b , g$ is in $L ^ { \infty } )$

• It suffices to show $\| g \| _ { L ^ { \infty } ( X ) } < \infty$

• Since we’re assuming $\| \Gamma \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } } < \infty ,$ it suffices to show the stated equality. Is this assumed..? Or did we show it..?

• Claim: $\| \Lambda \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } } = \| g \| _ { L ^ { \infty } ( X ) }$

– The result will follow since Λ was assumed to be in $L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } , \operatorname { s o } \| \Lambda \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } } < \infty$ – ≤:

$\| \Lambda \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } } = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| f \| _ { 1 } = 1 } | \Lambda ( f ) |$ $= \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| f \| _ { 1 } = 1 } \left| \int _ { X } f { \bar { g } } \right|$ by (i) $\operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| f \| _ { 1 } = 1 } \int _ { X } | f { \bar { g } } |$ $\mathbf { : } = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| f \| _ { 1 } = 1 } { \| f g \| _ { 1 } }$ $\leq \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| f \| _ { 1 } = 1 } \| f \| _ { 1 } \| g \| _ { \infty }$ by Holder with $p = 1 , q = \infty$ $= \| g \| _ { \infty } ,$

♦ Suppose toward a contradiction that $\| g \| _ { \infty } > \| \Lambda \| _ { 1 ^ { \vee } }$

♦ Then there exists some $E \subseteq X$ with $m ( E ) > 0$ such that

$$
x \in E \implies | g ( x ) | > \| \Lambda \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( X ) ^ { \vee } } .

♦ Note $\| h \| _ { L ^ { 1 } ( X ) } = 1$

♦ Then

$$

$$

## 12.1 Spring 2019 # 1

Let $C ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ denote the space of all continuous real-valued functions on [0, 1].

a. Prove that $C ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ is complete under the uniform norm $\| f \| _ { u } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { x \in [ 0 , 1 ] } | f ( x ) |$

b. Prove that $C ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ is not complete under the L1-norm $\| f \| _ { 1 } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } | f ( x ) | ~ d x .$

Add concepts.

Solution:

## Proof $( o f a )$

• Let $\left\{ f _ { n } \right\}$ be a Cauchy sequence in $C ( I , \| - \| _ { \infty } )$ , so lim lim $\| f _ { m } - f _ { n } \| _ { \infty } = 0$ , we will n m show it converges to some f in this space.

• For each fixed $x _ { 0 } \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ , the sequence of real numbers $\{ f _ { n } ( x _ { 0 } ) \}$ is Cauchy in R since

$$
x _ { 0 } \in I \implies | f _ { m } ( x _ { 0 } ) - f _ { n } ( x _ { 0 } ) | \le \operatorname* { s u p } _ { x \in I } | f _ { m } ( x ) - f _ { n } ( x ) | : = \| f _ { m } - f _ { n } \| _ { \infty } \overset { m > n \to \infty } { \to } 0 ,
$$

• Since R is complete, this sequence converges and we can define $f ( x ) : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } f _ { n } ( x )$

• Thus $f _ { n }  f$ pointwise by construction

• Claim: $\| f - f _ { n } \| \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } 0 .$ , so $f _ { n }$ converges to f in $C ( [ 0 , 1 ] , \| - \| _ { \infty } )$

– Proof:

Fix $\varepsilon \ > \ 0 ;$ we will show there exists an N such that $n \ \geq \ N \quad \Longrightarrow$ $\| f _ { n } - f \| < \varepsilon$

♦ Fix an $x _ { 0 } \in I .$ Since $f _ { n }  f$ pointwise, choose $N _ { 1 }$ large enough so that

$$
n \geq N _ { 1 } \implies | f _ { n } ( x _ { 0 } ) - f ( x _ { 0 } ) | < \varepsilon / 2 .
$$

♦ Since $\| f _ { n } - f _ { m } \| _ { \infty } \to 0$ , choose and $N _ { 2 }$ large enough so that

$$
n , m \geq N _ { 2 } \implies \left\| f _ { n } - f _ { m } \right\| _ { \infty } < \varepsilon / 2 .
$$

♦ Then for $n , m \ge \operatorname* { m a x } ( N _ { 1 } , N _ { 2 } )$ , we have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f ( z _ { 0 } ) | } & { = | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f ( z _ { 0 } ) | + f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) + f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) | } \\ & { = | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) + f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f ( z _ { 0 } ) | } \\ & { \leq | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) | + | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f ( z _ { 0 } ) | } \\ & { < | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) | + \frac { \epsilon } { 2 } } \\ & { \leq | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f _ { \Omega } ( z ) | + \frac { \epsilon } { 2 } } \\ & { \leq \frac { \epsilon } { 4 \pi G } | f _ { \Omega } ( z ) - f _ { \Omega } ( z ) | + \frac { \epsilon } { 2 } } \\ & { < | | f _ { \Omega } - f _ { \Omega } | | _ { \infty } + \frac { \epsilon } { 2 } } \\ & { \leq \frac { \epsilon } { 2 } + \frac { \epsilon } { 2 } } \\ { \implies | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f ( z _ { 0 } ) | \leq \epsilon } \\ { \implies \underset { \epsilon \in \mathcal { X } } { \operatorname* { s u p } } | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f ( z _ { 0 } ) | \leq \epsilon \underset { \epsilon \in \mathcal { X } } { \operatorname* { s u p } } } \\ & { \implies | | f _ { \Omega } ( z _ { 0 } ) - f ( z _ { 0 } ) | \leq \epsilon \underset { \epsilon \in \mathcal { X } } { \operatorname* { s u p } } \quad \mathrm { b y ~ o r e ~ I m i t ~ l a w s } } \end{array}
$$

$f$ is the uniform limit of continuous functions and thus continuous, so $f \in C ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

## Proof $( o f b )$

• It suffices to produce a Cauchy sequence that does not converge to a continuous function.

• Take the following sequence of functions:

$f _ { 1 }$ increases linearly from 0 to 1 on $[ 0 , 1 / 2 ]$ and is 1 on $[ 1 / 2 , 1 ]$   
$f _ { 2 }$ is 0 on $[ 0 , 1 / 4 ]$ increases linearly from 0 to 1 on $[ 1 / 4 , 1 / 2 ]$ and is 1 on $[ 1 / 2 , 1 ]$   
$f _ { 3 }$ is 0 on $[ 0 , 3 / 8 ]$ increases linearly from 0 to 1 on $[ 3 / 8 , 1 / 2 ]$ and is 1 on $[ 1 / 2 , 1 ]$   
$f _ { 3 }$ is 0 on $[ 0 , ( 1 / 2 - 3 / 8 ) / 2 ]$ increases linearly from 0 to 1 on $[ ( 1 / 2 - 3 / 8 ) / 2 , 1 / 2 ]$   
and is 1 on $[ 1 / 2 ,$ 1]

Idea: take sequence starting points for the tri  
angles: $0 , 0 + \frac { \mathrm { \hat { 1 } } } { 4 } , 0 + \frac { 1 } { 4 } + \frac { 1 } { 8 } ,$ · · · which converges   
$t o \ 1 / 2 \ s i n c e \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { 1 } { 2 ^ { k } } } = - { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } + \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } { \frac { 1 } { 2 ^ { k } } } .$

• Then each $f _ { n }$ is clearly integrable, since its graph is contained in the unit square.

$\{ f _ { n } \}$ is Cauchy: geometrically subtracting areas yields a single triangle whose area tends to 0.

• But $f _ { n }$ converges to $\chi _ { [ \frac { 1 } { 2 } , 1 ] }$ which is discontinuous.

Solution:

• Let $f _ { n }$ be a Cauchy sequence in this space, so $\| f _ { n } \| _ { u } < \infty$ for every n and $\| f _ { j } - \ b { f } _ { k } \| _ { u } \overset { j , k \to \infty } {  }$
0.
and define a candidate limit: for each $x \in I ,$ set
$f ( x ) : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } f _ { n } ( x ) .$
• Note that
$\| f _ { n } \| _ { \infty } \leq \| f _ { n } \| _ { u } < \infty$
$\left\| f _ { n } ^ { \prime } \right\| _ { \infty } \leq \left\| f _ { n } \right\| _ { u } < \infty .$
– Thus both $f _ { n } , f _ { n } ^ { \prime }$ are Cauchy sequences in $C ^ { 0 } ( [ a , b ] , \| - \| _ { \infty } )$ , which is a Banach space,
so they converge.
• So
$f _ { n }  f$ uniformly (by uniqueness of limits),
$f _ { n } ^ { \prime } \to g$ uniformly for some ${ \mathit { g } } ,$ and
$f , g \in C ^ { 0 } ( [ a , b ] ) .$
• Claim: $g = f ^ { \prime }$
– For any fixed $a \in I ,$ we have
$\begin{array} { r l } { f _ { n } ( x ) - f _ { n } ( a ) } & { { } \stackrel { u } { \to } f ( x ) - f ( a ) } \end{array}$
$\int _ { a } ^ { x } f _ { n } ^ { \prime } \quad { \overset { u } { \to } } \int _ { a } ^ { x } g .$
– By the FTC, the left-hand sides are equal.
– By uniqueness of limits so are the right-hand sides, so $f ^ { \prime } = g .$
• Claim: the limit f is an element in this space.
– Since $f , f ^ { \prime } \in C ^ { 0 } ( [ a , b ] )$ , they are bounded, and so $\| f \| _ { u } < \infty .$
• Claim: $\| f _ { n } - f \| _ { u } \stackrel { n \to \infty } { \to } 0$

## 12.3 Fall 2017 # 6

Let X be a complete metric space and define a norm

$$
\| f \| : = \operatorname* { m a x } \{ | f ( x ) | : x \in X \} .
$$

Show that $( C ^ { 0 } ( \mathbb { R } ) , \lVert - \rVert )$ (the space of continuous functions $f : X \to \mathbb { R } )$ is complete.

Add concepts.

Shouldn’t this be a supremum? The max may not exist?

Review and clean up.

## Solution:

Let $\{ f _ { k } \}$ be a Cauchy sequence, so $\| f _ { k } \| < \infty$ for all k. Then for a fixed $x ,$ the sequence $f _ { k } ( x )$ is Cauchy in R and thus converges to some $f ( x )$ , so define $f$ by $f ( x ) : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { k \to \infty } f _ { k } ( x )$

Then $\| f _ { k } - f \| = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { x \in X } | f _ { k } ( x ) - f ( x ) | \overset { k \to \infty } { \to } 0 ,$ , and thus $f _ { k }  \ f$ uniformly and thus $f$ is continuous. It just remains to show that f has bounded norm. Choose N large enough so that $\| f - f _ { N } \| < \varepsilon .$ , and write $\| f _ { N } \| : = M < \infty$

$$
\| f \| \leq \| f - f _ { N } \| + \| f _ { N } \| < \varepsilon + M < \infty .
$$

## 13 Extras

Exercise 13.0.1 (?)

Compute the following limits:

$$
\bullet \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \sum _ { k \geq 1 } { \frac { 1 } { k ^ { 2 } } } \sin ^ { n } ( k )
$$

$$
\bullet \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \sum _ { k \geq 1 } \frac { 1 } { k } e ^ { - k / n }
$$

## Solution:

For the first, use that

$$
\left| \sum _ { k \geq 1 } { \frac { 1 } { k ^ { 2 } } } \sin ^ { n } ( k ) \right| \leq \sum _ { k \geq 1 } \left| { \frac { 1 } { k ^ { 2 } } } \sin ^ { n } ( k ) \right| \sum _ { k \geq 1 } \left| { \frac { 1 } { k ^ { 2 } } } \right| < \infty ,
$$

since $| \mathrm { s i n } ( x ) | \leq 1$ and $x ^ { n } < x$ for $| x | \le 1$ . By the dominated convergence theorem, we can pass the limit inside. Using the same fact as above, $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \sin ^ { n } ( x ) = 0$ 7

For the second, the claim is that it diverges (very slowly). Note that lim $e ^ { - k / n } = 1$ for any k. n→∞ By Fatou, we have

$$
\operatorname* { l i m i n f } _ { n \to \infty } \sum _ { k \geq 1 } { \frac { e ^ { - k / n } } { k } } \geq \sum _ { k \geq 1 } \operatorname* { l i m i n f } _ { n \to \infty } { \frac { e ^ { - k / n } } { k } } = \sum _ { k \geq 1 } { \frac { 1 } { k } } = \infty .
$$

Exercise 13.0.2 (?)

Let (Ω, B) be a measurable space with a Borel σ-algebra and $\mu _ { n } : B \to [ 0 , \infty ]$ be a σ-additive measure for each n. Show that the following map is again a σ-additive measure on B:

$$
\mu ( B ) : = \sum _ { n \geq 1 } \mu _ { n } ( B ) .
$$

Solution:

Apply Fubini-Tonelli to commute two sums:

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \mu \displaystyle \left( \bigcup _ { 1 \leq k \leq M } E _ { k } \right) : = = \sum _ { n \geq 1 } \mu _ { n } \left( \bigcup _ { 1 \leq k \leq M } E _ { k } \right) } \\ { = \displaystyle \sum _ { n \geq 1 } \sum _ { 1 \leq k \leq M } \mu _ { n } \left( E _ { k } \right) } \\ { = \displaystyle \sum _ { 1 \leq k \leq M } \sum _ { n \geq 1 } \mu _ { n } \left( E _ { k } \right) \mathrm { F T } } \\ { : = \displaystyle \sum _ { 1 \leq k \leq M } \mu ( E _ { k } ) . } \end{array}
$$

a. Prove that if $f , g : \mathbb { R } ^ { n }  \mathbb { C }$ is both measurable then $F ( x , y ) : = f ( x )$ and $h ( x , y ) : = f ( x - y ) g ( y )$ is measurable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$

b. Show that if $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } ) \cap L ^ { \infty } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ and $g \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ , then $f \ast g \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } ) \cap L ^ { \infty } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ is well defined, and carefully show that it satisfies the following properties:

$$
\left\| f * g \right\| _ { \infty } \leq \left\| g \right\| _ { 1 } \left\| f \right\| _ { \infty } \left\| f * g \right\| _ { 1 }
$$

$$
\leq \| g \| _ { 1 } \| f \| _ { 1 } \| f * g \| _ { 2 } \leq \| g \| _ { 1 } \| f \| _ { 2 } .
$$

Hint: first show $\left| f * g \right| ^ { 2 } \leq \left\| g \right\| _ { 1 } \left( \left| f \right| ^ { 2 } * \left| g \right| \right)$

## 14.4 4 (Weierstrass Approximation Theorem)

Note: (a) is a repeat.

Let $f : [ 0 , 1 ] \to \mathbb { R }$ be continuous, and prove the Weierstrass approximation theorem: for any $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists a polynomial P such that $\| f - P \| _ { \infty } < \varepsilon$

Let $E \subseteq \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ be bounded. Prove the following are equivalent:

1. For any $\epsilon > 0$ there exists and open set $G$ and a closed set F such that

$$
F \subseteq E \subseteq G
$$

$$
m ( G \backslash F ) < \epsilon .
$$

2. There exists a $G _ { \delta }$ set V and an $F _ { \sigma }$ set H such that

$$
m ( V \setminus H ) = 0 .
$$

## 15.3 Problem 3

## 15.3.1 a

Prove that if $E \subseteq \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ is a Lebesgue measurable set, then for any $h \in \mathbb { R }$ the set

$$
E + h : = { \Big \{ } x + h ~ { \Big | } ~ x \in E { \Big \} }
$$

is also Lebesgue measurable and satisfies $m ( E + h ) = m ( E )$

## 15.3.2 b

Prove that if $f$ is a non-negative measurable function on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ and $h \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ then the function

$$
\tau _ { h } d ( x ) : = f ( x - h )
$$

is a non-negative measurable function and

$$
\int f ( x ) d x = \int f ( x - h ) d x .
$$

## 15.4 Problem 4

Let $f : \mathbb { R } ^ { n }  \mathbb { R }$ be a Lebesgue measurable function.

a. Prove that for all $\alpha > 0$

$$
A _ { \alpha } : = { \Big \{ } x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } { \Big | } | f ( x ) | > \alpha { \Big \} } \implies m ( A _ { \alpha } ) \leq { \frac { 1 } { \alpha } } \int | f ( x ) | d x .
$$

b. Prove that

$$
\int | f ( x ) | d x = 0 \iff f = 0 { \mathrm { ~ a l m o s t ~ e v e r y w h e r e . } }
$$

## 15.5 Problem 5

Let $\{ f _ { k } \} _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } \subseteq L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$ be a sequence which converges in $L ^ { 1 }$ to a function $f .$

a. Prove that $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

b. Give an example illustrating that $f _ { k }$ may not converge to f almost everywhere.

c. Prove that $\{ f _ { k } \}$ must contain a subsequence that converges to $f$ almost everywhere.

# 18 Practice Exam (November 2014)

## 18.1 1: Fubini-Tonelli

a. Carefully state Tonelli’s theorem for a nonnegative function $\boldsymbol { F } ( \boldsymbol { x } , t )$ on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R }$

b. Let $f : \mathbb { R } ^ { n }  [ 0 , \infty ]$ and define

$$
\mathcal { A } : = \left\{ ( x , t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R } \ \Big | \ 0 \leq t \leq f ( x ) \right\} .
$$

Prove the validity of the following two statements:

1. f is Lebesgue measurable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } \iff A$ is a Lebesgue measurable subset of $\mathbb { R } ^ { n + 1 }$

2. If f is Lebesgue measurable on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ then

$$
m ( A ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { n } } f ( x ) d x = \int _ { 0 } ^ { \infty } m \left( \left\{ x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \ \Big | \ f ( x ) \geq t \right\} \right) d t .
$$

## 18.2 2: Convolutions and the Fourier Transform

a. Let $f , g \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ and give a definition of $f * g .$

b. Prove that if $f , g$ are integrable and bounded, then

$$
( f * g ) ( x ) \stackrel { | x | \to \infty } { \to } 0 .
$$

c. In parts:

1. Define the Fourier transform of an integrable function $f$ on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$

2. Give an outline of the proof of the Fourier inversion formula.

3. Give an example of a function $f \in L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ such that $\widehat { f }$ is not in $L ^ { 1 } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$

## 18.3 3: Hilbert Spaces

Let $\{ u _ { n } \} _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty }$ be an orthonormal sequence in a Hilbert space $H .$

a. Let $x \in H$ and verify that

$$
\left\| x - \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } \left. x , u _ { n } \right. u _ { n } \right\| _ { H } ^ { 2 } = \| x \| _ { H } ^ { 2 } - \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N } \left| \left. x , u _ { n } \right. \right| ^ { 2 } .
$$

for any $N \in  { \mathbb { N } }$ and deduce that

$$
\sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } | \langle x , u _ { n } \rangle | ^ { 2 } \leq \| x \| _ { H } ^ { 2 } .
$$

b. Let $\{ a _ { n } \} _ { n \in \mathbb { N } } \in \ell ^ { 2 } ( \mathbb { N } )$ and prove that there exists an $x \in H$ such that $a _ { n } = \langle x , ~ u _ { n } \rangle$ for all $n \in \mathbb { N }$ , and moreover x may be chosen such that

$$
\| x \| _ { H } = \left( \sum _ { n \in \mathbb { N } } | a _ { n } | ^ { 2 } \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } .
$$

c. Prove that if $\{ u _ { n } \}$ is complete, Bessel’s inequality becomes an equality.

Solution (part b):

• Take $\{ a _ { n } \} \in \ell ^ { 2 }$ , then note that $\sum | a _ { n } | ^ { 2 } < \infty \implies$ the tails vanish.

• Define $x : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { N \to \infty } S _ { N }$ where $S _ { N } = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } a _ { k } u _ { k }$

$\{ S _ { N } \}$ is Cauchy and H is complete, so $x \in H$

• By construction,

$$
\langle x , \ u _ { n } \rangle = \left. \sum _ { k } a _ { k } u _ { k } , \ u _ { n } \right. = \sum _ { k } a _ { k } \langle u _ { k } , \ u _ { n } \rangle = a _ { n }
$$

since the $u _ { k }$ are all orthogonal.

• By Pythagoras since the $u _ { k }$ are normal,

$$
\| x \| ^ { 2 } = \left\| \sum _ { k } a _ { k } u _ { k } \right\| ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { k } \| a _ { k } u _ { k } \| ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { k } | a _ { k } | ^ { 2 } .
$$

Solution (part $\mathbf { c } )$ :

Let x and $u _ { n }$ be arbitrary.

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  {  x - \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty }  x , ~ u _ { k }  u _ { k } , ~ u _ { n }  =  x , ~ u _ { n }  -  \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty }  x , ~ u _ { k }  u _ { k } , ~ u _ { n }  } } \\ & { =  x , ~ u _ { n }  - \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty }   x , ~ u _ { k }  u _ { k } , ~ u _ { n }  } \\ & { =  x , ~ u _ { n }  - \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty }  x , ~ u _ { k }   u _ { k } , ~ u _ { n }  } \\ & { =  x , ~ u _ { n }  -  x , ~ u _ { n }  = 0 } \end{array}
$$

$$
\implies x - \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } \langle x , \ u _ { k } \rangle u _ { k } = 0 \quad \mathrm { b y ~ c o m p l e t e n e s s } .
$$

So

$$
x = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } \langle x , \ u _ { k } \rangle u _ { k } \implies \| x \| ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } | \langle x , \ u _ { k } \rangle | ^ { 2 } . \mathbf { u } .
$$

## 18.4 4: Lp Spaces

a. Prove Holder’s inequality: let $f \in L ^ { p } , g \in L ^ { q }$ with $p , q$ conjugate, and show that

$$
\left\| f g \right\| _ { p } \leq \left\| f \right\| _ { p } \cdot \left\| g \right\| _ { q } .
$$

b. Prove Minkowski’s Inequality:

$$
1 \leq p < \infty \implies \| f + g \| _ { p } \leq \| f \| _ { p } + \| g \| _ { p } .
$$

Conclude that if $f , g \in L ^ { p } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ then so is $f + g$

c. Let $X = [ 0 , 1 ] \subset \mathbb { R } .$

1. Give a definition of the Banach space $L ^ { \infty } ( X )$ of essentially bounded functions of $X$

2. Let $f$ be non-negative and measurable on $X$ , prove that

$$
\int _ { X } f ( x ) ^ { p } d x { \overset { p \to \infty } { \to } } \left\{ { \overset { \infty } { \underset { m } { \operatorname { m } } } } \left( \left\{ f ^ { - 1 } ( 1 ) \right\} \right) , \right.
$$

and characterize the functions of each type

Solution:

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \int f ^ { p } = \int _ { x < 1 } f ^ { p } + \int _ { x = 1 } f ^ { p } + \int _ { x > 1 } f ^ { p } } } \\ & { } & { = \int _ { x < 1 } f ^ { p } + \int _ { x = 1 } 1 + \int _ { x > 1 } f ^ { p } \ d t } \\ & { } & { = \int _ { x < 1 } f ^ { p } + m ( \{ f = 1 \} ) + \int _ { x > 1 } f ^ { p } \ d t } \\ & { } & { \stackrel { p \to \infty } { \to } 0 + m ( \{ f = 1 \} ) + \{ _ { \infty } \begin{array} { c } { 0 \ \ M ( \{ x \geq 1 \} ) = 0 } \\ { \infty \ m ( \{ x \geq 1 \} ) > 0 . } \end{array}  } \end{array}
$$

## 19.2 Fall 2021 #2

<!-- image-->

a. Let $F \subset \mathbb { R }$ be closed, and define

$$
\delta _ { F } ( y ) : = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { x \in F } | x - y | .
$$

For $y \notin F$ , show that

$$
\int _ { F } | x - y | ^ { - 2 } d x \leq \frac { 2 } { \delta _ { F } ( y ) } ,
$$

b. Let $F \subset \mathbb { R }$ be a closed set whose complement has finite measure, i.e. $m ( R \backslash F ) < \infty$ . Define the function

$$
I ( x ) : = \int _ { \mathbb { R } } \frac { \delta _ { F } ( y ) } { | x - y | ^ { 2 } } d y

Prove that $I ( x ) = \infty { \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } } x \not \in F$ , however $I ( x ) < \infty$ for almost every $x \in F$

## 19.3 Fall 2021 #3

Recall that a set $E \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ is measurable if for every $c > 0$ there is an open set $U \subseteq \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ such that $m ^ { * } ( U \setminus E ) < \epsilon$

a. Prove that if E is measurable then for all $\epsilon > 0$ there exists an elementary set F , such that m $( E \Delta F ) < \epsilon .$

Here $m ( E )$ denotes the Lebesgue measure of E, a set F is called elementary if it is a finite union of rectangles and $E \Delta F$ denotes the symmetric difference of the sets E and F .

b. Let $E \subset \mathbb { R }$ be a measurable set, such that $0 < m ( E ) < \infty$ . Use part (a) to show that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n  \infty } \int _ { E } \sin ( n t ) d t = 0
$$

## 19.4 Fall 2021 #4

Let f be a measurable function on R. Show that the graph of f has measure zero in $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$

## 19.5 Fall 2021 #5

Consider the Hilbert space $\mathcal { H } = L ^ { 2 } ( [ 0 , 1 ] )$

a. Prove that of $E \subset { \mathcal { H } }$ is closed and convex then E contains an element of smallest norm.

Hint: Show that if $\| f _ { n } \| _ { 2 } \to$ min $\{ f \in E : \| f \| _ { 2 } \}$ then $\left\{ f _ { n } \right\}$ is a Cauchy sequence.

b. Construct a non-empty closed subset $E \subset { \mathcal { H } }$ which does not contain an element of smallest norm.
